A working manual for C# developers, whether WPF is new to you or not

WPF for modern .NET

Everything the 2013 books still teach correctly, plus everything they can't: SDK-style projects, source-generated MVVM, dependency injection, the Fluent theme, and eight projects that take you from a first window to a signed, syncing, accessible production client.

HOW TO READ THIS BOOK

Every part is written in layers. If WPF is new to you, start with the In plain terms boxes, which explain each idea and the problem it solves, then read the main text and finish with the Recap. Sections marked DEEPER can wait for a second pass. If you already know WPF, skip the boxes: the main text keeps every precise rule, and the deeper sections are written for you.

PART 01

Where WPF stands today

WPF is a retained-mode, vector-based, resolution-independent UI framework. You describe a tree of elements; WPF composes it into a DirectX scene and re-renders what changed. Everything else in the framework follows from that one decision.

Each of those three terms answers a problem you would otherwise hit when building a desktop app.

  • Retained mode means WPF keeps your description of the screen in memory and redraws it itself. You never write "paint this rectangle now" code; you change an object in the tree and the screen follows.
  • Vector-based means shapes, text and borders are stored as geometry rather than as pixels, so they stay sharp when enlarged.
  • Resolution-independent means sizes are measured in units that follow the Windows display scaling setting rather than raw pixels, so a button keeps sensible proportions on a dense screen instead of shrinking.
IN PLAIN TERMS

Older Windows toolkits work like a whiteboard: whenever the window is uncovered or resized, your code is asked to draw everything again. WPF works like a scale model on a table: you place and move objects, and whenever something changes, a camera (DirectX) takes a fresh photograph of the model. Your job is to arrange the model, not to repaint the photograph.

WPF shipped in 2006 on .NET Framework 3.0 and moved to .NET Core 3.0 in 2019. It is now open source at dotnet/wpf and ships yearly with the rest of .NET. It is far from a museum piece: Visual Studio's shell is still WPF. So are large parts of Office's add-in surface, most industrial and defence line-of-business tooling on Windows, and a long tail of internal tools nobody is going to rewrite.

What that means practically:

  • It is Windows only. The TFM (target framework moniker, the value in your project file that names the .NET version and platform) is net10.0-windows. Your app runs only on Windows. The SDK can compile a WPF project elsewhere if you set EnableWindowsTargeting, which helps build servers, but you cannot run or debug it there. There is no Linux story and there never will be.
  • It is in maintenance-plus mode. Yearly releases contain performance work, accessibility fixes, and incremental Fluent styling. No new layout system, no new binding engine.
  • The core API surface is frozen, which is why a 2013 book still works. Dependency properties, routed events, templates and binding have not changed.
  • Trimming (the publish step that deletes code it believes is unused) and Native AOT (compiling the whole app ahead of time to machine code) are not supported. Both need to see every type use at build time, and WPF leans on reflection everywhere (binding paths, XAML type resolution). Publish self-contained or framework-dependent, not trimmed.
IN PLAIN TERMS

"Frozen" is good news for you as a learner. A book, blog post or Stack Overflow answer about bindings or templates from ten years ago still describes how WPF behaves today. What aged is the code around WPF: project files, how view models are written, how services are wired up. The list further down tells you exactly which parts to translate.

Choosing WPF over the alternatives

Several .NET UI frameworks overlap with WPF. The right one depends less on taste than on where the app has to run and who is building it.

PickWhen
WPFWindows-only desktop, rich data-heavy UI, dense forms, custom-drawn controls, an existing WPF codebase, or a team that already knows XAML. The deepest styling and templating system of anything in .NET.
WinUI 3You want the current Windows design language natively and can accept a smaller control set and a rockier tooling story.
AvaloniaYou want WPF-shaped XAML that also runs on Linux and macOS. Closest thing to a cross-platform WPF; different rendering stack.
.NET MAUIMobile is the actual requirement. Desktop is a side effect and the desktop control set shows it.
Blazor HybridYour team is a web team. Host a BlazorWebView and write Razor instead.

What actually changed since the books were written

Most of the best WPF material predates 2020. The framework in it is still accurate; the surrounding tooling and habits are not. Keep this list in your head while reading anything published before 2020, and translate as you go:

  • Project format. SDK-style .csproj (the short project file that lists only what differs from the defaults), no packages.config, no AssemblyInfo.cs, no app.config for settings.
  • MVVM plumbing. CommunityToolkit.Mvvm replaces the change-notification and command code that every old sample writes by hand. Its ObservableObject base class implements INotifyPropertyChanged, its source generators write the properties and command properties for you at compile time, and its RelayCommand class supplies the command logic.
  • Composition. Microsoft.Extensions.Hosting gives you dependency injection (DI), configuration and logging. The books construct view models with new.
  • Async. async/await existed by 2013 but the idioms did not. BackgroundWorker is gone from real code.
  • Theming. .NET 9 added a built-in Fluent theme and ThemeMode. The books' chapters on Aero and Luna theming are archaeology.
  • Gone entirely. XBAP and partial trust, WCF-as-default (HttpClient now), BinaryFormatter (removed in .NET 9, which breaks old clipboard and drag-drop code).
GOTCHA

WPF has no x:Bind. Compiled bindings are a UWP and WinUI feature, so tutorials for those platforms do not carry over here. Every binding in WPF is reflection-based and resolved at runtime, which is why a typo in a Path fails silently instead of failing to build: the app runs, and the field just stays empty. Part 11 covers how to make those failures loud.

RECAP
  • WPF keeps a tree of objects describing your UI and redraws it for you whenever something in the tree changes.
  • It runs only on Windows, and your project targets something like net10.0-windows.
  • The core concepts have not changed since the early books, so old explanations of bindings, templates and properties still hold.
  • The code around WPF has changed: SDK-style projects, the MVVM Toolkit, the generic host and async code replace the old habits.
  • Trimming and Native AOT do not work with WPF, because it discovers types by reflection at runtime.
  • A mistyped binding path does not fail the build; it silently shows nothing at runtime.
PART 02

Project setup and startup

Before any window appears, three things have to be right: the project file that tells the build what kind of app this is, the code that runs at startup, and the safety net that catches errors nobody anticipated. Get these right once and every later part builds on them.

The project file

A project file tells the .NET SDK what to build: which framework, what kind of executable, which packages. dotnet new wpf -n Tasks gets you most of this. The icon, the warnings setting and the three packages are additions you make yourself, shown here because later parts rely on this file. It is the whole build configuration:

Tasks.csproj 
<Project Sdk="Microsoft.NET.Sdk">

  <PropertyGroup>
    <OutputType>WinExe</OutputType>
    <TargetFramework>net10.0-windows</TargetFramework>
    <UseWPF>true</UseWPF>
    <Nullable>enable</Nullable>
    <ImplicitUsings>enable</ImplicitUsings>
    <ApplicationIcon>app.ico</ApplicationIcon>
    <!-- Treat XAML binding failures as build-visible where possible -->
    <TreatWarningsAsErrors>false</TreatWarningsAsErrors>
  </PropertyGroup>

  <ItemGroup>
    <PackageReference Include="CommunityToolkit.Mvvm" Version="8.4.0" />
    <PackageReference Include="Microsoft.Extensions.Hosting" Version="10.0.0" />
    <PackageReference Include="Microsoft.Xaml.Behaviors.Wpf" Version="1.1.135" />
  </ItemGroup>

</Project>

Notes on the properties that matter:

  • OutputType of WinExe produces a Windows GUI executable, so no console window opens behind your app.
  • UseWPF pulls in the WPF reference assemblies and turns on the XAML build tasks. Setting UseWindowsForms alongside it is legal and is how you host WinForms controls.
  • net10.0-windows can be pinned to a Windows SDK version, as in net10.0-windows10.0.19041.0, if you need WinRT APIs such as toast notifications.
  • Files under the project root ending in .xaml are implicitly Page items; App.xaml is implicitly ApplicationDefinition. You rarely need to declare either.

What the XAML compiler actually does

You write a window in two files: MainWindow.xaml for the markup and MainWindow.xaml.cs for your code. Something has to join them so that a control named in the markup is a field you can use in C#. That is the XAML compiler's job.

Each .xaml file with an x:Class produces a generated partial class. It contains a field declaration for every x:Name and an InitializeComponent() method. That method loads BAML, a compiled binary form of the markup embedded in your assembly as a resource, and walks it, instantiating objects and setting properties. Visual Studio shows the generated file if you go to the definition of InitializeComponent; the part to notice is the field and the one call to LoadComponent:

MainWindow.g.i.cs (generated) 
public partial class MainWindow : System.Windows.Window, IComponentConnector
{
    internal System.Windows.Controls.TextBox DraftBox;   // from x:Name="DraftBox"

    public void InitializeComponent()
    {
        if (_contentLoaded) return;
        _contentLoaded = true;
        var resourceLocater = new Uri("/Tasks;component/mainwindow.xaml", UriKind.Relative);
        System.Windows.Application.LoadComponent(this, resourceLocater);
    }

    void IComponentConnector.Connect(int connectionId, object target) { /* wires names + handlers */ }
}
IN PLAIN TERMS

Your .xaml file is a flat-pack instruction sheet, and InitializeComponent() is the moment someone follows it: every part is created and every setting applied, in order. Your constructor is the rest of your code around that moment. Anything you set before anyone follows the sheet is overwritten wherever the sheet says otherwise, and the named parts do not exist yet.

Two consequences worth internalising. First, anything you do before InitializeComponent() runs will be overwritten by the markup wherever the markup sets the same property, and fields named with x:Name are still null at that point. Set defaults after the call, or in the markup itself. Second, XAML is not interpreted at runtime in the normal case. It can be, via XamlReader.Parse, which is how plugin skinning works (Part 3 shows it).

Startup: the default, and the one you want

The App class (from App.xaml and App.xaml.cs) is where your application starts: the build generates the real Main method, which creates an App and runs it. The template gives you StartupUri, which constructs the first window for you. The resource dictionaries merged here make shared colours and styles available to every window (Part 8):

App.xaml: template default 
<Application x:Class="Tasks.App"
             xmlns="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml/presentation"
             xmlns:x="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml"
             StartupUri="MainWindow.xaml">
  <Application.Resources>
    <ResourceDictionary>
      <ResourceDictionary.MergedDictionaries>
        <ResourceDictionary Source="Themes/Colors.xaml" />
        <ResourceDictionary Source="Themes/Controls.xaml" />
      </ResourceDictionary.MergedDictionaries>
    </ResourceDictionary>
  </Application.Resources>
</Application>

That is fine for a toy. The catch is that WPF creates the window itself, with a parameterless constructor, so you cannot hand it the services it needs (a database, a logger, settings). For anything real you want the window to come out of a container, an object that knows how to build each class along with everything that class depends on. So you delete StartupUri and override OnStartup. The container lives inside a host, an object from Microsoft.Extensions.Hosting that also owns configuration, logging and background services (long-running tasks, such as a periodic sync, that start and stop with the app). Part 15 builds this out properly; the shape is:

App.xaml.cs 
public partial class App : Application
{
    private readonly IHost _host;

    public App()
    {
        var builder = Host.CreateApplicationBuilder();
        builder.Services.AddSingleton<MainWindow>();
        _host = builder.Build();
    }

    protected override async void OnStartup(StartupEventArgs e)
    {
        base.OnStartup(e);
        await _host.StartAsync();
        _host.Services.GetRequiredService<MainWindow>().Show();
    }

    protected override void OnExit(ExitEventArgs e)
    {
        using (_host)
        {
            Task.Run(() => _host.StopAsync(TimeSpan.FromSeconds(5))).GetAwaiter().GetResult();
        }
        base.OnExit(e);
    }
}

When this runs, the constructor builds the host and registers MainWindow with its container. OnStartup starts the host's background services, then asks the container for MainWindow, which arrives with its dependencies already supplied. OnExit waits for the host to stop, giving it up to five seconds, before the app finishes closing. The stop runs on a thread-pool thread through Task.Run, so blocking the UI thread while it waits cannot deadlock.

Shutdown mode

A WPF app runs as long as its message loop runs: the loop on the UI thread that receives clicks, key presses and repaint requests from Windows. Closing a window does not necessarily end it. ShutdownMode decides when the loop ends:

  • OnLastWindowClose (the default): when the last open window closes.
  • OnMainWindowClose: when the window in Application.MainWindow closes, even if others are open.
  • OnExplicitShutdown: only when your code calls Application.Current.Shutdown(). You need this for tray applications, which must keep running with no window open.

Single instance DEEPER

Some apps must not run twice: a second copy would fight the first over the same files or tray icon. Single-instance has no framework support. Use a named mutex, an operating-system lock that any process can ask for by name. The first process creates and owns it; a second process finds the name already taken and quits:

C# 
private static Mutex? _single;

protected override void OnStartup(StartupEventArgs e)
{
    _single = new Mutex(initiallyOwned: true, @"Global\Tasks.App.SingleInstance", out bool isFirst);
    if (!isFirst)
    {
        // Optionally signal the running instance over a named pipe, then:
        Shutdown();
        return;
    }
    base.OnStartup(e);
}

The Global\ prefix makes the name visible across all user sessions on the machine; without it, each signed-in user could run one copy. The mutex is kept in a static field so it lives, and stays owned, for as long as the process does.

Unhandled exceptions

An exception nobody catches normally ends the process: the window vanishes and the user loses their work without a word. You cannot catch everything at the source, so WPF and .NET give you last-chance hooks where you can at least log the error and, in some cases, keep the app alive.

There are three separate hooks, and you want all three, because each sees a different kind of failure:

  • DispatcherUnhandledException catches exceptions on the UI thread and lets you mark them handled, so the app keeps running.
  • TaskScheduler.UnobservedTaskException catches faulted tasks nobody awaited. It fires late, when the garbage collector finalises the abandoned task, and may never fire at all if no collection happens before the app exits.
  • AppDomain.CurrentDomain.UnhandledException is the last resort. It sees exceptions escaping any thread, including UI-thread errors the first hook left unhandled. It is not recoverable: you can log, but the process ends afterwards. A failed task nobody awaited never reaches it.

Subscribe to all three in the App constructor, before anything else can fail. Notice that only the first one can stop the crash, with e.Handled = true:

C# 
public App()
{
    DispatcherUnhandledException += (_, e) =>
    {
        _logger.LogError(e.Exception, "Unhandled UI exception");
        MessageBox.Show("Something went wrong. The action was cancelled.",
                        "Tasks", MessageBoxButton.OK, MessageBoxImage.Error);
        e.Handled = true;              // keep the app alive
    };

    TaskScheduler.UnobservedTaskException += (_, e) =>
    {
        _logger.LogError(e.Exception, "Unobserved task exception");
        e.SetObserved();
    };

    AppDomain.CurrentDomain.UnhandledException += (_, e) =>
        _logger.LogCritical(e.ExceptionObject as Exception, "Fatal");
}
IN PLAIN TERMS

Think of three smoke alarms in different rooms. The UI-thread alarm has an extinguisher next to it: you can put the fire out and carry on. The task alarm only goes off when the rubbish is collected, long after the fire. The process-wide alarm can only tell you the building is coming down, so write the log entry and accept the loss.

GOTCHA

async void OnStartup is one of the few places async void is correct, because the framework gives you no awaitable override. An exception anywhere inside it does reach DispatcherUnhandledException, but only after OnStartup has returned, so marking it handled abandons startup half-way. If no window was shown yet, the default shutdown mode never fires and the process keeps running with no window at all. Wrap the body in a try/catch yourself, and on failure log it, tell the user and call Shutdown().

RECAP
  • The .csproj is the whole build configuration, and UseWPF is the switch that makes it a WPF project.
  • InitializeComponent() builds the objects described in your XAML and overwrites any property the markup also sets, so set your defaults after it.
  • Replace StartupUri with an OnStartup override once windows need services passed to them.
  • ShutdownMode decides whether closing windows ends the app; tray apps need OnExplicitShutdown.
  • Hook all three unhandled-exception events, and remember only the UI-thread one can keep the app alive.
PART 03

XAML, precisely

XAML is an object graph serialisation format. Every element is a type, every attribute is a property or an event, and the whole document is a constructor call tree. Once you see it that way the odd corners stop being odd.

Builds on Part 2, project setup and startup, which shows how the build turns a .xaml file into a class.

Markup is a way of writing objects

You could build every window in C#: create a panel, create a button, set its properties, add it to the panel. For a real screen that code becomes long, deeply nested and hard to read, and a designer tool cannot edit it. XAML exists so you can write the same object tree as nested markup instead.

Precisely, XAML is a serialisation format: a text form of objects that can be turned back into those objects. An element such as <Button> means "create an instance of the Button class"; an attribute such as Width="80" means "set its Width property"; nesting means "put this object inside that one". These two snippets produce identical objects:

XAML 
<StackPanel>
  <Button Content="Save" Width="80" />
</StackPanel>
C#, the same objects 
var panel = new StackPanel();
var button = new Button { Content = "Save", Width = 80 };
panel.Children.Add(button);
IN PLAIN TERMS

XAML is a recipe, not a picture. Nothing in it is drawn directly: each line is an instruction to create an object or set a value, carried out in order when the window loads. When some markup puzzles you, ask what C# it would turn into; the answer is almost always the explanation.

Namespaces

Markup only names types, such as Button, so the parser needs to know which Button you mean and in which assembly to find it. An XML namespace declaration (xmlns) answers that: it maps a prefix to a set of .NET namespaces. Every WPF file starts with a block of them on its root element:

XAML 
<Window
  xmlns="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml/presentation"
  xmlns:x="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml"
  xmlns:sys="clr-namespace:System;assembly=System.Runtime"
  xmlns:vm="clr-namespace:Tasks.ViewModels"
  xmlns:ctrl="clr-namespace:Tasks.Controls"
  xmlns:b="http://schemas.microsoft.com/xaml/behaviors"
  xmlns:d="http://schemas.microsoft.com/expression/blend/2008"
  xmlns:mc="http://schemas.openxmlformats.org/markup-compatibility/2006"
  mc:Ignorable="d"
  d:DataContext="{d:DesignInstance vm:MainViewModel}">
  • The default namespace (the xmlns with no prefix) maps to every WPF UI assembly at once, via XmlnsDefinition attributes on those assemblies. That is why <Button> needs no prefix.
  • The x: namespace is the XAML language itself: x:Class, x:Name, x:Key, x:Type, x:Static, x:Array, x:Null.
  • clr-namespace: maps your own types. <vm:MainViewModel /> then means the class in Tasks.ViewModels. The ;assembly= part is only needed for types outside the current assembly.
  • d: and mc:Ignorable="d" are design-time only; the compiler strips them. d:DataContext gives the designer and IntelliSense a type to bind against, which is how you get autocomplete on binding paths.

Property syntax, three ways

Attributes are strings, but properties have real types: a margin is a Thickness, a background is a Brush. XAML gives you three ways to set a property, depending on whether the value fits in a short string, needs a whole object, or is the element's main content:

XAML 
<!-- 1. attribute syntax: value goes through a TypeConverter -->
<Button Background="SteelBlue" Margin="4,8" />

<!-- 2. property element syntax: for values that aren't expressible as a string -->
<Button>
  <Button.Background>
    <LinearGradientBrush StartPoint="0,0" EndPoint="0,1">
      <GradientStop Offset="0" Color="#3B6FD4" />
      <GradientStop Offset="1" Color="#22458A" />
    </LinearGradientBrush>
  </Button.Background>
</Button>

<!-- 3. content syntax: the type's [ContentProperty] takes the children implicitly -->
<Button>                         <!-- Button's ContentProperty is Content -->
  <StackPanel Orientation="Horizontal">
    <Path Data="M0,0 L8,4 L0,8 Z" Fill="White" Width="8" Height="8" />
    <TextBlock Text="Run" Margin="6,0,0,0" />
  </StackPanel>
</Button>

A type converter is a class that turns a string into a value of some type. "4,8" becoming a Thickness and "SteelBlue" becoming a SolidColorBrush are both TypeConverter work. Your own types get the same treatment if you attribute them with [TypeConverter(typeof(MyConverter))].

The third form relies on [ContentProperty], an attribute on the class that names which property receives child elements written directly inside it. For Button that is Content; for panels it is Children, which is why the StackPanel example at the top of this part needed no <StackPanel.Children> wrapper.

Markup extensions

A plain attribute value is fixed when you write it. Many values are not known then: a colour defined in a shared resource file, a value read from a static field, a property that should follow your view model as it changes. Markup extensions cover those cases.

Anything in braces is a markup extension: an object that is constructed at parse time and asked to provide a value for the property. They are not string interpolation.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Type =B2 into a spreadsheet cell and the cell does not store the text "=B2"; it asks for a value and shows that. Braces in XAML work the same way. Some extensions behave like pasting a value ({StaticResource} is looked up once and never again), while {Binding} behaves like a live formula that updates when its source changes. The comparison stops at recalculation: most extensions run once, and only the ones that create a binding or resource reference stay live.

The ones you will use constantly:

ExtensionDoes
{Binding}Creates a live binding to a source property. Part 11.
{StaticResource k}One-time lookup at load time. Fast. Fails hard if the key is missing.
{DynamicResource k}Live lookup that re-evaluates if the resource is replaced. Slower, and only valid on dependency properties.
{x:Static local:Config.Version}Reads a static field, property or enum member.
{x:Type Button}A Type object. Needed for TargetType, DataType.
{x:Null}Literal null: the only way to say "no brush" rather than "a brush named Null".
{TemplateBinding P}Inside a ControlTemplate, a fast one-way binding to the templated parent's P.
{RelativeSource ...}Not standalone: a binding source specifier. Part 11.

"Only valid on dependency properties" refers to WPF's special kind of property, explained in Part 5. Nearly every property you set on a control is one.

Writing your own markup extension DEEPER

Writing your own is a two-method job: a constructor that receives the text after the name, and ProvideValue, which returns the value. This one looks up a localised string by key, and because it runs in the designer as well as in the app, it shows real text at design time too:

C# 
[MarkupExtensionReturnType(typeof(string))]
public sealed class LocExtension : MarkupExtension
{
    public LocExtension() { }
    public LocExtension(string key) => Key = key;

    [ConstructorArgument("key")]
    public string Key { get; set; } = "";

    public override object ProvideValue(IServiceProvider serviceProvider)
    {
        // serviceProvider gives you IProvideValueTarget, IRootObjectProvider,
        // IXamlTypeResolver and friends if you need context.
        return Localisation.Current[Key] ?? $"!{Key}!";
    }
}

In markup, you drop the Extension suffix from the class name, just as with C# attributes. Commands.Save is passed to the constructor as the key:

XAML 
xmlns:loc="clr-namespace:Tasks.Localisation"
...
<Button Content="{loc:Loc Commands.Save}" />

x:Name versus Name, and escaping

You give an element a name so your code-behind can reach it as a field, or so other markup can refer to it. There are two ways to spell that, and they overlap.

Name is a real CLR property on FrameworkElement; x:Name is the XAML-level directive. On a FrameworkElement they do the same thing and you cannot set both. On an object with no Name property of its own, such as a RotateTransform or a SolidColorBrush you want to animate, only x:Name works. Default to x:Name and stop thinking about it.

Because braces, commas and whitespace mean something to the parser, some literal values need escaping. The escapes you will hit:

XAML 
<!-- literal braces: escape with an empty pair -->
<TextBlock Text="{}{not a binding}" />

<!-- comma inside a markup extension parameter: quote it -->
<TextBlock Text="{Binding Total, StringFormat='{}{0:N2} kr'}" />

<!-- whitespace preservation -->
<TextBlock xml:space="preserve">  indented  </TextBlock>

<!-- null, not an empty string: the TextBlock shows nothing -->
<TextBlock Text="{x:Null}" />
GOTCHA

StringFormat needs a leading {} whenever the format string itself starts with {, otherwise the parser sees a markup extension. StringFormat='{}{0:N2}' works; StringFormat='{0:N2}' throws at load.

Loading XAML at runtime DEEPER

Normally all XAML is compiled into your assembly. Sometimes the markup only exists once the app is running: user-supplied templates, or report layouts stored in a database. XamlReader.Parse turns such a string into objects on the spot.

Note the security exposure: XamlReader can instantiate arbitrary types, so never feed it markup from a source you do not trust. The example builds a list item template from a string and applies it:

C# 
const string markup = """
    <DataTemplate xmlns="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml/presentation">
      <TextBlock Text="{Binding Title}" FontWeight="SemiBold" />
    </DataTemplate>
    """;

var template = (DataTemplate)XamlReader.Parse(markup);
listBox.ItemTemplate = template;

The markup must declare its own namespaces, because it is parsed on its own with no surrounding file to inherit them from.

RECAP
  • Every XAML element creates an object and every attribute sets a property, exactly as the equivalent C# would.
  • Namespace declarations tell the parser where to find types; the unprefixed one covers all of WPF, and clr-namespace: covers your own code.
  • Simple values are converted from strings, complex values use property element syntax, and child elements go to the content property.
  • Anything in braces is a markup extension that supplies a value when the markup loads, and only some of them stay live afterwards.
  • Prefer x:Name over Name, and escape a leading brace with {}.
PART 04

Layout

Layout is a two-pass recursive negotiation. Understand the two passes and every panel's behaviour becomes predictable.

Builds on Part 3, XAML precisely. The custom panel at the end also uses a dependency property, explained in Part 5.

If you placed every control at fixed pixel coordinates, your window would break the first time the user resized it, changed the system text size, or switched to a language whose words are longer. WPF instead lets each element say how big it would like to be, and lets containers called panels decide where each child goes. The result adapts to whatever space exists.

Measure, then arrange

Layout runs in two passes over the element tree: one to find out what everyone wants, one to hand out space. After the first full layout, WPF revisits only the elements marked as needing layout again.

Measure. A parent calls child.Measure(availableSize), saying "here is the space I could give you". The child measures its own children, then reports DesiredSize: what it wants. Available size can be double.PositiveInfinity in a dimension, which means "ask for whatever you like"; a StackPanel passes infinity along its orientation, a ScrollViewer passes infinity in its scrolling direction.

Arrange. The parent decides and calls child.Arrange(finalRect), saying "here is what you actually get". The child may be given less than it asked for, and must cope. This is where HorizontalAlignment and VerticalAlignment are applied, positioning the child inside the slot it was given. Margin counts in both passes: it is added to the size the child asks for and kept clear around it when placed.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Picture a host seating guests at a long table. First the host asks each group "how many chairs would you like, if I have up to this many?" and writes the answers down. Only then, knowing all the requests, does the host hand out the actual chairs. A group can be given fewer than it asked for, and has to squeeze in. The difference from a dinner party is that each group first asks its own members the chair question before answering, and later divides the chairs it receives.

The rules that follow from this:

  • A child inside a StackPanel with Orientation="Vertical" gets infinite height at measure, so Height="Auto" means "as tall as I want" and vertical alignment does nothing: the panel gives each child exactly the height it asked for.
  • A ScrollViewer only scrolls if its content asked for more than it got, which is why putting a ListBox inside a vertical StackPanel produces an infinitely tall list that never scrolls. Use a Grid or DockPanel instead.
  • Width/Height constrain the measure pass. MinWidth/MaxWidth clamp it. ActualWidth/ActualHeight are the arrange results and are zero until the first layout pass completes.
IN PLAIN TERMS

A vertical StackPanel is a roll of paper with no end. Ask a ListBox on that roll how tall it wants to be, and it answers "tall enough for all ten thousand items", and gets exactly that. Its scroll bar never appears, because nothing is hidden: the list simply runs off the bottom of the window. A Grid row is a sheet of fixed size, so the list is told how much room there really is and scrolls within it.

The panels

Each panel is one layout strategy. You nest them: a Grid for the page, a StackPanel for a row of buttons inside one cell, and so on.

PanelUse it for
GridThe default. Rows and columns, star-sizing, spanning. Almost every screen root.
StackPanelShort sequences of items along one axis. Never for a scrolling list.
DockPanelClassic chrome: toolbar top, status bar bottom, content fills. Last child fills by default.
WrapPanelFlowing items that wrap. Tag clouds, toolbars, thumbnail walls.
CanvasAbsolute positioning. Diagram surfaces, designers, charts.
UniformGridEqual cells. Keypads, colour pickers, calendar grids.
VirtualizingStackPanelThe default ItemsPanel of ListBox. Realises only containers near the visible area.

In that last row, to realise a container is to actually create the ListBoxItem element for a data item. A list of ten thousand entries with twenty visible creates those twenty plus a cache of about a page above and below the visible area, which is what keeps long lists fast.

Grid: the useful details

A Grid declares its columns and rows first, then places each child with the Grid.Row and Grid.Column attributes (row and column 0 when omitted). Each column width is one of three kinds:

  • Auto: as wide as the widest content in that column.
  • A number: fixed, in device-independent units (explained at the end of this part).
  • Star sizing, * or 2*: a share of whatever width is left after the Auto and fixed columns are served.

Rows work the same way with Height. Notice the ColumnSpan attributes, which let one child cover several columns:

XAML 
<Grid>
  <Grid.ColumnDefinitions>
    <ColumnDefinition Width="Auto" />        <!-- as wide as content -->
    <ColumnDefinition Width="*"    />        <!-- 1 share of the leftover -->
    <ColumnDefinition Width="2*"   />        <!-- 2 shares of the leftover -->
    <ColumnDefinition Width="120"  MinWidth="80" />
  </Grid.ColumnDefinitions>
  <Grid.RowDefinitions>
    <RowDefinition Height="Auto" />
    <RowDefinition Height="*"    />
  </Grid.RowDefinitions>

  <TextBlock Text="Name" Grid.Row="0" Grid.Column="0" />
  <TextBox   Grid.Row="0" Grid.Column="1" Grid.ColumnSpan="3" />
  <ListBox   Grid.Row="1" Grid.ColumnSpan="4" />
</Grid>
IN PLAIN TERMS

Star sizing is splitting a bill. The fixed-price items (Auto and numeric columns) are paid first, then whatever remains is shared out by the number of stars: a 2* column gets two portions for every one a * column gets. Widen the window and only the shared remainder grows.

Two features people miss. SharedSizeGroup makes columns in separate grids the same width, which lines up forms built from repeated rows. GridSplitter gives the user a draggable divider between columns. Notice that the first needs Grid.IsSharedSizeScope on a common ancestor, and the splitter here sits in a narrow column of its own:

XAML 
<!-- SharedSizeGroup: align label columns across separate Grids -->
<StackPanel Grid.IsSharedSizeScope="True">
  <Grid>
    <Grid.ColumnDefinitions>
      <ColumnDefinition Width="Auto" SharedSizeGroup="Labels" />
      <ColumnDefinition Width="*" />
    </Grid.ColumnDefinitions>
    <TextBlock Text="Registration number" />
    <TextBox Grid.Column="1" />
  </Grid>
  <Grid>
    <Grid.ColumnDefinitions>
      <ColumnDefinition Width="Auto" SharedSizeGroup="Labels" />
      <ColumnDefinition Width="*" />
    </Grid.ColumnDefinitions>
    <TextBlock Text="Owner" />
    <TextBox Grid.Column="1" />
  </Grid>
</StackPanel>

<!-- GridSplitter: user-resizable columns -->
<Grid>
  <Grid.ColumnDefinitions>
    <ColumnDefinition Width="240" MinWidth="160" />
    <ColumnDefinition Width="4" />
    <ColumnDefinition Width="*" MinWidth="300" />
  </Grid.ColumnDefinitions>
  <TreeView />
  <GridSplitter Grid.Column="1" HorizontalAlignment="Stretch"
                ResizeBehavior="PreviousAndNext" />
  <ContentControl Grid.Column="2" />
</Grid>

Writing a custom panel DEEPER

The built-in panels cover nearly every screen. When none fits, such as a radial menu or a timeline, you write your own, and the measure and arrange passes above become two methods you override. This one lays children out on a circle; the shape of the code is the same for any layout algorithm you need:

C# 
public sealed class RadialPanel : Panel
{
    public static readonly DependencyProperty RadiusProperty =
        DependencyProperty.Register(
            nameof(Radius), typeof(double), typeof(RadialPanel),
            new FrameworkPropertyMetadata(100d,
                FrameworkPropertyMetadataOptions.AffectsMeasure |
                FrameworkPropertyMetadataOptions.AffectsArrange));

    public double Radius
    {
        get => (double)GetValue(RadiusProperty);
        set => SetValue(RadiusProperty, value);
    }

    protected override Size MeasureOverride(Size availableSize)
    {
        // Let every child take as much as it likes, then claim a square.
        var loose = new Size(double.PositiveInfinity, double.PositiveInfinity);
        double maxChild = 0;
        foreach (UIElement child in InternalChildren)
        {
            child.Measure(loose);
            maxChild = Math.Max(maxChild,
                Math.Max(child.DesiredSize.Width, child.DesiredSize.Height));
        }
        double side = (Radius + maxChild / 2) * 2;
        return new Size(side, side);
    }

    protected override Size ArrangeOverride(Size finalSize)
    {
        int n = InternalChildren.Count;
        if (n == 0) return finalSize;

        var centre = new Point(finalSize.Width / 2, finalSize.Height / 2);
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        {
            var child = InternalChildren[i];
            double angle = 2 * Math.PI * i / n - Math.PI / 2;
            var size = child.DesiredSize;
            var topLeft = new Point(
                centre.X + Radius * Math.Cos(angle) - size.Width / 2,
                centre.Y + Radius * Math.Sin(angle) - size.Height / 2);
            child.Arrange(new Rect(topLeft, size));
        }
        return finalSize;
    }
}

Read the two methods against the negotiation described earlier. MeasureOverride measures every child and returns the size the panel wants. ArrangeOverride gives each child a rectangle, here centred on a point of the circle, and returns the size actually used. The Radius declaration at the top is a dependency property (Part 5); its flags are what the rule below is about.

RULE

Any dependency property that changes how big something is must declare AffectsMeasure; anything that changes where it sits must declare AffectsArrange. Forget these and your panel simply won't update when the property changes.

Layout rounding and DPI

Monitors differ in how many pixels they pack into an inch, and Windows lets users scale everything up (125%, 150%, 200%). A framework that counted in raw pixels would draw tiny buttons on a sharp laptop screen.

WPF units are device-independent: 1 unit = 1/96 of a logical inch, and the Windows display scaling setting decides how many physical pixels that is. At 100% one unit is one pixel; on a 150% display, 96 units is 144 physical pixels. The physical size on screen still differs between devices, but a layout keeps its proportions instead of shrinking on a dense screen. That is why WPF scales cleanly and why a 1-unit border sometimes renders blurry: it lands between device pixels, and the edge is smeared across two of them.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Units are the measurements on a drawing, and pixels are the squares of the grid paper you trace it onto. The Windows scaling setting decides how many paper squares each unit covers, so on finer paper at a higher setting the drawing keeps its proportions instead of shrinking. A line that falls halfway between two rows of squares gets shaded across both, which is the blur. Layout rounding nudges each edge onto the nearest grid line.

UseLayoutRounding="True" (set it on your window; it inherits) snaps layout results to device pixels and fixes almost all of it. For hairlines inside a shape, SnapsToDevicePixels="True" is the older, cruder sibling; prefer layout rounding.

Mixed-DPI setups are the other half. Modern WPF can rescale a window as it moves between monitors with different scaling, but only if the application manifest asks for it. Check that app.manifest contains PerMonitorV2 if you have a mixed-DPI setup, which matters immediately for anyone docking a laptop to a 4K display. Without it, the window is stretched as a bitmap on the second monitor and looks soft.

RECAP
  • Layout runs in two passes: children say what size they want, then parents decide what they get.
  • A vertical StackPanel gives unlimited height, so a list inside one never scrolls; put lists in a Grid row instead.
  • Grid is the default panel: Auto fits content, numbers are fixed, and stars share what is left.
  • ActualWidth and ActualHeight stay zero until the first layout pass has run.
  • WPF measures in units of 1/96 of a logical inch, scaled by the Windows display setting, and UseLayoutRounding="True" on the window keeps edges crisp.
PART 05

Dependency properties

The single most important idea in WPF, and the one with no equivalent in the rest of .NET.

Builds on Part 3, XAML precisely and Part 4, layout, where the custom panel's Radius was a first glimpse of one.

IN PLAIN TERMS

An ordinary C# property holds one value, and whoever set it last wins. In WPF, many parties have an opinion about a button's background: you, a style, the theme, a running animation. A dependency property keeps every one of those opinions and shows the highest-ranked one, like a thermostat where a manual override beats the weekly schedule, which beats the factory setting. Remove the override and the schedule is back in charge without anyone re-entering it.

Precisely: a dependency property is a property whose value is computed from many possible sources, ranked by a fixed order called precedence, and stored only when something actually sets it.

Why they exist

A Button has several hundred properties. Storing a field for each, on every button in a grid of ten thousand rows, would be ruinous. Instead, a dependency property is a static registration shared by the type, and values are stored per-instance in a sparse table: an entry exists only for properties that were actually set. A button that sets three properties pays for three.

Sparse storage is the cheap benefit. The real benefit is that the framework can participate in the value. Styles, templates, animations, inheritance from the parent element, and data bindings all set values without fighting each other, because each is a distinct source at a distinct precedence.

Declaring one

A dependency property has two halves. The identifier is a public static readonly DependencyProperty field, created once per type by DependencyProperty.Register; name it after the property plus Property, because designers and parts of the property system rely on that convention. The CLR wrapper is an ordinary C# property whose getter and setter just call GetValue and SetValue with that identifier, so your own C# code can write bar.Value = 3.

Registration also takes metadata: the default value, flags that tell WPF what a change affects, and optional callbacks. Here is a complete declaration for a rating control. Notice the flags (a change must repaint the control, and bindings to it are two-way unless told otherwise) and the three callbacks, which are covered straight after:

C# 
public class RatingBar : Control
{
    public static readonly DependencyProperty ValueProperty =
        DependencyProperty.Register(
            name:         nameof(Value),
            propertyType: typeof(int),
            ownerType:    typeof(RatingBar),
            typeMetadata: new FrameworkPropertyMetadata(
                defaultValue:     0,
                flags:            FrameworkPropertyMetadataOptions.AffectsRender |
                                  FrameworkPropertyMetadataOptions.BindsTwoWayByDefault,
                propertyChangedCallback: OnValueChanged,
                coerceValueCallback:     CoerceValue),
            validateValueCallback: v => v is int i && i >= 0);

    // The CLR wrapper is a convenience only. WPF NEVER calls it:
    // XAML and binding go straight to SetValue/GetValue.
    public int Value
    {
        get => (int)GetValue(ValueProperty);
        set => SetValue(ValueProperty, value);
    }

    private static void OnValueChanged(DependencyObject d, DependencyPropertyChangedEventArgs e)
    {
        var bar = (RatingBar)d;
        bar.RaiseEvent(new RoutedEventArgs(ValueChangedEvent, bar));
    }

    private static object CoerceValue(DependencyObject d, object baseValue)
    {
        var bar = (RatingBar)d;
        return Math.Clamp((int)baseValue, 0, bar.Maximum);
    }
}
GOTCHA

Never put logic in the CLR wrapper. Put it in the PropertyChangedCallback. The wrapper is bypassed entirely when the value is set from XAML, from a style, from an animation, or from a binding, which is most of the time.

The three callbacks fire in a fixed order and do different jobs:

  • ValidateValueCallback: static, no instance access, returns bool. Throws ArgumentException on false. Use for "no negative sizes ever".
  • CoerceValueCallback: has the instance, so it can clamp against another property. Use for "Value must not exceed Maximum". Call CoerceValue(ValueProperty) from Maximum's changed callback so the clamp re-runs.
  • PropertyChangedCallback: fires only when the effective value actually changed. Where your side effects go.
IN PLAIN TERMS

Validation is a form field that rejects a negative number outright: the value never gets in. Coercion is a speed limiter: ask for 150 with a limit of 120 and you get 120, but the car remembers you asked for 150. Raise the limit and re-run coercion, and you are back at 150 without asking again. The changed callback is the dashboard light that comes on only when the speed you are actually doing changes.

Value precedence

Precedence is what decides the winner when several sources set the same property. The figure shows the case that trips up almost everyone: two identical buttons with the same hover trigger (a rule in a style or template that sets a property while a condition holds, here while the mouse is over the button), where only one reacts.

Here is the full order, highest first. Memorise it; almost every "why isn't my style applying" question is answered here.

#Source
1Property system coercion
2Active animations, or a held animation
3Local value: SetValue, an attribute in XAML, or a Binding you wrote directly on the element
4TemplatedParent template properties (triggers in the template, then setters)
5Implicit style (a Style keyed by {x:Type}): this rank applies only to the Style property itself, picking which style the element gets; that style's triggers then rank at row 6 and its setters at row 8
6Style triggers
7Template triggers (from the ControlTemplate)
8Style setters
9Theme style triggers
10Theme style setters
11Property value inheritance (FontSize, DataContext, FlowDirection…)
12Default value from metadata

A few terms in that table: a local value is one set directly on this particular element rather than arriving from a style or template. Triggers, introduced above, are covered fully in Part 9. The theme style is the default look WPF ships for each control.

The practical consequences:

  • A local value beats every style and trigger. Set Background="Red" on a button and no IsMouseOver trigger will ever change it. This is the single most common WPF confusion. Fix it by moving the value into the style's setters.
  • An animation beats a local value and keeps beating it after the animation finishes, unless the animation has FillBehavior="Stop". Call BeginAnimation(prop, null) to release the hold.
  • Clearing is not setting to default. ClearValue(prop) removes the local value and lets the next source win; SetValue(prop, default) installs a local value that happens to equal the default and continues to block styles.

Attached properties

Some settings belong to a relationship rather than to an element. Which grid row a text box sits in means nothing to the text box; only the Grid around it cares. Giving TextBox a Row property, and a Dock property, and a Left property for every panel that might ever contain it, would not scale.

An attached property is a dependency property registered by one type and set on instances of another. Grid.Row is the canonical example: the Grid owns it, but you set it on the child.

IN PLAIN TERMS

An attached property is a sticky note. Grid.Row="2" sticks a note saying "row 2" on the text box. The text box carries the note around but never reads it; the Grid reads the notes on its children when it lays them out. Any element can carry notes from any owner, which is why one text box can have Grid.Row, ToolTipService.ShowDuration and your own notes at the same time.

Declaring your own looks like a normal dependency property, with RegisterAttached and a pair of static Get/Set methods in place of the wrapper. This one shows a watermark hint in any TextBox it is attached to; the changed callback is where it hooks into the text box's events:

C# 
public static class Watermark
{
    public static readonly DependencyProperty TextProperty =
        DependencyProperty.RegisterAttached(
            "Text", typeof(string), typeof(Watermark),
            new FrameworkPropertyMetadata(null, OnTextChanged));

    public static void SetText(DependencyObject element, string value)
        => element.SetValue(TextProperty, value);

    public static string GetText(DependencyObject element)
        => (string)element.GetValue(TextProperty);

    private static void OnTextChanged(DependencyObject d, DependencyPropertyChangedEventArgs e)
    {
        if (d is not TextBox box) return;
        box.GotFocus  -= Repaint;  box.GotFocus  += Repaint;
        box.LostFocus -= Repaint;  box.LostFocus += Repaint;
        Repaint(box, EventArgs.Empty);
    }

    private static void Repaint(object? sender, EventArgs e) { /* swap an adorner in/out */ }
}

Using it from XAML is one attribute on the element:

XAML 
<TextBox local:Watermark.Text="Search tasks…" />

The Get/Set static methods are not optional: the XAML parser finds the property by convention from those names. This pattern is how you add behaviour to a sealed framework control without subclassing, and it is the basis of attached behaviours: attached properties whose changed callback subscribes to the element's events, so that setting the property switches a behaviour on.

Attached behaviours in practice DEEPER

This section leans on commands and routed events (Part 6), styles (Part 9) and bindings (Part 11). Come back to it once those are familiar.

A frequent need: give an untouchable third-party control a command-on-double-click. No subclass required. When the property is set, the callback subscribes to MouseDoubleClick; when the user double-clicks, the handler runs the command with the element's data as the parameter:

C# 
public static class DoubleClick
{
    public static readonly DependencyProperty CommandProperty =
        DependencyProperty.RegisterAttached(
            "Command", typeof(ICommand), typeof(DoubleClick),
            new PropertyMetadata(null, OnCommandChanged));

    public static void SetCommand(DependencyObject d, ICommand? v) => d.SetValue(CommandProperty, v);
    public static ICommand? GetCommand(DependencyObject d) => (ICommand?)d.GetValue(CommandProperty);

    private static void OnCommandChanged(DependencyObject d, DependencyPropertyChangedEventArgs e)
    {
        if (d is not Control c) return;
        c.MouseDoubleClick -= Handle;
        if (e.NewValue is not null) c.MouseDoubleClick += Handle;
    }

    private static void Handle(object sender, MouseButtonEventArgs e)
    {
        var c = (Control)sender;
        var command = GetCommand(c);
        var parameter = (c as FrameworkElement)?.DataContext;
        if (command?.CanExecute(parameter) == true) command.Execute(parameter);
    }
}

Set through a style, the property lands on every item container the list creates. The binding reaches up to the ListBox to find the command on the view model:

XAML 
<ListBox ItemsSource="{Binding Items}">
  <ListBox.ItemContainerStyle>
    <Style TargetType="ListBoxItem">
      <Setter Property="local:DoubleClick.Command"
              Value="{Binding DataContext.OpenCommand, RelativeSource={RelativeSource AncestorType=ListBox}}" />
    </Style>
  </ListBox.ItemContainerStyle>
</ListBox>

The packaged alternative is Microsoft.Xaml.Behaviors.Wpf, which gives you EventTrigger + InvokeCommandAction declaratively, turning any event into a command call without writing a class:

XAML 
xmlns:b="http://schemas.microsoft.com/xaml/behaviors"
...
<ListBox ItemsSource="{Binding Items}">
  <b:Interaction.Triggers>
    <b:EventTrigger EventName="SelectionChanged">
      <b:InvokeCommandAction Command="{Binding SelectionChangedCommand}"
                             PassEventArgsToCommand="True" />
    </b:EventTrigger>
  </b:Interaction.Triggers>
</ListBox>

Read-only and inherited properties

Two variations come up often. A read-only dependency property is one that outside code can read and bind to, but only the owning class can set, such as a busy indicator the control manages itself. An inherited property is one whose value flows down to every descendant element unless one of them sets its own.

For read-only, registration returns a DependencyPropertyKey. Keep the key private (it is the permission to write) and expose the ordinary identifier publicly. For inheritance, add the Inherits flag; registering it as attached lets the value pass reliably through elements of any type:

C# 
// Read-only: expose the key privately, the property publicly.
private static readonly DependencyPropertyKey IsBusyPropertyKey =
    DependencyProperty.RegisterReadOnly(
        nameof(IsBusy), typeof(bool), typeof(Shell), new PropertyMetadata(false));

public static readonly DependencyProperty IsBusyProperty = IsBusyPropertyKey.DependencyProperty;

public bool IsBusy
{
    get => (bool)GetValue(IsBusyProperty);
    private set => SetValue(IsBusyPropertyKey, value);   // note: the KEY
}

// Inheritable: flows down the logical tree to every descendant.
public static readonly DependencyProperty DensityProperty =
    DependencyProperty.RegisterAttached(
        "Density", typeof(Density), typeof(Theme),
        new FrameworkPropertyMetadata(
            Density.Comfortable,
            FrameworkPropertyMetadataOptions.Inherits));

Inheritance is how FontSize set on a Window reaches a TextBlock six levels down, and how DataContext reaches every binding in a subtree. It is not free, because inherited properties invalidate a whole subtree on change, so use the flag deliberately.

RECAP
  • A dependency property keeps values from many sources and shows the one with the highest precedence.
  • Its C# wrapper is skipped by XAML, styles, bindings and animations, so put logic in the changed callback, never in the wrapper.
  • A value set directly on an element beats every style and trigger; move it into the style if a trigger must change it.
  • ClearValue hands control back to styles, while setting the default value keeps blocking them.
  • Attached properties let a parent such as Grid store its settings on the child elements it lays out.
  • Inherited properties such as FontSize and DataContext flow down the tree until something sets its own value.
PART 06

Routed events and commands

In WPF a click does not belong to a single object. It travels through the elements that make up the window, and any of them can respond. Commands then separate what the user asked for from the control they used to ask.

Builds on Part 3, XAML precisely (elements nested inside elements) and Part 5, dependency properties (the static-field registration pattern). The visual tree gets its full treatment in Part 7.

Three routing strategies

Picture a toolbar with twenty buttons. If every click fired only on the button itself, you would wire twenty handlers. Worse, a WPF button is not one object on screen: its caption, border and icon are separate elements drawn inside it, and the mouse lands on one of those. An event that fired only on the element under the pointer would reach the caption, not the button.

WPF solves both problems by letting the event travel. A routed event is an event that, once raised, visits a chain of elements in the visual tree rather than firing on one object. The visual tree is every element actually drawn, including the pieces inside each control, arranged parent to child. The element where the event starts is its source.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Think of a click as news passing through an organisation chart. First it is announced from the top down to the person it concerns, so each manager on that line can step in early. Then that person reports it back up the same line, and each manager can act on it. Anyone who deals with it can stamp it "handled", and the rest ignore it unless they asked to see everything. Where the analogy stops: only the direct line between the top and that person ever hears about it, never colleagues on other branches.

Every routed event uses one of three routing strategies, which fix the direction of travel:

  • Tunnelling: from the root down to the source. Named PreviewXxx by convention. Fires first.
  • Bubbling: from the source up to the root. Named Xxx. Fires after the tunnel completes.
  • Direct: fires only on the source, like a normal CLR event, but still participates in the event system (so it can be used in an EventSetter, Part 9).

Input events come in tunnelling and bubbling pairs. The figure follows one mouse press on the text inside a button.

Bubbling is why you can put one handler on a panel and catch clicks from every button inside it. StackPanel has no Click event of its own, so the attribute names the class that defines it: ButtonBase.Click means "the Click event of ButtonBase, the base class of every button, arriving here from below".

XAML + C# 
<StackPanel ButtonBase.Click="OnAnyButtonClick">
  <Button Content="Copy"  Tag="copy"  />
  <Button Content="Paste" Tag="paste" />
</StackPanel>

The comments name what the handler receives; for Click, two of them are the same button. Notice the last line: marking the event handled keeps it from reaching the window.

C# 
private void OnAnyButtonClick(object sender, RoutedEventArgs e)
{
    // sender     = the StackPanel (where the handler is attached)
    // e.Source   = the Button that raised Click
    // e.OriginalSource = also the Button for Click; an inner TextBlock only for raw mouse events
    if (e.OriginalSource is not DependencyObject d) return;
    var button = d as Button ?? TreeSearch.FindAncestor<Button>(d);   // Part 07
    Dispatch(button?.Tag as string);
    e.Handled = true;             // stops the bubble here
}

Click Copy and Dispatch receives "copy". TreeSearch.FindAncestor is a small helper that walks up the visual tree; it is listed in Part 7. For raw input such as MouseDown the two sources can differ, which is what the gotcha is about.

GOTCHA

e.Source and e.OriginalSource can differ. OriginalSource is the raw visual hit, often a TextBlock or Border inside a control template. Source is the logical-tree element the event is attributed to. For hit-testing in a list, you almost always want OriginalSource plus a walk up to the container.

Setting e.Handled = true stops further handlers, unless a handler was registered with handledEventsToo. That option exists only in code, through AddHandler:

C# 
// Runs even if something lower already marked the event handled.
myPanel.AddHandler(UIElement.MouseLeftButtonDownEvent,
                   new MouseButtonEventHandler(OnDown),
                   handledEventsToo: true);

You need this more often than you would like, because several built-in controls mark events handled internally. TextBox handles MouseLeftButtonDown; ListBoxItem handles it too. Tunnelled Preview events are the other escape hatch: they fire before the control gets a chance to swallow anything.

Declaring a routed event DEEPER

When you write your own control and want its parents to hear about something, you declare a routed event the way the framework does. The pattern mirrors a dependency property: a static field registered with the system, plus an ordinary-looking CLR event whose add and remove forward to it.

C# 
public static readonly RoutedEvent RatingChangedEvent =
    EventManager.RegisterRoutedEvent(
        nameof(RatingChanged), RoutingStrategy.Bubble,
        typeof(RoutedPropertyChangedEventHandler<int>), typeof(RatingBar));

public event RoutedPropertyChangedEventHandler<int> RatingChanged
{
    add    => AddHandler(RatingChangedEvent, value);
    remove => RemoveHandler(RatingChangedEvent, value);
}

private void Raise(int oldValue, int newValue) =>
    RaiseEvent(new RoutedPropertyChangedEventArgs<int>(oldValue, newValue, RatingChangedEvent));

RaiseEvent sends the event on its way. Because the strategy is Bubble, any ancestor can listen for it in XAML with local:RatingBar.RatingChanged="...", exactly as the panel above listened for ButtonBase.Click.

Commands

A Save action is usually reachable from a menu item, a toolbar button and Ctrl+S. With plain click handlers you write the logic three times, and when there is nothing to save you have to disable three controls yourself.

A command is an object that represents an action: you can execute it, and you can ask whether it can execute right now. Controls such as Button and MenuItem accept a command, run it when activated, and disable themselves when it says it cannot run. A command decouples "what the user asked for" from "which control asked".

IN PLAIN TERMS

A command is a named request slip, "Save", rather than a wire from one button to one piece of code. The menu, the toolbar and the shortcut all hand in the same slip. Whoever owns the slip decides what happens, and also posts a notice saying whether requests are being accepted right now. Every control that uses the slip reads that notice and greys itself out when the answer is no.

WPF ships two mechanisms and they are easy to confuse.

RoutedCommand: the built-in one

ApplicationCommands.Save, EditingCommands.ToggleBold and friends are RoutedUICommands. They route through the tree looking for a CommandBinding that handles them, and they come with default key gestures for free. A CommandBinding attaches the handlers for one command to an element: Executed does the work, CanExecute answers whether it is available.

XAML 
<Window.CommandBindings>
  <CommandBinding Command="ApplicationCommands.Save"
                  Executed="OnSave" CanExecute="OnCanSave" />
</Window.CommandBindings>

<Menu>
  <MenuItem Header="_File">
    <!-- picks up the header text AND Ctrl+S automatically -->
    <MenuItem Command="ApplicationCommands.Save" />
  </MenuItem>
</Menu>

When this runs, the menu shows Save with Ctrl+S beside it, the item greys out whenever OnCanSave sets e.CanExecute to false, and pressing Ctrl+S calls OnSave.

Good for text-editing verbs and anything where the target depends on focus: a cut/copy/paste toolbar that should act on whichever text box is active gets this for free. Bad for view-model logic, because CommandBinding handlers live in code-behind.

ICommand on the view model: what you will actually use

A view model is a plain C# class that holds a screen's state and actions without referring to any control (Part 14 covers the pattern). Its commands implement ICommand, the three-member interface every WPF command source understands:

C# 
public interface ICommand
{
    bool CanExecute(object? parameter);
    void Execute(object? parameter);
    event EventHandler? CanExecuteChanged;
}

A Button or MenuItem calls CanExecute to decide whether it is enabled, Execute when activated, and listens to CanExecuteChanged to know when to ask again. Controls do not poll: a greyed-out Save button stays grey until the command raises that event.

Write these by hand exactly once, to understand them, then use [RelayCommand] from the Community Toolkit forever after (Part 14). The one thing worth knowing is how CanExecuteChanged gets raised, because that is where hand-rolled implementations go wrong.

WPF's CommandManager re-queries CanExecute on every focus change, keyboard event and mouse-up. Hooking into it is a one-liner and is what the old RelayCommand samples do:

C# 
public event EventHandler? CanExecuteChanged
{
    add    => CommandManager.RequerySuggested += value;
    remove => CommandManager.RequerySuggested -= value;
}

It works, and it is wasteful: every command in the visual tree is re-evaluated on every keystroke. Prefer explicit notification (command.NotifyCanExecuteChanged()) and reserve CommandManager.InvalidateRequerySuggested() for the cases where something outside your control changed.

Input bindings

A built-in RoutedUICommand brings its own shortcut; the SaveCommand on your view model does not. An input binding maps a key or mouse gesture to any command, with an optional parameter.

XAML 
<Window.InputBindings>
  <KeyBinding Key="S" Modifiers="Ctrl"       Command="{Binding SaveCommand}" />
  <KeyBinding Key="F5"                       Command="{Binding RefreshCommand}" />
  <KeyBinding Key="Delete"                   Command="{Binding DeleteCommand}"
                                             CommandParameter="{Binding SelectedItem}" />
  <MouseBinding MouseAction="MiddleClick"    Command="{Binding CloseTabCommand}" />
</Window.InputBindings>

These fire when the input reaches the window, so they work wherever focus is inside it. A focused control that handles the key itself gets it first, and then the binding never sees it.

GOTCHA

InputBindings is not a dependency property, so a Style setter cannot fill it. The Command binding on a KeyBinding follows the DataContext like any other binding, so it works whenever the data context arrives. A KeyBinding only fires while keyboard focus is inside the element that owns it: shortcuts on a UserControl do nothing until something inside it has focus, so put window-wide shortcuts on the Window.

RECAP
  • A routed event travels the tree: the Preview event tunnels down from the window to the element, then the plain event bubbles back up.
  • One handler on a container can catch an event from everything inside it.
  • Setting e.Handled makes later handlers skip the event unless they were added with handledEventsToo, and Preview events run before a control can swallow it.
  • e.OriginalSource is the element actually hit, while e.Source is the element the event is attributed to.
  • A command bundles an action with whether it is currently allowed, and controls that use it enable and disable themselves.
  • Put ICommand on the view model and raise CanExecuteChanged explicitly; keep RoutedCommand for editing verbs that follow focus.
PART 07

The control content model

WPF has fewer control types than you would expect because most controls are one of three shapes, and the shape tells you what you can put inside.

Builds on Part 3, XAML precisely and Part 4, layout (panels such as StackPanel and Grid). Styles, resources and templates appear briefly here; Parts 8 to 10 explain them.

The three shapes

In many UI toolkits a button holds a caption and perhaps an icon. If you want a button with a spinner and two lines of text, you need a different control or a custom-drawn one. WPF avoids that by separating what a control does from what it contains. The rule for what a control can contain is its content model, and nearly every control follows one of three.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A ContentControl is a picture frame: the frame decides the border and how it reacts when pressed, and you can put any picture in it. A HeaderedContentControl is a frame with a caption plate. An ItemsControl is a photo album: it holds many pictures and decides how they are laid out. Where the analogy stops: a real frame never looks at its picture, whereas WPF inspects the content to decide how to draw it, as the next paragraph shows.

BaseKey propertyExamples
ContentControlContent: one object of any typeButton, Label, CheckBox, ScrollViewer, Window, UserControl
HeaderedContentControlHeader + ContentGroupBox, Expander, TabItem
ItemsControlItems / ItemsSourceListBox, ComboBox, TreeView, Menu, DataGrid, TabControl

Content being object is the whole trick. WPF decides how to draw it by looking at what you gave it:

  • A string becomes a TextBlock.
  • A UIElement (anything that can draw itself, such as a panel or another control) is rendered as it is.
  • Anything else, say a Customer, makes WPF look for a DataTemplate whose DataType matches, and failing that call ToString(). A DataTemplate is a piece of XAML describing how to draw objects of one type (Part 10).

The three cases side by side:

XAML 
<Button Content="Save" />                      <!-- string  → TextBlock -->

<Button>                                        <!-- element → rendered as-is -->
  <StackPanel Orientation="Horizontal">
    <ctrl:Spinner Width="14" Height="14" />
    <TextBlock Text="Saving…" Margin="6,0,0,0" />
  </StackPanel>
</Button>

<ContentControl Content="{Binding SelectedCustomer}" />  <!-- object → DataTemplate lookup -->

ItemsControl and containers

A list of tasks needs each row to be selectable, highlighted on hover and reachable with the arrow keys. Your TaskViewModel knows nothing about selection, and should not. So the list wraps each item in a container: a control created per item that carries selection, focus and hover behaviour, and draws your item inside it.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Think of a cloakroom. You hand over a coat (the item), the attendant puts it on a hanger (the container), and the hangers go on a rail (the panel). How the coat is displayed, how every hanger behaves, which rail they hang on, and what the counter looks like are four separate decisions. Where the analogy stops: a long list usually makes hangers only for the coats currently in view (virtualisation, defined below).

This is the mechanism behind every list in WPF. For each item in ItemsSource, the control runs through a few overridable methods that each list control customises:

  1. Checks IsItemItsOwnContainerOverride: if the item is already a ListBoxItem, use it directly.
  2. Otherwise calls GetContainerForItemOverride to create one (ListBoxItem, ComboBoxItem, TreeViewItem, DataGridRow…).
  3. Sets the container's Content to the item and its DataContext to the item.
  4. Applies ItemTemplate as the container's ContentTemplate.

Which is why there are four separate template hooks and each does a different job. The ListBox below sets all four; the numbered comments match the four hooks named in the figure caption. Style and DataTrigger are explained in Part 9 and ControlTemplate in Part 10; for now, notice only which layer each hook reaches.

XAML 
<ListBox ItemsSource="{Binding Tasks}">

  <!-- 1. How each ITEM looks -->
  <ListBox.ItemTemplate>
    <DataTemplate DataType="{x:Type vm:TaskViewModel}">
      <Grid>
        <Grid.ColumnDefinitions>
          <ColumnDefinition Width="Auto" />
          <ColumnDefinition Width="*" />
        </Grid.ColumnDefinitions>
        <CheckBox IsChecked="{Binding IsDone}" VerticalAlignment="Center" />
        <TextBlock Grid.Column="1" Text="{Binding Title}" Margin="8,0,0,0" />
      </Grid>
    </DataTemplate>
  </ListBox.ItemTemplate>

  <!-- 2. How each CONTAINER behaves: selection, spacing, bindings to the item -->
  <ListBox.ItemContainerStyle>
    <Style TargetType="ListBoxItem">
      <Setter Property="Padding" Value="6,4" />
      <Setter Property="HorizontalContentAlignment" Value="Stretch" />
      <Style.Triggers>
        <DataTrigger Binding="{Binding IsOverdue}" Value="True">
          <Setter Property="Foreground" Value="{DynamicResource DangerBrush}" />
        </DataTrigger>
      </Style.Triggers>
    </Style>
  </ListBox.ItemContainerStyle>

  <!-- 3. What PANEL lays the containers out -->
  <ListBox.ItemsPanel>
    <ItemsPanelTemplate>
      <VirtualizingStackPanel />
    </ItemsPanelTemplate>
  </ListBox.ItemsPanel>

  <!-- 4. The CHROME around the whole control -->
  <ListBox.Template>
    <ControlTemplate TargetType="ListBox">
      <Border BorderBrush="{TemplateBinding BorderBrush}"
              BorderThickness="{TemplateBinding BorderThickness}"
              CornerRadius="4">
        <ScrollViewer>
          <ItemsPresenter />
        </ScrollViewer>
      </Border>
    </ControlTemplate>
  </ListBox.Template>

</ListBox>
GOTCHA

Inside an ItemTemplate the DataContext is the item, not the parent view model. To bind a per-item button to a command on the list's view model, hop out with RelativeSource, which finds a binding source by walking up the tree: {Binding DataContext.DeleteCommand, RelativeSource={RelativeSource AncestorType=ListBox}}, with CommandParameter="{Binding}" to pass the item.

Choosing a list control

Pick the lightest control that does the job; the table says what each one adds over a bare ItemsControl. One term first: virtualisation means creating containers only for the rows currently on screen, which is what keeps a list of 100,000 items fast.

ControlAdds
ItemsControlNothing. No selection, no scrolling, no virtualisation by default. Perfect for rendering a read-only repeated block.
ListBoxSelection, keyboard navigation, a ScrollViewer, virtualisation.
ListViewA ListBox with a pluggable View, in practice GridView. Mostly superseded by DataGrid.
DataGridColumns, sorting, in-place editing, row headers, frozen columns. Heavy: reach for it when you need editing, not just a table.
TreeViewHierarchy, via HierarchicalDataTemplate.
TabControlYes, really an ItemsControl. Bind ItemsSource to a collection of documents and template ItemTemplate (the tab header) and ContentTemplate (the page).

Hierarchies

A folder tree has items that contain items. A plain DataTemplate describes one level. A HierarchicalDataTemplate is a DataTemplate that also says where each item's children are, through its own ItemsSource, so the TreeView can keep going down.

In the example, folders and files are different view-model types. Each gets a template in the TreeView's resources, keyed by type, so WPF picks the right one per node. The container style then connects each TreeViewItem to its view model.

XAML 
<TreeView ItemsSource="{Binding RootFolders}">
  <TreeView.Resources>
    <HierarchicalDataTemplate DataType="{x:Type vm:FolderViewModel}"
                              ItemsSource="{Binding Children}">
      <StackPanel Orientation="Horizontal">
        <Path Data="{StaticResource FolderGeometry}" Fill="{DynamicResource IconBrush}"
              Width="14" Height="14" Stretch="Uniform" />
        <TextBlock Text="{Binding Name}" Margin="6,0,0,0" />
      </StackPanel>
    </HierarchicalDataTemplate>

    <DataTemplate DataType="{x:Type vm:FileViewModel}">
      <TextBlock Text="{Binding Name}" Opacity=".8" />
    </DataTemplate>
  </TreeView.Resources>

  <TreeView.ItemContainerStyle>
    <Style TargetType="TreeViewItem">
      <Setter Property="IsExpanded" Value="{Binding IsExpanded, Mode=TwoWay}" />
      <Setter Property="IsSelected" Value="{Binding IsSelected, Mode=TwoWay}" />
    </Style>
  </TreeView.ItemContainerStyle>
</TreeView>

Binding IsExpanded and IsSelected back to the view model is the standard way to control a TreeView from MVVM. TreeView.SelectedItem is read-only, so there is no other route.

Logical tree versus visual tree

WPF keeps two trees for the same window, and each feature follows one or the other. Mixing them up is behind many "why is this null" questions.

The logical tree is what you wrote in XAML: window → panel → button → string. It carries DataContext, resource lookup, and property value inheritance.

The visual tree is the expansion after templates are applied: window → border → panel → button → chrome border → content presenter → text block. It is what gets rendered and hit-tested, and what routed events travel (Part 6).

IN PLAIN TERMS

Your XAML is the floor plan: a window, a panel, a button. The visual tree is the finished building, with every wall, frame and fitting the builder added to realise that plan, such as the border and text block inside the button. Settings that flow from room to room, like DataContext, follow the plan; drawing, hit-testing and routed events work on the building. The fittings inside each control are only added when its template is applied, which is why they are missing in a constructor.

Walking the visual tree from code DEEPER

Occasionally you need an element that only exists in the visual tree, such as the container around the item that was clicked. VisualTreeHelper exposes parents and children; these two helpers wrap it. FindAncestor walks up from an element, and FindDescendants yields every matching element below one.

C# 
public static class TreeSearch
{
public static T? FindAncestor<T>(DependencyObject start) where T : DependencyObject
{
    for (var d = start; d is not null; d = VisualTreeHelper.GetParent(d))
        if (d is T match) return match;
    return null;
}

public static IEnumerable<T> FindDescendants<T>(DependencyObject root) where T : DependencyObject
{
    for (int i = 0; i < VisualTreeHelper.GetChildrenCount(root); i++)
    {
        var child = VisualTreeHelper.GetChild(root, i);
        if (child is T match) yield return match;
        foreach (var nested in FindDescendants<T>(child)) yield return nested;
    }
}
}
GOTCHA

Template parts do not exist until the template has been applied. After InitializeComponent the panels and controls you wrote are there, but the elements inside each control's template are not, so VisualTreeHelper cannot find them in a constructor. Do this work in OnApplyTemplate for a custom control, or in the Loaded event otherwise. And if you find yourself walking the visual tree in application code rather than control code, there is usually a binding that would do the job instead.

RECAP
  • A ContentControl holds one thing, a HeaderedContentControl holds a header and one thing, and an ItemsControl holds a collection.
  • Content can be any object: strings become text, elements draw themselves, and other objects are drawn by a matching DataTemplate or shown with ToString().
  • A list wraps each item in a container, and ItemTemplate, ItemContainerStyle, ItemsPanel and Template each customise a different layer.
  • Inside an item template the DataContext is the item, not the list's view model.
  • The logical tree is what you wrote; the visual tree is what gets drawn, and the elements inside each control's template only appear once that template is applied.
PART 08

Resources

Resources are named, shared objects (brushes, styles, templates, even plain numbers) that you define once and use anywhere below the point where you defined them.

Builds on Part 3, XAML precisely (markup extensions and x:Key), Part 5, dependency properties and Part 7, the control content model (the logical tree).

Without resources, your accent colour is typed into two hundred attributes, and changing it means a search-and-replace across the project. With them, it has one definition and one name.
