Precisely: every FrameworkElement has a ResourceDictionary in its Resources property. A resource dictionary is a dictionary of objects, each stored under a key you give with x:Key. Lookup walks up the logical tree, then the application, then the theme dictionaries, then the system.

The smallest useful example defines one brush on a window and uses it twice. {StaticResource AccentBrush} is the markup extension that fetches it by key:

XAML 
<Window.Resources>
  <SolidColorBrush x:Key="AccentBrush" Color="#286983" />
</Window.Resources>

<StackPanel>
  <Button Content="Save"   Background="{StaticResource AccentBrush}" />
  <Button Content="Cancel" Background="{StaticResource AccentBrush}" />
</StackPanel>

Both buttons share the same brush object. Edit the colour in that one line and both follow.

The lookup order

When an element asks for a key, WPF searches these places in turn and stops at the first match:

  1. The element's own Resources.
  2. Each logical-tree ancestor's Resources, in order, up to the window.
  3. Application.Current.Resources (and its merged dictionaries, searched last to first).
  4. The theme dictionaries of the assembly that defines the type being styled (or named in a ComponentResourceKey), as its ThemeInfo attribute directs: a file for the current Windows theme if there is one, with themes/generic.xaml as the fallback. Plain string keys skip this step.
  5. SystemColors, SystemFonts, SystemParameters.
IN PLAIN TERMS

Resource lookup works like finding a word's meaning in a textbook. You check the glossary of the section you are reading, then the chapter's, then the book's, then the publisher's house dictionary, and finally a standard dictionary. The first definition you find is the one you use. That is what lets you override a general definition for one part of the window without touching the rest.

A merged dictionary is a separate dictionary, usually its own file, pulled into another through its MergedDictionaries list. Their search order catches almost everyone:

GOTCHA

Merged dictionaries are searched in reverse order, so the last one listed wins. This is the opposite of what almost everyone assumes and the reason your override dictionary has no effect when you put it first.

Static versus dynamic

Most resources never change after the window loads. A theme brush does, when the user switches to dark mode. WPF gives you two ways to reference a resource, and the difference is when the lookup happens.

IN PLAIN TERMS

StaticResource is copying a phone number out of the directory once, when the page is opened. DynamicResource is saving the contact by name, so a changed number still reaches the right person. The copy is cheaper and tells you at once if the name is not in the directory. The saved contact follows changes, but if the name never existed it quietly rings nobody.

StaticResourceDynamicResource
ResolvedOnce, at loadOn every change, at runtime
Missing keyThrows at load (loud, good)Silently nothing
CostNear zeroKeeps a live reference; measurable in large trees
TargetAny propertyDependency properties only
Forward refsNo: must be defined earlier in the fileYes

Rule of thumb: StaticResource everywhere, DynamicResource only for values that genuinely change at runtime: theme brushes, system colours, and anything a user can switch in settings.

Organising a dictionary

Once a project has more than a handful of resources, App.xaml turns into a very long file. Split resources into dictionary files by purpose and merge them in. A colour dictionary might hold raw colours first, then brushes built from them, then other shared values. Anything can be a resource, including a CornerRadius and a plain double (sys:Double, from the System namespace).

Themes/Colors.xaml 
<ResourceDictionary
  xmlns="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml/presentation"
  xmlns:x="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml"
  xmlns:sys="clr-namespace:System;assembly=System.Runtime"
  xmlns:po="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml/presentation/options">

  <Color x:Key="Accent"      >#286983</Color>
  <Color x:Key="Danger"      >#B4352C</Color>
  <Color x:Key="SurfaceLow"  >#faf4ed</Color>

  <SolidColorBrush x:Key="AccentBrush" Color="{StaticResource Accent}" po:Freeze="True" />
  <SolidColorBrush x:Key="DangerBrush" Color="{StaticResource Danger}" po:Freeze="True" />

  <CornerRadius x:Key="ControlCornerRadius">4</CornerRadius>
  <sys:Double   x:Key="DenseRowHeight">22</sys:Double>
</ResourceDictionary>

po:Freeze="True" on a brush makes it immutable, which removes change-tracking overhead and lets it be shared across threads. Freezing turns a brush, pen or geometry into a read-only object that WPF no longer watches for changes; the po prefix maps to the presentation options namespace that exists for this attribute. Freeze every brush, pen and geometry in your theme dictionary. The saving is measurable on large trees.

Then merge the files in App.xaml. Because the last merged dictionary wins, the overrides file goes last:

App.xaml 
<Application.Resources>
  <ResourceDictionary>
    <ResourceDictionary.MergedDictionaries>
      <ResourceDictionary Source="/Tasks;component/Themes/Colors.xaml" />
      <ResourceDictionary Source="/Tasks;component/Themes/Typography.xaml" />
      <ResourceDictionary Source="/Tasks;component/Themes/Controls.xaml" />
      <!-- last wins: project-specific overrides go here -->
      <ResourceDictionary Source="/Tasks;component/Themes/Overrides.xaml" />
    </ResourceDictionary.MergedDictionaries>

    <!-- Converters and shared data templates -->
    <conv:BoolToVisibilityConverter x:Key="BoolToVis" />
  </ResourceDictionary>
</Application.Resources>

The /Assembly;component/Path.xaml form is a pack URI, WPF's address format for files compiled into an assembly. Use it when the dictionary lives in another assembly; a plain relative path is fine within one project.

Swapping themes at runtime DEEPER

To let users switch between, say, a light and a dark palette, you need every themed value to be replaceable after load. The pattern: one slot in the merged dictionary list that you replace wholesale, and DynamicResource at every usage site.

C# 
public sealed class ThemeService
{
    private ResourceDictionary? _current;

    public void Apply(AppTheme theme)
    {
        var uri = new Uri($"/Tasks;component/Themes/{theme}.xaml", UriKind.Relative);
        var next = new ResourceDictionary { Source = uri };

        var merged = Application.Current.Resources.MergedDictionaries;
        if (_current is not null) merged.Remove(_current);
        merged.Add(next);          // last wins
        _current = next;
    }
}

The service remembers the dictionary it added, removes it, and appends the new one so it wins. Every DynamicResource reference to a key in it updates on screen straight away. StaticResource references keep the value they resolved at load, which is why the usage sites must be dynamic.

The built-in Fluent theme

Out of the box, a WPF window still looks like Windows 8. You can now get the Windows 11 look with one property instead of a third-party library.

SINCE .NET 9

WPF ships a Windows 11 Fluent theme with light and dark variants and support for the user's system accent colour. It is applied with a ThemeMode property rather than a resource dictionary. The API is still marked experimental, so you must suppress diagnostic WPF0001 to use it.

Set ThemeMode on the Application to theme every window, or on one Window:

XAML 
<!-- Whole application -->
<Application ... ThemeMode="System">      <!-- Light | Dark | System | None -->

<!-- Or per window -->
<Window ... ThemeMode="Dark">

From code, the #pragma lines silence the experimental-API diagnostic:

C# 
#pragma warning disable WPF0001
Application.Current.ThemeMode = ThemeMode.System;
this.ThemeMode = ThemeMode.Dark;              // just this window
#pragma warning restore WPF0001

None is the default and gives you the old Aero2 look. The accent colour is available independently of the theme, through SystemColors:

XAML 
<Border Background="{DynamicResource {x:Static SystemColors.AccentColorBrushKey}}" />
<Border Background="{DynamicResource {x:Static SystemColors.AccentColorLight2BrushKey}}" />

Fluent styling is still being filled in release by release. .NET 10 added default Fluent styles for Label, Hyperlink, GroupBox, GridSplitter and NavigationWindow, and fixed right-to-left layout and high-contrast crashes. Coverage is not yet complete, so audit your control set before committing to it. The WPF Gallery app in the Microsoft Store shows you exactly what is styled today.

If you need fuller Fluent coverage now, WPF-UI and MaterialDesignInXamlToolkit are the two maintained third-party options.

RECAP
  • A resource is an object defined once in a Resources dictionary under a key and reused by that key.
  • Lookup starts at the element, walks up to the window, then tries the application, the theme and the system, and the first match wins.
  • Among merged dictionaries, the last one listed wins.
  • Use StaticResource by default and DynamicResource only for values that change while the app runs, such as theme brushes.
  • Freeze the brushes, pens and geometries in shared dictionaries.
  • Since .NET 9, setting ThemeMode gives you the built-in Windows 11 Fluent look.
PART 09

Styles and triggers

A style is a named bag of property setters plus conditional overrides. It is CSS with type safety and without the cascade.

Builds on Part 5, dependency properties (value precedence) and Part 8, resources.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A style is a checklist you hand to a control: padding 12,6, minimum width 88, this font. A trigger is a line on that checklist with a condition: while the mouse is over you, use this background instead. When the condition stops being true, the control goes back to what the checklist said, and you write no clean-up code. Unlike CSS, nothing combines checklists for you: a control takes one style of yours, on top of the theme's built-in one, and your styles build on each other only when you say so.

Keyed and implicit styles

Twenty buttons that need the same padding and font would otherwise repeat the same attributes twenty times, and a redesign means editing every one. A style collects those values in one place. Each value is a setter: a property name and the value to give it. TargetType says which kind of control the style is for.

A style can reach controls in two ways. An implicit style has no key and applies to every control of its target type in scope. A keyed style has an x:Key and applies only to controls that ask for it:

XAML 
<!-- Implicit: applies to every Button in scope, no opt-in -->
<Style TargetType="{x:Type Button}">
  <Setter Property="Padding"    Value="12,6" />
  <Setter Property="MinWidth"   Value="88" />
  <Setter Property="FontFamily" Value="{StaticResource UiFont}" />
</Style>

<!-- Keyed: opt-in by key -->
<Style x:Key="PrimaryButton" TargetType="Button"
       BasedOn="{StaticResource {x:Type Button}}">
  <Setter Property="Background" Value="{StaticResource AccentBrush}" />
  <Setter Property="Foreground" Value="White" />
  <Setter Property="FontWeight" Value="SemiBold" />
</Style>

<Button Style="{StaticResource PrimaryButton}" Content="Save" />

BasedOn="{StaticResource {x:Type Button}}" is the idiom for "inherit from the implicit style": an implicit style is stored under its target type as the key, so this finds it and extends it. Without it, a keyed style silently replaces the implicit one instead of extending it, and the button loses the padding and font you set for every button. The theme's own style still applies underneath, so the button keeps its default look. Its default look is lost only when a style replaces Template (your template is drawn instead) or sets OverridesDefaultStyle to True (nothing is drawn at all).

IN PLAIN TERMS

BasedOn is a recipe that says "the house recipe, plus chilli". Leave it out and you have written a new recipe from scratch that happens to include chilli, so the house seasoning is gone. The restaurant's standard plating, which is the theme style, comes with the dish either way.

GOTCHA

Implicit styles do not apply to derived types. A Style TargetType="ToggleButton" will not touch a CheckBox, even though CheckBox derives from ToggleButton, and a Style TargetType="Button" will not touch your own class derived from Button. There is no wildcard. You write one style per concrete type, usually with BasedOn chaining to a shared base.

Trigger types

A button should change colour on hover, fade when disabled, and show an asterisk when there are unsaved changes. Doing that from event handlers means writing the change and its undo for every case. A trigger is a condition plus setters that apply only while the condition holds. EventTrigger is the exception: it has no setters, and instead starts an animation when an event occurs.

The style below shows all five kinds in one place, and the comments say what each one watches. The difference that matters most: a Trigger watches a property of the element itself, while a DataTrigger watches a binding, usually a view-model property. Animations, which EventTrigger starts, are covered in Part 18.

XAML 
<Style TargetType="Button" x:Key="PrimaryButton">
  <Setter Property="Background" Value="{StaticResource AccentBrush}" />

  <Style.Triggers>

    <!-- Property trigger: one dependency property on THIS element -->
    <Trigger Property="IsMouseOver" Value="True">
      <Setter Property="Background" Value="{StaticResource AccentHoverBrush}" />
    </Trigger>

    <Trigger Property="IsEnabled" Value="False">
      <Setter Property="Opacity" Value="0.45" />
    </Trigger>

    <!-- MultiTrigger: all conditions must hold -->
    <MultiTrigger>
      <MultiTrigger.Conditions>
        <Condition Property="IsMouseOver" Value="True" />
        <Condition Property="IsPressed"   Value="True" />
      </MultiTrigger.Conditions>
      <Setter Property="RenderTransform">
        <Setter.Value><ScaleTransform ScaleX="0.98" ScaleY="0.98" /></Setter.Value>
      </Setter>
    </MultiTrigger>

    <!-- DataTrigger: any BINDING, so any view-model property -->
    <DataTrigger Binding="{Binding IsDirty}" Value="True">
      <Setter Property="Content" Value="Save *" />
    </DataTrigger>

    <!-- MultiDataTrigger: several bindings -->
    <MultiDataTrigger>
      <MultiDataTrigger.Conditions>
        <Condition Binding="{Binding IsDirty}"    Value="True" />
        <Condition Binding="{Binding IsReadOnly}" Value="False" />
      </MultiDataTrigger.Conditions>
      <Setter Property="IsDefault" Value="True" />
    </MultiDataTrigger>

    <!-- EventTrigger: starts a storyboard, cannot set properties -->
    <EventTrigger RoutedEvent="Button.Click">
      <BeginStoryboard>
        <Storyboard>
          <DoubleAnimation Storyboard.TargetProperty="Opacity"
                           From="1" To="0.6" Duration="0:0:0.08" AutoReverse="True" />
        </Storyboard>
      </BeginStoryboard>
    </EventTrigger>

  </Style.Triggers>
</Style>

Read that figure slowly, because three triggers can fail without an error. The asterisk never appears if the button sets its own Content, because a local value outranks a style trigger (Part 5). The hover colour never appears because the default template's own hover trigger paints the border inside the template directly, so the Background your trigger changes is not what you see; Part 10 shows how replacing the template puts you in charge of that border. And after the first click, the Click animation holds Opacity at 1; an animation outranks every trigger, so the disabled fade stops working.

Triggers are declarative and reversible. When the condition stops holding, the previous value comes back automatically: you never write the "undo". That is the reason to prefer a trigger over an event handler that sets a property.

EventSetter DEEPER

Sometimes every control a style touches needs the same handler, such as a double-click on every row of a list. EventSetter is the one way to attach a handler from a style. It only works in a Style, not a ControlTemplate, and the handler must live in code-behind:

XAML 
<Style TargetType="ListBoxItem">
  <EventSetter Event="MouseDoubleClick" Handler="OnItemDoubleClick" />
</Style>

Conditional styling without triggers

Two alternatives worth knowing, because triggers get verbose. Option A uses one DataTemplate per type (Part 10). Option B uses a value converter, a small class that turns a bound value into another value, here a severity into a brush (Part 11).

XAML 
<!-- A. Style selector via DataTemplate DataType: different type, different look -->
<DataTemplate DataType="{x:Type vm:ErrorMessage}">   ... </DataTemplate>
<DataTemplate DataType="{x:Type vm:InfoMessage}">    ... </DataTemplate>

<!-- B. A converter that maps a value to a brush, for many-valued cases -->
<TextBlock Foreground="{Binding Severity, Converter={StaticResource SeverityToBrush}}" />

Rule: two states, use a trigger. Many states driven by an enum, use a converter. Genuinely different shapes of content, use a DataTemplate per type.

RECAP
  • A style is a reusable set of property values, and an implicit style with no key applies to every control of exactly its target type in scope.
  • Use BasedOn to extend a style instead of replacing it.
  • Implicit styles never apply to derived types, so each concrete type needs its own style.
  • Triggers apply setters while a condition holds and undo them automatically when it stops.
  • A value set directly on the element beats every style setter and trigger.
  • Two states call for a trigger, many enum values for a converter, and different shapes for a DataTemplate per type.
PART 10

Templates

A ControlTemplate replaces a control's appearance while keeping its behaviour. A DataTemplate decides how a data object is drawn. Between them they are the reason WPF is still worth using.

Builds on Part 7, the control content model (content, containers and the visual tree) and Part 9, styles and triggers.

ControlTemplate

A style can only set properties a control already has. Ask for a fully rounded button and you find Button has no CornerRadius to set: the corners belong to elements drawn inside it. To change those, you replace the drawing itself. A ControlTemplate is the tree of elements that draws a control, and every control's template is replaceable.

A Button's behaviour (click detection, keyboard activation, command invocation, IsPressed) lives in ButtonBase. Its appearance is entirely in a replaceable template. Swap the template and you still have a real button.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A control is like a car: the engine, steering and brakes are its behaviour, the bodywork is its appearance. A ControlTemplate is a new body on the same chassis, so a pill-shaped button still clicks, takes keyboard focus and runs its command. Where the analogy stops: the new body shows only the settings you connect to it, so a template that ignores Background makes the button's Background do nothing.

The style below sets ordinary properties first, then replaces Template with a Border that has fully rounded corners and a ContentPresenter inside it. The triggers belong to the template, so they can change the Border directly by its name.

XAML 
<Style x:Key="PillButton" TargetType="Button">
  <Setter Property="Background"      Value="{StaticResource AccentBrush}" />
  <Setter Property="Foreground"      Value="White" />
  <Setter Property="Padding"         Value="16,7" />
  <Setter Property="BorderThickness" Value="1" />
  <Setter Property="Cursor"          Value="Hand" />

  <Setter Property="Template">
    <Setter.Value>
      <ControlTemplate TargetType="Button">
        <Border x:Name="Root"
                Background="{TemplateBinding Background}"
                BorderBrush="{TemplateBinding BorderBrush}"
                BorderThickness="{TemplateBinding BorderThickness}"
                Padding="{TemplateBinding Padding}"
                CornerRadius="999"
                SnapsToDevicePixels="True">
          <ContentPresenter x:Name="Content"
                            HorizontalAlignment="Center"
                            VerticalAlignment="Center"
                            RecognizesAccessKey="True" />
        </Border>

        <ControlTemplate.Triggers>
          <Trigger Property="IsMouseOver" Value="True">
            <Setter TargetName="Root" Property="Opacity" Value="0.9" />
          </Trigger>
          <Trigger Property="IsPressed" Value="True">
            <Setter TargetName="Content" Property="Margin" Value="0,1,0,-1" />
          </Trigger>
          <Trigger Property="IsKeyboardFocused" Value="True">
            <Setter TargetName="Root" Property="BorderBrush"
                    Value="{StaticResource FocusBrush}" />
          </Trigger>
          <Trigger Property="IsEnabled" Value="False">
            <Setter TargetName="Root" Property="Opacity" Value="0.4" />
          </Trigger>
        </ControlTemplate.Triggers>
      </ControlTemplate>
    </Setter.Value>
  </Setter>
</Style>

Three things carry the weight here. The first two refer to the templated parent, which simply means the control the template is applied to: here, the button.

  • TemplateBinding is a lightweight, one-way binding to the templated control's own property, set up when the template is applied. Use it inside templates; it is meaningfully faster than {Binding RelativeSource={RelativeSource TemplatedParent}}. It accepts a converter but does no automatic type conversion. Use the long form when you need two-way, a nested path, a type conversion without a converter, or a binding inside a Freezable such as a brush, where TemplateBinding does not work.
  • ContentPresenter is where Content lands. It automatically picks up ContentTemplate and ContentStringFormat from the templated parent. For an ItemsControl the equivalent is ItemsPresenter.
  • Setter TargetName is only legal inside a template's own triggers (ControlTemplate.Triggers, or DataTemplate.Triggers), not Style.Triggers. A style trigger cannot reach into the template's named parts.
IN PLAIN TERMS

A template is a printed form with blanks. Each TemplateBinding is a blank labelled with a property name, filled in with that button's own value. The ContentPresenter is the large box where whatever the button contains gets drawn. Anything not written as a blank is printed on the form: CornerRadius="999" is fixed, so no button using this template can change it.

The parts-and-states contract DEEPER

If you write a control that others can retemplate, your code still needs particular elements from whatever template it is given, such as the thumb of a slider. The agreement is by name. A template part is a named element, conventionally prefixed PART_, that the control's code looks for.

When you write a custom control you should publish which named parts your template is expected to contain, and look them up defensively. [TemplatePart] documents them for tools and template authors; nothing enforces it at runtime, so a template may leave a part out (Part 27 covers the whole authoring story):

C# 
[TemplatePart(Name = PartTrack,  Type = typeof(FrameworkElement))]
[TemplatePart(Name = PartThumb,  Type = typeof(Thumb))]
public class RangeSlider : Control
{
    public const string PartTrack = "PART_Track";
    public const string PartThumb = "PART_Thumb";

    private Thumb? _thumb;

    static RangeSlider()
    {
        // Tells WPF to look up the default style (and its template) in the theme dictionaries, usually Themes/Generic.xaml
        DefaultStyleKeyProperty.OverrideMetadata(
            typeof(RangeSlider), new FrameworkPropertyMetadata(typeof(RangeSlider)));
    }

    public override void OnApplyTemplate()
    {
        base.OnApplyTemplate();

        if (_thumb is not null) _thumb.DragDelta -= OnDrag;

        _thumb = GetTemplateChild(PartThumb) as Thumb;   // may legitimately be null
        if (_thumb is not null) _thumb.DragDelta += OnDrag;
    }

    private void OnDrag(object sender, DragDeltaEventArgs e) { /* … */ }
}

Unhooking before re-hooking matters: OnApplyTemplate runs again every time the template is replaced, and a theme switch does exactly that.

DataTemplate

Your view models are plain classes that know nothing about the screen. Something has to decide what an InvoiceViewModel looks like when it appears in a ContentControl or a list, and ideally the view model should not be involved in that decision.

A DataTemplate keyed by DataType rather than x:Key becomes an implicit template: any time WPF is asked to render an object of that type without an explicit template, it finds this one.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Implicit templates work like file associations in Windows. Double-click a PDF and the PDF reader opens; you never tell each file which program to use, because the association was registered once, by file type. Put an InvoiceViewModel in a ContentControl and WPF opens it with InvoiceView the same way. Where the analogy stops: with no association registered, WPF does not ask, it shows the ToString() text.

XAML 
<Application.Resources>
  <DataTemplate DataType="{x:Type vm:CustomerViewModel}">
    <views:CustomerView />
  </DataTemplate>
  <DataTemplate DataType="{x:Type vm:InvoiceViewModel}">
    <views:InvoiceView />
  </DataTemplate>
</Application.Resources>

<!-- Anywhere in the app: -->
<ContentControl Content="{Binding CurrentPage}" />

That six-line pattern is view-model-first navigation. Set CurrentPage to an InvoiceViewModel and the invoice view appears. The view's DataContext is the view model it was chosen for, so its bindings work with no further wiring, unless the view sets its own DataContext. No navigation framework, no view locator, no code. It is the single most valuable thing in WPF's design and the thing most worth building your application architecture around.

DataTemplateSelector DEEPER

For when the choice is not by type, say by a property value. A DataTemplateSelector is a class with one method that receives the item and returns the template to use:

C# 
public sealed class MessageTemplateSelector : DataTemplateSelector
{
    public DataTemplate? Compact { get; set; }
    public DataTemplate? Expanded { get; set; }

    public override DataTemplate? SelectTemplate(object? item, DependencyObject container)
        => item is MessageViewModel { IsExpanded: true } ? Expanded : Compact;
}

You give it the candidate templates as properties and set it on the list. CompactMessage and ExpandedMessage are ordinary keyed DataTemplate resources:

XAML 
<ListBox ItemsSource="{Binding Messages}">
  <ListBox.ItemTemplateSelector>
    <sel:MessageTemplateSelector
        Compact="{StaticResource CompactMessage}"
        Expanded="{StaticResource ExpandedMessage}" />
  </ListBox.ItemTemplateSelector>
</ListBox>
GOTCHA

SelectTemplate is called once when the container is created, not when your properties change. If IsExpanded flips later, nothing re-runs. For state that changes, use a single template with a DataTrigger swapping ContentTemplate, or trigger container regeneration.

Finding the default template of a built-in control

Before you write a template from scratch, start from the real one. The default template already handles states you would otherwise forget, such as keyboard focus and disabled. Three routes:

  • The WPF source at dotnet/wpf, in src/…/Themes/PresentationFramework.Aero2/Themes/Aero2.NormalColor.xaml.
  • Right-click a control in the Visual Studio designer → Edit Template → Edit a Copy, which emits the full default into your dictionary.
  • Programmatically, for the curious:
C# 
// The theme style for a type lives under the type itself as a resource key.
var style = (Style?)Application.Current.TryFindResource(typeof(Button));

var template = style?.Setters
    .OfType<Setter>()
    .FirstOrDefault(x => x.Property == Control.TemplateProperty)
    ?.Value as ControlTemplate;

if (template is not null)
{
    var settings = new XmlWriterSettings { Indent = true, IndentChars = "  " };
    using var writer = XmlWriter.Create(Console.Out, settings);
    XamlWriter.Save(template, writer);
}
RECAP
  • A ControlTemplate replaces how a control looks, while its behaviour stays in the control class.
  • Inside a template, TemplateBinding connects elements to the control's properties and ContentPresenter shows its content.
  • Triggers inside a template can change named elements with TargetName, and style triggers cannot.
  • A DataTemplate with a DataType and no key is used for every object of that type, which gives you view-model-first navigation.
  • A DataTemplateSelector runs when an item's container is created, and again when a recycled container is reused, but not when the item's properties change.
  • Start a new template from a copy of the default one.
PART 11

Data binding

The engine everything else leans on.

Builds on Part 3, XAML precisely (markup extensions such as {Binding}), Part 5, dependency properties and Part 8, resources.

Without binding, you write code to copy a customer's name into a text box when the form opens, more code to copy it back when the user types, and more again whenever the name changes somewhere else. Every missed copy is a bug where the screen and the data disagree.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A binding is that copying done for you. Instead of a one-off assignment, you leave a standing instruction: "keep this box showing Customer.Name", and, if you ask, "write the user's edits back". The instruction stays in force until you replace or clear it. It can only react to changes the source announces, though, which is the job of INotifyPropertyChanged (or of a dependency property, when the source is another element).

Precisely: a binding is an object that watches a source property and pushes values into a target dependency property, with optional conversion, validation and fallbacks. The target is the property on the element that shows the value, such as TextBox.Text, and it must be a dependency property. The source is the object that owns the data, usually a view model.

Anatomy

Every binding follows the same route. Knowing the hops tells you where to look when a value does not appear.

Reading the figure left to right: the binding starts from a source object (by default the element's DataContext, explained in the next section), follows the Path to a property, optionally passes the value through a converter, and sets the target. INotifyPropertyChanged is a one-event interface a class implements to announce "this property just changed". Without it the binding picks up the value when it starts and misses every later change. For a two-way binding the value also travels back, from target to source, when the update trigger fires.

The markup below shows most of a binding's options at once; no real text box needs them all. The table explains the ones that change behaviour most.

XAML 
<TextBox Text="{Binding Path=Customer.Name,
                        Mode=TwoWay,
                        UpdateSourceTrigger=PropertyChanged,
                        Converter={StaticResource Trim},
                        ConverterParameter=NFC,
                        StringFormat='{}{0}',
                        FallbackValue='(unavailable)',
                        TargetNullValue='(none)',
                        Delay=300,
                        ValidatesOnDataErrors=True,
                        NotifyOnValidationError=True}" />
PartMeaning
PathDotted property path from the source. Path= is optional in first position.
ModeOneWay, TwoWay, OneWayToSource, OneTime, or Default (per-property metadata; TextBox.Text defaults to two-way).
UpdateSourceTriggerWhen the target pushes back: LostFocus (default for Text), PropertyChanged, Explicit.
FallbackValueUsed when the binding fails to resolve at all.
TargetNullValueUsed when the source resolves to null.
DelayMilliseconds to debounce source updates. Excellent on search boxes.

Two groups of settings in the example are not in the table. StringFormat applies a .NET format string, and only takes effect when the target property is a string; the leading {} stops XAML reading {0} as the start of a markup extension. Converter and ConverterParameter are covered under Converters below. ValidatesOnDataErrors turns on checks from the older IDataErrorInfo interface, and NotifyOnValidationError raises the Validation.Error event on the element; Part 13 covers validation and why new code uses INotifyDataErrorInfo instead.

Two settings cause most of the early surprises: Mode, which way values flow, and UpdateSourceTrigger, when a two-way binding writes back. With the defaults, a TextBox leaves your view model untouched while the user types, and writes the text only when focus leaves the box. Set PropertyChanged to write on every change, and add Delay to write once the user pauses.

Where the source comes from

A binding needs an object to read from. You can point it at one explicitly, but most bindings do not: they use the DataContext. DataContext is a property every element has, and its value is inherited by the elements inside it. Set it once on a window and every binding in the window reads from that object.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Setting DataContext on a window is like telling a team "unless I say otherwise, take your questions to Anna". Every control inside asks Anna. ElementName, RelativeSource and Source are the "unless I say otherwise": each points one binding at someone else without changing the default for anyone. Unlike a team rule, any element can set its own DataContext, and that changes the default for everything inside that element.

A binding uses exactly one of the sources below, or none, in which case it uses the inherited DataContext:

XAML 
<!-- 1. DataContext (inherited down the logical tree). The normal case. -->
<TextBlock Text="{Binding Title}" />

<!-- 2. ElementName: another named element in the same name scope -->
<Slider x:Name="Zoom" Minimum="0.5" Maximum="3" />
<TextBlock Text="{Binding Value, ElementName=Zoom, StringFormat={}{0:P0}}" />

<!-- 3. RelativeSource Self: this element -->
<TextBox ToolTip="{Binding Text, RelativeSource={RelativeSource Self}}" />

<!-- 4. RelativeSource AncestorType: walk up the visual tree -->
<Button Command="{Binding DataContext.DeleteCommand,
                          RelativeSource={RelativeSource AncestorType=DataGrid}}"
        CommandParameter="{Binding}" />

<!-- 5. RelativeSource TemplatedParent: inside a ControlTemplate -->
<TextBlock Text="{Binding Header, RelativeSource={RelativeSource TemplatedParent}}" />

<!-- 6. Source: an explicit object, usually a resource or a static -->
<TextBlock Text="{Binding Version, Source={x:Static app:BuildInfo.Current}}" />

A few terms from those comments. A name scope is the set of x:Names that can see each other, typically one window, user control or template. The visual tree is the full tree of elements that are actually drawn, including the parts inside control templates. TemplatedParent means the control that a ControlTemplate is drawing (Part 10).

Case 4 is the one you will write most after DataContext. A button inside a grid row has the row's item as its DataContext, but the delete command lives on the grid's view model. The binding walks up to the DataGrid, reads its DataContext, and CommandParameter="{Binding}" passes the row item itself.

AncestorLevel disambiguates nesting: {RelativeSource AncestorType=ListBox, AncestorLevel=2} skips the first ListBox and finds the one above it.

Path syntax

The Path is a small language of its own. Most bindings use only the first form, a dotted chain of property names; the others are here so you recognise them.

XAML 
{Binding Customer.Address.City}          <!-- nested -->
{Binding Items[0].Title}                 <!-- indexer -->
{Binding Tags[status]}                   <!-- string key, no quotes needed -->
{Binding .}                              <!-- the DataContext itself -->
{Binding}                                <!-- same thing, shorter -->
{Binding (Validation.HasError), RelativeSource={RelativeSource Self}}   <!-- attached prop -->
{Binding Path=(vm:Doc.Title)}            <!-- attached prop, qualified -->
{Binding Items/Title}                    <!-- current item of a collection view -->

An attached property (Part 5) is one defined by one class but set on elements of another, such as Grid.Row or Validation.HasError. The slash form in the last line reads a property of a collection view's current item, a concept from Part 12.

GOTCHA

Attached properties in a Path must be parenthesised. {Binding Validation.HasError} looks for a property called Validation and fails silently; {Binding (Validation.HasError)} is correct.

Converters

A view model exposes data in its own terms, for example a bool IsBusy. The element often wants something else: Visibility is an enum with Visible, Hidden and Collapsed, not a bool. A value converter is a small class implementing IValueConverter that translates between the two: Convert runs on the way to the target, ConvertBack on the way back to the source.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A converter is a travel plug adapter: the view model offers one shape, the element accepts another, and the adapter joins them without either side changing. Like an adapter, it should do nothing but make the connection fit. Deciding anything belongs to the view model, which is the rule at the end of this section.

The converter below turns a bool into a Visibility. Its two properties let one class cover three common cases. ConvertBack throws, which is the convention for a converter meant only for one-way bindings.

C# 
[ValueConversion(typeof(bool), typeof(Visibility))]
public sealed class BoolToVisibilityConverter : IValueConverter
{
    public Visibility WhenFalse { get; set; } = Visibility.Collapsed;
    public bool Invert { get; set; }

    public object Convert(object? value, Type targetType, object? parameter, CultureInfo culture)
    {
        bool flag = value is true;
        if (Invert) flag = !flag;
        return flag ? Visibility.Visible : WhenFalse;
    }

    public object ConvertBack(object? value, Type targetType, object? parameter, CultureInfo culture)
        => throw new NotSupportedException();
}
XAML 
<Application.Resources>
  <conv:BoolToVisibilityConverter x:Key="BoolToVis" />
  <conv:BoolToVisibilityConverter x:Key="BoolToVisInverted" Invert="True" />
  <conv:BoolToVisibilityConverter x:Key="BoolToHidden" WhenFalse="Hidden" />
</Application.Resources>

<ProgressBar Visibility="{Binding IsBusy, Converter={StaticResource BoolToVis}}" />

WPF already ships a BooleanToVisibilityConverter; the custom one earns its place with the Invert and WhenFalse options.

Multi-value bindings combine several sources. A MultiBinding feeds several child bindings into one converter, which implements IMultiValueConverter and receives all the current values as an array. The classic case is enabling a button on a conjunction of conditions:

C# 
public sealed class AllTrueConverter : IMultiValueConverter
{
    public object Convert(object[] values, Type t, object? p, CultureInfo c)
        => values.All(v => v is true);

    public object[] ConvertBack(object value, Type[] t, object? p, CultureInfo c)
        => throw new NotSupportedException();
}
XAML 
<Button Content="Submit">
  <Button.IsEnabled>
    <MultiBinding Converter="{StaticResource AllTrue}">
      <Binding Path="IsValid" />
      <Binding Path="IsConnected" />
      <Binding Path="HasPermission" />
    </MultiBinding>
  </Button.IsEnabled>
</Button>

Whenever any of the three source properties changes, the converter runs again with all three values. A child binding that fails to resolve passes DependencyProperty.UnsetValue rather than a bool, which v is true treats as false.

RULE

Converters are presentation glue, not logic. A BoolToVisibility or a ByteCountToHumanString is fine. A converter that queries a database or decides business rules belongs on the view model as a computed property. If a converter needs a ConverterParameter to know what it's doing, that's usually a sign it should be two converters or one view-model property.

Making binding failures loud

Bindings fail silently by design. A misspelled path does not stop the build and does not throw: WPF writes a message to the debug output and leaves the target at its fallback or default value, so the screen just shows nothing. In development, do not accept that.

To investigate one suspicious binding, raise its trace level. The output window then shows each step the binding took while resolving its path, including the one where it failed.

XAML 
<!-- Per binding: raises the trace level for this one binding only -->
xmlns:diag="clr-namespace:System.Diagnostics;assembly=WindowsBase"
<TextBlock Text="{Binding Whatever, diag:PresentationTraceSources.TraceLevel=High}" />

To catch every broken binding in the app, attach a listener once at startup, for example in the App constructor. In Debug builds each binding error is then written to the output with its message, which names the missing property and the type it was looked for on. It breaks into the debugger only when one is attached, so the app still runs normally outside Visual Studio.

C# 
// App-wide, in Debug builds: log every binding error, and break on it under a debugger.
#if DEBUG
PresentationTraceSources.Refresh();
PresentationTraceSources.DataBindingSource.Listeners.Add(new BindingErrorListener());
PresentationTraceSources.DataBindingSource.Switch.Level = SourceLevels.Error;
#endif

internal sealed class BindingErrorListener : TraceListener
{
    public override void Write(string? message) { }

    public override void WriteLine(string? message)
    {
        Debug.WriteLine("Binding error: " + message);
        if (Debugger.IsAttached) Debugger.Break();
    }
}

Also set Tools → Options → Debugging → Output Window → WPF Trace Settings → Data Binding to Warning. Most "the value just doesn't show up" bugs are a misspelled path that has been printing an error to the output window the whole time.

Binding in code DEEPER

Occasionally you need to create a binding at runtime, for example for columns generated from data. {Binding} in XAML creates an instance of this same Binding class, so every option above is a property you can set from C#.

C# 
var binding = new Binding(nameof(TaskViewModel.Title))
{
    Source = viewModel,
    Mode = BindingMode.TwoWay,
    UpdateSourceTrigger = UpdateSourceTrigger.PropertyChanged,
    Converter = new TrimConverter(),
};
BindingOperations.SetBinding(textBox, TextBox.TextProperty, binding);

// Later:
BindingOperations.ClearBinding(textBox, TextBox.TextProperty);

// Force a push/pull on an Explicit binding:
textBox.GetBindingExpression(TextBox.TextProperty)?.UpdateSource();
textBox.GetBindingExpression(TextBox.TextProperty)?.UpdateTarget();

A binding with UpdateSourceTrigger=Explicit never writes back on its own. Calling UpdateSource is how you commit it, which suits a dialog that should change nothing until the user presses OK. UpdateTarget goes the other way and re-reads the source.

RECAP
  • A binding keeps a property on an element in step with a property on a source object, so you stop writing copy code.
  • With no explicit source, a binding reads from the DataContext inherited from the elements around it.
  • The source must announce changes, through INotifyPropertyChanged or by being a dependency property such as a Slider's Value, or the screen shows the first value and never updates.
  • Mode decides which way values flow, and UpdateSourceTrigger decides when a text box writes back.
  • Converters change a value's type or shape for display; decisions belong in the view model.
  • A broken binding fails silently, so turn up binding trace output while you develop.
PART 12

Collections and views

A list on screen is a live view of a collection.

Builds on Part 11, data binding and Part 10, templates (item templates).

The notification contract

Controls that show many items, such as ListBox, ComboBox and DataGrid, have an ItemsSource property you bind to a collection. Bind it to a plain List<T> and the items appear once. Add an item afterwards and the screen does not change, because a List<T> has no way to tell anyone it changed.

For a bound list to update itself, two interfaces matter and they do different jobs:

  • INotifyCollectionChanged announces that the collection changed: items added, removed, moved, or reset (the whole contents replaced at once, which is what Clear raises). ObservableCollection<T> implements it.
  • INotifyPropertyChanged announces that an item changed. Your item type must implement this itself; ObservableCollection does nothing for you here.

A list of plain records inside an ObservableCollection will update when you add or remove, and will never update when you edit a field. This surprises people weekly.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Picture a noticeboard. INotifyCollectionChanged is the board calling out "a notice was pinned up" or "a notice was taken down". INotifyPropertyChanged is a single notice calling out "my text was just edited". ObservableCollection<T> gives the board a voice, but it cannot give the notices one: each item class has to announce its own changes.

CollectionView: sorting, filtering, grouping

Users want the task list sorted by due date, narrowed to what they typed and grouped by project. You could keep a second, sorted and filtered copy of the collection, but then every add, remove and edit has to update both. WPF puts a layer between the collection and the control instead.

A collection view (ICollectionView) is a sorted, filtered and grouped way of looking at a collection, plus a current item, a position marker that list controls can keep in step with their selection. The collection itself is never reordered.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A collection view works like a saved search in an email client. The mailbox keeps every message in arrival order; the saved search shows only unread mail from one sender, newest first. Deleting the saved search deletes no mail, and a new message that matches shows up in it. Where the comparison breaks: by default, editing an existing item does not re-filter or re-sort it until you call Refresh (live shaping, below, changes that).

Every ItemsSource binding is silently wrapped in an ICollectionView. That automatic one is the collection's default view: there is one per collection, shared by every control bound to it. You can get at it, or declare your own with a CollectionViewSource, a XAML-friendly object that creates a separate view over its Source.

The markup below sorts by due date, then by title, and groups by project. SortDescription lives in a CLR namespace that XAML does not map by default, hence the scm prefix and the xmlns comment in the middle. The GroupStyle gives each group a header; inside its template the DataContext is the group, whose Name is the value being grouped on.

XAML 
<Window.Resources>
  <CollectionViewSource x:Key="SortedTasks" Source="{Binding Tasks}">
    <CollectionViewSource.SortDescriptions>
      <scm:SortDescription PropertyName="DueDate" Direction="Ascending" />
      <scm:SortDescription PropertyName="Title"   Direction="Ascending" />
    </CollectionViewSource.SortDescriptions>
    <CollectionViewSource.GroupDescriptions>
      <PropertyGroupDescription PropertyName="Project" />
    </CollectionViewSource.GroupDescriptions>
  </CollectionViewSource>
</Window.Resources>

<!-- xmlns:scm="clr-namespace:System.ComponentModel;assembly=WindowsBase" -->

<ListBox ItemsSource="{Binding Source={StaticResource SortedTasks}}">
  <ListBox.GroupStyle>
    <GroupStyle>
      <GroupStyle.HeaderTemplate>
        <DataTemplate>
          <TextBlock Text="{Binding Name}" FontWeight="SemiBold"
                     Margin="0,10,0,4" Opacity=".7" />
        </DataTemplate>
      </GroupStyle.HeaderTemplate>
    </GroupStyle>
  </ListBox.GroupStyle>
</ListBox>

Run as written, the groups come out right but every row shows a type name. The figure shows why, and the one-attribute fix.

From the view model, the same thing with a live filter that follows a search box. The class uses the Community Toolkit's [ObservableProperty], covered in Part 14: read it as a Search property that raises change notifications and calls OnSearchChanged whenever its value changes.

C# 
public partial class TaskListViewModel : ObservableObject
{
    public ObservableCollection<TaskViewModel> Tasks { get; } = [];
    public ICollectionView View { get; }

    [ObservableProperty]
    public partial string Search { get; set; }

    public TaskListViewModel()
    {
        View = CollectionViewSource.GetDefaultView(Tasks);
        View.SortDescriptions.Add(new SortDescription(nameof(TaskViewModel.DueDate),
                                                      ListSortDirection.Ascending));
        View.Filter = o => o is TaskViewModel t &&
            (Search.Length == 0 ||
             t.Title.Contains(Search, StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase));
        Search = "";
    }

    partial void OnSearchChanged(string value) => View.Refresh();
}

View.Filter is a predicate: return true to keep an item. The view cannot know that the predicate reads Search, so OnSearchChanged calls Refresh to run it again. That is also why Search is set last in the constructor: setting it calls OnSearchChanged, which needs View. Bind the list with ItemsSource="{Binding View}" and a text box to Search with UpdateSourceTrigger=PropertyChanged, and the list narrows as the user types.

GOTCHA

View.Refresh() re-evaluates the filter over the whole collection and resets selection. On a large list, debounce it: a Delay=300 on the search box binding, or a timer. And note that GetDefaultView returns a shared view: two ListBoxes bound to the same collection share sorting, filtering and current item. Use separate CollectionViewSource instances when you need them independent.

Live shaping DEEPER

By default a view places an item when it is added, not when it is edited: rename a task and it keeps its old sort position, and stays visible even if it no longer matches the filter. ListCollectionView, the view WPF creates over an ObservableCollection, implements ICollectionViewLiveShaping to fix that. On a CollectionViewSource, set IsLiveFilteringRequested and IsLiveSortingRequested to true and list the properties to watch in LiveFilteringProperties and LiveSortingProperties. Grouping has the matching IsLiveGroupingRequested and LiveGroupingProperties.

For a view created in code, cast the result of GetDefaultView to ICollectionViewLiveShaping, check CanChangeLiveFiltering, then set IsLiveFiltering to true and add property names to LiveFilteringProperties (sorting has the matching members). Either way, edits to those properties then re-filter and re-sort without Refresh, provided the items raise PropertyChanged; a DataGrid needs none of this for its own edits, because committing a row edit re-positions that row in a sorted or filtered view.

Large lists DEEPER

Beyond roughly 10,000 items, ObservableCollection plus a filter starts to hurt, because every change notification walks the view. Options, in order of effort:

  • Virtualise properly. Virtualisation means the list creates visual rows only for the items on screen, and Recycling reuses those rows as you scroll instead of building new ones. Grouping turns virtualisation off unless you also set VirtualizingPanel.IsVirtualizingWhenGrouping="True". Confirm VirtualizationMode="Recycling" and that you have not disabled virtualisation by accident (Part 19 lists the ways).
  • Filter at the source. Query the database or the in-memory index and replace the collection, rather than filtering a view over everything.
  • Batch. ObservableCollection raises one event per Add. Build the list first, then assign, or use a collection that supports range operations.
  • Data virtualisation. Implement IList over a paged, lazily-loaded backing store so only the visible window is materialised. Non-trivial; only do it when you must.

Cross-thread collection updates

Loading data on a background thread keeps the window responsive, but adding to a bound ObservableCollection from a background thread throws a NotSupportedException. A bound collection is effectively part of the screen, and only the UI thread may change the screen (Part 17 covers the threading model).

IN PLAIN TERMS

Think of a whiteboard in a room where only one person, the UI thread, may redraw anything. Fix A passes that person a note for each change. Fix B puts a single key on the table: any thread may change the list while holding the key, and WPF takes the same key whenever it reads the list. The redrawing still happens only on the UI thread: WPF forwards each change notice there itself.

Two fixes. In A, the Dispatcher is the UI thread's work queue, and InvokeAsync puts a piece of work on it:

C# 
// A. Marshal to the UI thread (fine for occasional updates).
await Application.Current.Dispatcher.InvokeAsync(() => Tasks.Add(item));

// B. Enable collection synchronisation (better for high-frequency streams).
private readonly object _lock = new();

public TaskListViewModel()
{
    BindingOperations.EnableCollectionSynchronization(Tasks, _lock);
}

// Then from any thread:
lock (_lock) { Tasks.Add(item); }

Option B lets WPF take the same lock while it reads the collection during layout, so background producers don't have to hop threads for every item. It is the right answer for telemetry feeds and log tails.

RECAP
  • ObservableCollection<T> tells the list when items are added or removed, but each item must raise PropertyChanged for edits to show.
  • Every bound collection is shown through a collection view, which sorts, filters and groups without changing the collection.
  • The default view is shared by every control bound to the same collection; use a CollectionViewSource when two lists need independent views.
  • After changing what a filter depends on, call Refresh, and debounce it on large lists.
  • Only the UI thread may change a bound collection, unless you enable collection synchronisation with a lock.
PART 13

Validation

Two places a rule can live, and one way to show what they find. Pick the mechanism that matches where the rule belongs.

Builds on Part 11, data binding, Part 9, styles and triggers and Part 10, templates.

A form that accepts anything and complains only when you press Save is frustrating: people want to know which field is wrong, next to that field, while they are filling it in. WPF's validation system keeps track of which bindings currently hold an error and gives every control a way to show it. The mechanisms differ in where the rule is written and what it can see.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Picture two desks. A ValidationRule is the front desk checking that a form is legible before it goes anywhere: "that is not a date". INotifyDataErrorInfo is the back office that knows the business: "a task needs a title". Both send their complaints to the same place, the error display on the control. The difference that matters: a form the front desk rejects normally never reaches the back office, while the back office has already filed the form by the time it complains.

1. ValidationRule: rules about the input

A ValidationRule is a class attached to one binding. By default it runs on the binding before conversion, on the raw value the user typed, and either accepts it or returns an error message. At that default step a rejected value never reaches your view model; the rule's ValidationStep property can move it later in the pipeline. That makes it right for rules about the input itself, such as "this must parse as a date".

This rule rejects empty or whitespace text. FieldName is an ordinary property, so each use in XAML can set it:

C# 
public sealed class RequiredRule : ValidationRule
{
    public string FieldName { get; set; } = "Value";

    public override ValidationResult Validate(object? value, CultureInfo culture)
        => string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(value as string)
            ? new ValidationResult(false, $"{FieldName} is required.")
            : ValidationResult.ValidResult;
}
XAML 
<TextBox>
  <TextBox.Text>
    <Binding Path="Title" UpdateSourceTrigger="PropertyChanged">
      <Binding.ValidationRules>
        <rules:RequiredRule FieldName="Title" />
      </Binding.ValidationRules>
    </Binding>
  </TextBox.Text>
</TextBox>

Attaching a rule needs the long, property-element form of the binding, because ValidationRules is a collection and cannot be filled in inside {Binding ...} braces. With UpdateSourceTrigger=PropertyChanged, clearing the box shows the error at once rather than when focus leaves.

2. INotifyDataErrorInfo: rules about the model

Most rules are about your data rather than about keystrokes: a task needs a title, progress is a percentage, an end date follows a start date. Those rules belong with the data, where a unit test can reach them and the Save command can check them.

INotifyDataErrorInfo is the interface a source object implements to report such errors: a HasErrors flag, a GetErrors method that returns the errors for one property, and an ErrorsChanged event that bindings listen to. It is the modern interface, and the one you want by default. It is async-friendly, and supports multiple errors per property and object-level errors. IDataErrorInfo is its older predecessor (WPF has supported it since .NET Framework 3.5): synchronous, one error per property. Do not start new work with it.

You rarely implement it by hand. The Community Toolkit's ObservableValidator base class (the toolkit is covered in Part 14) implements it, driven by the standard attributes from System.ComponentModel.DataAnnotations. [NotifyDataErrorInfo] makes the generated setter validate the property each time it is set.

C# 
// CommunityToolkit.Mvvm implements INotifyDataErrorInfo for you.
public partial class TaskEditorViewModel : ObservableValidator
{
    [ObservableProperty]
    [NotifyDataErrorInfo]
    [Required(ErrorMessage = "Give the task a title.")]
    [MinLength(3, ErrorMessage = "At least three characters.")]
    public partial string Title { get; set; }

    [ObservableProperty]
    [NotifyDataErrorInfo]
    [Range(0, 100, ErrorMessage = "Progress is a percentage.")]
    public partial int Progress { get; set; }

    [RelayCommand(CanExecute = nameof(CanSave))]
    private async Task SaveAsync(CancellationToken ct) { /* … */ }

    private bool CanSave() => !HasErrors;

    public TaskEditorViewModel()
    {
        ErrorsChanged += (_, _) => SaveCommand.NotifyCanExecuteChanged();
    }
}

Look at the last three members together. CanSave asks HasErrors, and the constructor tells the command to ask again whenever the set of errors changes, so the Save button greys out while any field is invalid. One consequence of validating in the setter: a property nobody has set has not been checked, so a fresh form reports no errors. The last section of this part deals with that.

Bind with ValidatesOnNotifyDataErrors=True, which is the default when the source implements the interface. No extra markup is needed:

XAML 
<TextBox Text="{Binding Title, UpdateSourceTrigger=PropertyChanged}" />

3. Showing the errors

The default error visual is a red border and nothing else: no message. Replace Validation.ErrorTemplate to do better. The error template is shown as an adorner, a visual drawn in a separate layer on top of an element (Part 24 covers adorners in general).

IN PLAIN TERMS

The error template is a sticky note on a sheet of glass in front of the form. It can frame the field or hang a message under it, but it never pushes the other fields along. So a message placed below a text box overlaps whatever sits below it: leave room with a margin.

The style below gives every TextBox an error template with a rounded border and the first error message underneath. Its trigger also puts that message in the tooltip while the box has an error.

XAML 
<Style TargetType="TextBox">
  <Setter Property="Validation.ErrorTemplate">
    <Setter.Value>
      <ControlTemplate>
        <StackPanel>
          <Border BorderBrush="{StaticResource DangerBrush}" BorderThickness="1"
                  CornerRadius="4">
            <AdornedElementPlaceholder x:Name="Adorned" />
          </Border>
          <TextBlock Margin="2,3,0,0" FontSize="11"
                     Foreground="{StaticResource DangerBrush}"
                     Text="{Binding [0].ErrorContent}" />
        </StackPanel>
      </ControlTemplate>
    </Setter.Value>
  </Setter>

  <Style.Triggers>
    <Trigger Property="Validation.HasError" Value="True">
      <Setter Property="ToolTip"
              Value="{Binding (Validation.Errors)[0].ErrorContent,
                              RelativeSource={RelativeSource Self}}" />
    </Trigger>
  </Style.Triggers>
</Style>

AdornedElementPlaceholder is where the original control is rendered, so the border wraps the real text box. Inside the error template, the DataContext is the control's list of errors, a ReadOnlyObservableCollection<ValidationError>; hence [0].ErrorContent, the message of the first error.

GOTCHA

Error adorners are drawn in the AdornerLayer, which is created by the nearest AdornerDecorator. A Window's default template has one; a Popup, a ToolTip and some custom templates do not. If your error visuals vanish inside a popup, add an <AdornerDecorator> around its content.

Validating the whole form before save

Errors appear only for values that have been checked, and values are checked when they change. Someone who opens a blank form and presses Save straight away has changed nothing, so nothing has reported an error yet. Check everything explicitly before saving.

With ObservableValidator, ValidateAllProperties runs every rule on every property and raises the errors that were waiting. If your rules are ValidationRules, the view model cannot see them, so you have to ask the view: the helper below walks the element tree and fails if any element has an error.

C# 
// View model side (ObservableValidator):
ValidateAllProperties();
if (HasErrors) return;

// If you must check the view's bindings (ValidationRules case):
public static bool IsValid(DependencyObject node)
{
    if (Validation.GetHasError(node)) return false;
    return LogicalTreeHelper.GetChildren(node)
        .OfType<DependencyObject>()
        .All(IsValid);
}

The walk follows the logical tree, so it does not look inside control templates. It also finds only errors that already exist: a ValidationRule runs when a value moves towards the source, or also when the binding first loads if you set the rule's ValidatesOnTargetUpdated to true.

RECAP
  • Use a ValidationRule for rules about what the user typed; at its default step a rejected value never reaches the view model.
  • Put rules about your data in the view model through INotifyDataErrorInfo, which ObservableValidator implements for you.
  • Bindings show INotifyDataErrorInfo errors with no extra markup.
  • The default error visual is a red border with no message; replace Validation.ErrorTemplate to show the text.
  • Error templates are adorners: they take no layout space and need an AdornerDecorator above them.
  • Call ValidateAllProperties before saving, because fields the user never touched have not been checked.
PART 14

MVVM with the Community Toolkit

This is the part that has changed most since the books. Everything below replaces roughly two hundred lines of hand-written boilerplate in every old sample you'll read.

Builds on Part 11, data binding, Part 6, routed events and commands and Part 10, templates.

What MVVM actually buys you

Put a screen's logic in its event handlers and it works, until you want to test it without opening a window, give it a different layout, or find which of forty handlers changed a value. MVVM (Model, View, ViewModel) is a way of splitting each screen so that the XAML only displays state and forwards input, while an ordinary C# class holds the state and the actions.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A view model is the screen with the pixels taken out: what the title says, whether Save is available, which task is selected, what happens when Delete is pressed. The XAML draws that state and passes the user's typing and clicks to it through bindings and commands. Because the view model is plain C#, a unit test can press Delete on it without a window ever existing.

Testability is part of the payoff, but not all of it: WPF's binding and templating engine is designed around this split. An implicit DataTemplate (one with a DataType and no key) chooses visuals by the type of the data object, which pays off once every screen and every row is a view model type. Triggers only reach state that is bound. Fight MVVM and you fight the framework.

The division:

  • Model: domain types and services. Knows nothing about WPF.
  • ViewModel: state and commands for one screen or one item. Knows nothing about WPF types, though it may know about abstractions like IDialogService. No using System.Windows; the usual exception is the collection view from Part 12 (CollectionViewSource lives in System.Windows.Data), which you either accept or wrap behind your own interface.
  • View: XAML plus, ideally, an empty code-behind, the .xaml.cs file paired with the XAML. Not zero code-behind as a religion; view-only concerns (focus management, animations, drag adorners) legitimately live there.

Observable properties

For the screen to follow a property, the property has to raise PropertyChanged (Part 11). Written by hand, that means a backing field and a setter with an equality check and a notification call, repeated for every property, plus extra notifications for any property computed from it. This is what every property looks like in older samples:

The old way 
public class TaskViewModel : INotifyPropertyChanged
{
    private string _title = "";
    public string Title
    {
        get => _title;
        set
        {
            if (_title == value) return;
            _title = value;
            OnPropertyChanged();
            OnPropertyChanged(nameof(DisplayName));
        }
    }

    public event PropertyChangedEventHandler? PropertyChanged;
    protected void OnPropertyChanged([CallerMemberName] string? n = null)
        => PropertyChanged?.Invoke(this, new PropertyChangedEventArgs(n));
}

The CommunityToolkit.Mvvm package produces all of that from attributes. It uses a source generator, a compiler plug-in that writes extra C# into your class during the build. That is why the class and the property are marked partial: you write one half, the generator writes the other. The class derives from ObservableObject, the toolkit's base class that implements INotifyPropertyChanged for you.

The current way 
using CommunityToolkit.Mvvm.ComponentModel;

public partial class TaskViewModel : ObservableObject
{
    public int Id { get; init; }

    [ObservableProperty]
    [NotifyPropertyChangedFor(nameof(DisplayName))]
    public partial string Title { get; set; }

    [ObservableProperty]
    [NotifyPropertyChangedFor(nameof(DisplayName))]
    public partial bool IsDone { get; set; }

    public string DisplayName => IsDone ? $"\u2713 {Title}" : Title;

    public TaskViewModel() => Title = "";   // see the note below
}

[NotifyPropertyChangedFor] makes the generated setters for Title and IsDone also announce DisplayName, which is computed from both.

GOTCHA

A partial property declaration cannot have an initialiser. public partial string Title { get; set; } = ""; does not compile, because the generator supplies the implementing declaration and that is where an auto-property initialiser would have to live. Set defaults in the constructor, or mark the property required. This is the one place the new syntax is less convenient than the old field form, and it catches everyone once.

The source generator writes the backing field, the setter with its equality check, the OnPropertyChanged call, and the dependent notifications. Two optional partial hooks let you intercept. A partial method hook is one the generated setter calls if you implement it; if you do not, the call is compiled away.

C# 
partial void OnTitleChanging(string oldValue, string newValue)
{
    // runs before the field is assigned; throw here to veto
}

partial void OnTitleChanged(string value)
{
    _logger.LogDebug("Title is now {Title}", value);
    IsDirty = true;
}
SINCE C# 13 / TOOLKIT 8.4

The public partial string Title { get; set; } form replaces the old [ObservableProperty] private string title; field syntax. Partial properties mean the generated member is the one you declared, so XML docs, attributes, nullability and IntelliSense all work properly. The field form still compiles; new code should use partial properties.

Commands

A button needs something to run when clicked, and a way to know whether it is allowed right now. WPF's answer is ICommand (Part 6): Execute does the work, CanExecute says whether it may, and the CanExecuteChanged event tells the button to ask again. Writing a command class per action is tedious, so [RelayCommand] generates a command property from an ordinary method.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A command is a job card with a status light: what to do, and whether it can be done now. A button bound to it greys itself out while the light is off. The button only looks at the light again when told it may have changed. WPF's routed commands are re-asked after almost any keyboard or mouse activity; the toolkit's commands are asked again only when you say so.

[NotifyCanExecuteChangedFor] is how you say so: whenever Draft or Selected changes, the matching command raises CanExecuteChanged and its button checks again. The listing shows the three shapes you will write most: synchronous with a guard, async with cancellation, and async with a parameter. Watch how each method name becomes a generated property. This TaskListViewModel and its TaskViewModel are separate, simpler versions of the ones in Part 12.

C# 
using CommunityToolkit.Mvvm.Input;

public partial class TaskListViewModel : ObservableObject
{
    private readonly ITaskRepository _repo;

    public TaskListViewModel(ITaskRepository repo) => _repo = repo;

    public ObservableCollection<TaskViewModel> Items { get; } = [];

    [ObservableProperty]
    [NotifyCanExecuteChangedFor(nameof(AddCommand))]
    public partial string Draft { get; set; }

    [ObservableProperty]
    [NotifyCanExecuteChangedFor(nameof(DeleteCommand))]
    public partial TaskViewModel? Selected { get; set; }

    // Synchronous, with a CanExecute guard
    [RelayCommand(CanExecute = nameof(CanAdd))]
    private void Add()
    {
        Items.Add(new TaskViewModel { Title = Draft.Trim() });
        Draft = "";
    }

    private bool CanAdd() => !string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(Draft);

    // Async, cancellable, with automatic re-entrancy blocking
    [RelayCommand(IncludeCancelCommand = true, AllowConcurrentExecutions = false)]
    private async Task RefreshAsync(CancellationToken ct)
    {
        var rows = await _repo.GetAllAsync(ct);
        Items.Clear();
        foreach (var r in rows) Items.Add(new TaskViewModel { Id = r.Id, Title = r.Title, IsDone = r.IsDone });
    }

    // Parameterised
    [RelayCommand(CanExecute = nameof(CanDelete))]
    private async Task DeleteAsync(TaskViewModel item, CancellationToken ct)
    {
        await _repo.DeleteAsync(item.Id, ct);
        Items.Remove(item);
    }

    private bool CanDelete(TaskViewModel? item) => item is not null;
}

The generator produces AddCommand, RefreshCommand, RefreshCancelCommand and DeleteCommand: the method name without its Async suffix, plus Command. IncludeCancelCommand adds RefreshCancelCommand, which cancels the token passed to RefreshAsync. The async ones are IAsyncRelayCommand, which exposes IsRunning: bind a progress indicator straight to {Binding RefreshCommand.IsRunning} and delete your IsBusy property.

XAML 
<Button Content="Refresh" Command="{Binding RefreshCommand}" />
<Button Content="Cancel"  Command="{Binding RefreshCancelCommand}" />
<ProgressBar IsIndeterminate="True" Height="2"
             Visibility="{Binding RefreshCommand.IsRunning,
                          Converter={StaticResource BoolToVis}}" />

The converter is the BoolToVisibilityConverter from Part 11. The figure shows the two states you would see.

GOTCHA

AllowConcurrentExecutions = false is the default and it is what you want: it stops a user double-clicking Refresh into two overlapping requests. But it also means CanExecute returns false while running, so the button greys out. If the button shouldn't grey out, either allow concurrency and guard yourself, or bind IsEnabled separately.

Messaging between view models DEEPER

Sometimes two view models that do not know each other need to talk: a settings dialog telling the shell the theme changed, a save completing and a list needing a nudge. Handing each a reference to the other ties them together. A messenger gives you loose coupling instead: one side sends a message object, and any side registered for that message type receives it, with neither holding a reference to the other.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A messenger is a building's public address system. The sender announces "task 42 saved" without knowing who is listening, and only those who asked to hear that kind of announcement react. The same property is the cost: reading the code that reacts, you cannot see who made the announcement, or when. That is why it should stay an occasional tool, not the default way view models talk.

C# 
using CommunityToolkit.Mvvm.Input;
using CommunityToolkit.Mvvm.Messaging;

public sealed record TaskSaved(int Id);

// Publisher
WeakReferenceMessenger.Default.Send(new TaskSaved(task.Id));

// Subscriber: ObservableRecipient auto-registers/unregisters with IsActive
public partial class SidebarViewModel : ObservableRecipient, IRecipient<TaskSaved>
{
    public void Receive(TaskSaved message) => RefreshCommand.Execute(null);

    [RelayCommand]
    private void Refresh() { /* reload the sidebar's counts */ }
}

ObservableRecipient registers every IRecipient<T> handler when you set its IsActive to true, and unregisters them when you set it back to false. WeakReferenceMessenger holds weak references (references that do not keep the recipient alive), so a forgotten unregister leaks nothing. StrongReferenceMessenger is faster and will leak if you forget. Default to the weak one.

There is also a request/response shape, useful for "does anyone want to veto this close?":

C# 
public sealed class HasUnsavedChangesRequest : CollectionRequestMessage<bool> { }

// Ask:
bool dirty = WeakReferenceMessenger.Default.Send<HasUnsavedChangesRequest>().Any(r => r);

// Answer:
public void Receive(HasUnsavedChangesRequest m) => m.Reply(IsDirty);

The request derives from CollectionRequestMessage<T> (in CommunityToolkit.Mvvm.Messaging.Messages) because several open editors may answer: it collects every reply, and Any asks whether any of them has unsaved changes. A plain RequestMessage<T> accepts exactly one reply and throws on a second.

A view model base worth having DEEPER

Most pages in an app need the same few things: a title, a moment to load their data when shown, and a chance to object before the user navigates away. A small base class gives every page those hooks in the same shape.

C# 
public abstract partial class PageViewModel : ObservableObject
{
    [ObservableProperty] public partial string Title { get; set; }
    [ObservableProperty] public partial bool IsLoaded { get; set; }

    protected PageViewModel(string title) => Title = title;

    /// <summary>Called once when the page becomes visible.</summary>
    public virtual Task LoadAsync(CancellationToken ct) => Task.CompletedTask;

    /// <summary>Return false to veto navigation away.</summary>
    public virtual Task<bool> CanLeaveAsync() => Task.FromResult(true);
}

Resist the urge to grow this into a framework. A base class with Title, a load hook and a leave hook covers almost everything; anything more is usually a service in disguise.

Design-time data DEEPER

The Visual Studio XAML designer draws your view without running the app, so no view model exists and every bound value is blank. Design-time data gives the designer a view model of its own. It is worth five minutes of setup, because it turns the XAML designer from useless into useful. First, a view model subclass filled with sample content:

C# 
public sealed class DesignTaskListViewModel : TaskListViewModel
{
    public DesignTaskListViewModel() : base(new FakeRepository())
    {
        Items.Add(new TaskViewModel { Title = "Read the value precedence list" });
        Items.Add(new TaskViewModel { Title = "Freeze the theme brushes", IsDone = true });
        Selected = Items[0];
    }
}

Then point the view at it. Attributes with the d: prefix are for the designer only: mc:Ignorable="d" tells the compiler and the runtime XAML reader to skip them; only the designer reads them, so the real DataContext is untouched.

XAML 
<UserControl ...
  xmlns:d="http://schemas.microsoft.com/expression/blend/2008"
  xmlns:mc="http://schemas.openxmlformats.org/markup-compatibility/2006"
  mc:Ignorable="d"
  d:DataContext="{d:DesignInstance design:DesignTaskListViewModel, IsDesignTimeCreatable=True}">

IsDesignTimeCreatable=True actually constructs the object, so you see real content. Without it you get type information only: still enough for binding IntelliSense, which is most of the value.

RECAP
  • A view model is a plain C# class holding a screen's state and actions; the XAML only draws it and forwards input through bindings and commands.
  • [ObservableProperty] on a partial property generates the change notification you used to write by hand.
  • A partial property cannot have an initialiser, so set defaults in the constructor.
  • [RelayCommand] turns a method into a command, and [NotifyCanExecuteChangedFor] tells its button when to check again.
  • Async commands expose IsRunning and, by default, refuse to start again until the current run finishes.
  • Use the messenger for occasional announcements between unrelated view models, not as the main way they talk.
PART 15

Dependency injection and the host

WPF predates Microsoft.Extensions.* by a decade, so nothing is wired up for you. Fifty lines gets you the same composition root you'd have in an ASP.NET Core app.

Builds on Part 2, project setup and startup and Part 14, MVVM with the Community Toolkit.

A task list view model needs a repository; the repository needs a connection string from configuration; the API client needs a base address and a logger. If each class creates what it needs with new, the app is wired together in a hundred places, changing one constructor ripples through all of them, and a test cannot swap the real database for a fake.

Dependency injection (DI) turns that around. A class lists what it needs as constructor parameters and never creates those things itself. A container builds objects on request, filling in the parameters from a list of registrations that say which concrete class to use for each type. The one place where you write all the registrations is the composition root.

IN PLAIN TERMS

The container is a restaurant kitchen and the registrations are its recipes. Order a TaskListViewModel and the kitchen sees that it needs a repository, which needs a connection, and assembles the whole plate. The composition root places the order; dishes never order each other. Because a dish takes its ingredients rather than fetching them, a test can hand it fake ones.
