The generic host (IHost, from Microsoft.Extensions.Hosting) wraps that container together with configuration, logging and background services, and gives them all a start and a stop. ASP.NET Core is built on it, and nothing stops a WPF app from using it too.

The wiring

The whole composition root lives in App.xaml.cs. Read it in three steps. The constructor reads configuration, sets up logging, registers every service and builds the host. OnStartup starts the host and asks it for the main window. StartAsync runs through Task.Run so no background service starts on the UI thread (see Background work below). OnExit stops the host, so background work and log files shut down cleanly. Besides Microsoft.Extensions.Hosting, the listing uses the Microsoft.Extensions.Http, Serilog.Extensions.Hosting, Serilog.Settings.Configuration and Serilog.Sinks.File packages.

App.xaml.cs 
public partial class App : Application
{
    private readonly IHost _host;

    public App()
    {
        var builder = Host.CreateApplicationBuilder(new HostApplicationBuilderSettings
        {
            ContentRootPath = AppContext.BaseDirectory
        });

        builder.Configuration
            .AddJsonFile("appsettings.json", optional: false)
            .AddJsonFile($"appsettings.{builder.Environment.EnvironmentName}.json", optional: true)
            .AddEnvironmentVariables("TASKS_")
            .AddUserSecrets<App>(optional: true);

        builder.Logging.ClearProviders();
        builder.Services.AddSerilog((_, cfg) => cfg
            .ReadFrom.Configuration(builder.Configuration)
            .WriteTo.File(
                Path.Combine(Environment.GetFolderPath(Environment.SpecialFolder.LocalApplicationData),
                             "Tasks", "logs", "log-.txt"),
                rollingInterval: RollingInterval.Day));

        builder.Services.Configure<ApiOptions>(builder.Configuration.GetSection("Api"));

        // Infrastructure
        builder.Services.AddSingleton<IClock, SystemClock>();
        builder.Services.AddSingleton<ITaskRepository, SqliteTaskRepository>();
        builder.Services.AddHttpClient<ITaskApi, TaskApi>((sp, http) =>
        {
            var o = sp.GetRequiredService<IOptions<ApiOptions>>().Value;
            http.BaseAddress = new Uri(o.BaseUrl);
        });

        // UI services
        builder.Services.AddSingleton<IDialogService, DialogService>();
        builder.Services.AddSingleton<INavigationService, NavigationService>();

        // Windows are singletons, page/dialog view models are transient
        builder.Services.AddSingleton<MainWindow>();
        builder.Services.AddSingleton<ShellViewModel>();
        builder.Services.AddTransient<TaskListViewModel>();
        builder.Services.AddTransient<TaskEditorViewModel>();

        _host = builder.Build();
    }

    protected override async void OnStartup(StartupEventArgs e)
    {
        try
        {
            base.OnStartup(e);
            await Task.Run(() => _host.StartAsync());

            var window = _host.Services.GetRequiredService<MainWindow>();
            window.DataContext = _host.Services.GetRequiredService<ShellViewModel>();
            window.Show();
        }
        catch (Exception ex)
        {
            MessageBox.Show(ex.ToString(), "Startup failed");
            Shutdown(1);
        }
    }

    protected override void OnExit(ExitEventArgs e)
    {
        using (_host)
        {
            Task.Run(() => _host.StopAsync(TimeSpan.FromSeconds(5))).GetAwaiter().GetResult();
        }
        base.OnExit(e);
    }
}

Setting ContentRootPath to the executable's folder matters because appsettings.json is looked up relative to the content root, which otherwise is the working folder: launched from a shortcut with a different "Start in" folder, the app would not find its settings.

WPF does not wait for OnExit to finish, so an async version could let the process end halfway through shutdown. The stop has to block, and running it through Task.Run keeps its awaits from needing the UI thread while that thread is blocked.

Nothing is created while you register. AddSingleton, AddTransient and AddHttpClient only record what to build. GetRequiredService is the moment of building: it constructs MainWindow and everything its constructor asks for, or throws if something is not registered. The try/catch matters because OnStartup is async void: an exception escaping it would surface as an unhandled dispatcher exception rather than a readable message.

Delete StartupUri (the attribute that tells WPF which window to open at launch) from App.xaml, or the framework will construct a second, unwired window behind the one you showed.

Lifetimes, translated to desktop

Every registration also says how long an instance lives and who shares it: its lifetime. The names come from web servers, where a scope is one HTTP request. A desktop app has no requests, so each lifetime needs translating.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A singleton is the office printer: one for everybody, for as long as the building is open. A transient is a paper cup: a fresh one every time anyone asks. A scoped service is the pen handed out with a visitor badge: everyone working on that visit shares it, and it is collected when the visit ends. In a desktop app you decide what counts as a visit, typically one dialog.

LifetimeDesktop meaning
SingletonLives as long as the process. Repositories, the shell view model, settings, the main window.
TransientNew instance per resolve. Dialog view models, page view models, anything holding per-screen state.
ScopedNo ambient scope exists in a desktop app. Create one explicitly per window or per unit of work; this is exactly how you get a per-dialog DbContext.

If you resolve a scoped service outside any scope, straight from _host.Services, it silently lives as long as the app, like a singleton. The host only catches that mistake when its environment is Development (set by the DOTNET_ENVIRONMENT variable; the default is Production), where it turns on scope validation and throws instead.

The method below opens an editor dialog with its own scope. Everything the editor's view model pulls in as scoped, such as a DbContext, is created fresh for this dialog and disposed when the method returns. Because ShowDialog blocks until the dialog closes, that is right after the user closes it.

C# 
// A scope per editor window, disposed when the window closes.
public async Task<bool> EditAsync(int taskId)
{
    using var scope = _services.CreateScope();
    var vm = scope.ServiceProvider.GetRequiredService<TaskEditorViewModel>();
    await vm.LoadAsync(taskId);

    var window = new TaskEditorWindow { DataContext = vm, Owner = _shell };
    return window.ShowDialog() == true;
}
GOTCHA

Never inject IServiceProvider into a view model so it can resolve things later. That is the service locator anti-pattern, and it hides your dependency graph: the constructor no longer shows what the class needs, so a missing registration only surfaces when some later code path runs. Inject a typed factory instead. Microsoft.Extensions.DependencyInjection does not supply a Func<TaskEditorViewModel> by itself, so either register one or define a small ITaskEditorFactory. The one place a container reference is legitimate is your navigation service, which is by definition a locator.

Background work with the host DEEPER

Because you started a real host, IHostedService works. A hosted service is a class the host starts when it starts and stops when it stops. This is a clean home for sync loops, file watchers and cache warmers. BackgroundService is the convenient base class: you override ExecuteAsync with a long-running loop, and the host cancels its token at shutdown.

This one pulls remote changes every five minutes, merges them into the local store and announces the result with the messenger from Part 14. The when filter logs and retries every failure, including an HTTP timeout, which surfaces as a cancellation exception too. Only a cancellation caused by the stopping token, at shutdown, ends the loop.

C# 
public sealed class SyncService : BackgroundService
{
    private readonly ITaskApi _api;
    private readonly ITaskRepository _repo;
    private readonly ILogger<SyncService> _log;

    public SyncService(ITaskApi api, ITaskRepository repo, ILogger<SyncService> log)
        => (_api, _repo, _log) = (api, repo, log);

    protected override async Task ExecuteAsync(CancellationToken stoppingToken)
    {
        using var timer = new PeriodicTimer(TimeSpan.FromMinutes(5));
        while (await timer.WaitForNextTickAsync(stoppingToken))
        {
            try
            {
                var remote = await _api.GetChangesAsync(stoppingToken);
                await _repo.MergeAsync(remote, stoppingToken);
                WeakReferenceMessenger.Default.Send(new SyncCompleted(remote.Count));
            }
            catch (Exception ex) when (!stoppingToken.IsCancellationRequested)
            {
                _log.LogWarning(ex, "Sync failed; will retry");
            }
        }
    }
}

Register with builder.Services.AddHostedService<SyncService>(). It starts with _host.StartAsync() and stops cleanly on exit.

Do not assume which thread it runs on. On .NET 10 the host runs all of ExecuteAsync on a thread-pool thread. On .NET 9 the part before its first await runs on whichever thread called StartAsync, which is why OnStartup calls it through Task.Run: otherwise that part, and every later await, would run on the UI thread, and shutdown would wait out its full timeout. Either way, anything touching bound collections needs the marshalling from Part 12. That includes the message it sends: messenger recipients run on the sending thread, so a message from a background service arrives off the UI thread.

RECAP
  • Classes ask for what they need in their constructor, and the container builds them from registrations kept in one place, App.xaml.cs.
  • The generic host adds configuration, logging and background services around the container, as in ASP.NET Core.
  • Remove StartupUri and show the main window yourself, resolved from the host.
  • A singleton lives for the whole app, a transient is new every time, and a scoped service needs a scope you create, typically one per dialog.
  • Inject specific services or a factory, never IServiceProvider itself.
  • A background service's thread is not guaranteed, so marshal any change to a bound collection.
PART 16

Navigation, windows, dialogs

Most desktop apps show one screen at a time in a main area, open a separate window now and then, and occasionally stop to ask the user a question. This part covers all three without tying your view models to the screen.

Builds on Part 10, templates (implicit data templates), Part 14, MVVM with the Community Toolkit (the PageViewModel base class) and Part 15, dependency injection.

Forget Frame and Page

Picture an app with a sidebar: Tasks, Projects, Settings. Click one and the main area shows that screen. You need a way to swap what fills the main area, and WPF appears to offer one out of the box.

WPF ships a browser-style navigation stack: NavigationWindow, Frame, Page and NavigationService. It dates from the XBAP era, when WPF applications could run inside a web browser, something .NET Core and later never supported. It keeps a journal, a browser-like back and forward history of every page shown, which you rarely want. It also fights MVVM: the frame keeps its own idea of what is on screen, and your view model ends up chasing it. Skip it.

The pattern that works is the one from Part 10: a ContentControl, a bound view model property, and implicit DataTemplates. An implicit DataTemplate is a template declared with a DataType and no key, which WPF applies automatically to any object of that type.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Think of the main area as a stage. The view model decides which scene is on by setting one property. The implicit data templates are the stage crew: each knows which set to build for which scene, so nobody has to name a view. Where the picture breaks: when the scene changes, the old set is thrown away and rebuilt from scratch next time, so anything that lives only in the view (scroll position, a half-typed box not bound to anything) is lost. State you want to keep belongs in the view model.

The shell below puts the list of pages down the left and a ContentControl in the rest. Notice that no view is named anywhere: the list selects a view model, the ContentControl receives it, and the matching template picks the view.

Shell.xaml 
<DockPanel>
  <ListBox DockPanel.Dock="Left" Width="200"
           ItemsSource="{Binding Pages}"
           SelectedItem="{Binding Current}"
           DisplayMemberPath="Title" />

  <ContentControl Content="{Binding Current}" Margin="12" />
</DockPanel>

The shell view model holds the list and the current page. Each page derives from the PageViewModel base class from Part 14, which provides Title, IsLoaded, a load hook and a leave hook. The first time a page becomes current, it loads its data.

C# 
public partial class ShellViewModel : ObservableObject
{
    public ObservableCollection<PageViewModel> Pages { get; }

    [ObservableProperty]
    public partial PageViewModel? Current { get; set; }

    // The generated hook returns void, so delegate rather than making it async void.
    partial void OnCurrentChanged(PageViewModel? value)
    {
        if (value is { IsLoaded: false }) _ = LoadPageAsync(value);
    }

    private async Task LoadPageAsync(PageViewModel page)
    {
        try
        {
            await page.LoadAsync(CancellationToken.None);
            page.IsLoaded = true;
        }
        catch (Exception ex)
        {
            _log.LogError(ex, "Failed to load {Page}", page.Title);
        }
    }
}

The detail to notice is the hand-off in OnCurrentChanged. The toolkit generates that hook as a method returning void, so it cannot be awaited. Rather than make it async void, it starts a Task-returning method that catches and logs its own errors (Part 17 explains why that matters). When it runs, the first click on a page loads it; clicking back to it later does not load it again, because IsLoaded is already true.

A navigation service, when you need one DEEPER

The sidebar pattern is enough while the user is the only one choosing pages. It gets crowded once pages move to each other: a task list that opens a task's detail page with a Back button, or an edit page that must say "you have unsaved changes, stay here". A navigation service puts that logic in one place that any view model can ask for through dependency injection.

The interface has four members, and the implementation keeps a stack of the pages you came from. Three things to notice in NavigateToAsync. It asks the current page's CanLeaveAsync first, so a page can veto leaving. It resolves the next page from the container, so the page gets its own dependencies injected; register each page view model, usually as transient (Part 15). And the configure callback lets the caller pass parameters, such as which task to show, before the page loads.

C# 
public interface INavigationService
{
    PageViewModel? Current { get; }
    event EventHandler? CurrentChanged;
    Task<bool> NavigateToAsync<TViewModel>(Action<TViewModel>? configure = null)
        where TViewModel : PageViewModel;
    Task<bool> GoBackAsync();
}

public sealed class NavigationService(IServiceProvider services) : INavigationService
{
    private readonly Stack<PageViewModel> _history = new();

    public PageViewModel? Current { get; private set; }
    public event EventHandler? CurrentChanged;

    public async Task<bool> NavigateToAsync<TViewModel>(Action<TViewModel>? configure = null)
        where TViewModel : PageViewModel
    {
        if (Current is not null && !await Current.CanLeaveAsync()) return false;

        var next = services.GetRequiredService<TViewModel>();
        configure?.Invoke(next);
        await next.LoadAsync(CancellationToken.None);

        if (Current is not null) _history.Push(Current);
        Current = next;
        CurrentChanged?.Invoke(this, EventArgs.Empty);
        return true;
    }

    public async Task<bool> GoBackAsync()
    {
        if (_history.Count == 0) return false;
        if (Current is not null && !await Current.CanLeaveAsync()) return false;
        Current = _history.Pop();
        CurrentChanged?.Invoke(this, EventArgs.Empty);
        return true;
    }
}

If LoadAsync throws, the exception reaches the caller and the current page stays where it was, because the history and Current change only after loading succeeds. GoBackAsync discards the page you are leaving, so this service has Back but no Forward. A shell driven by this service keeps no Current or load hook of its own: it exposes the service's Current read-only, raises a property change when CurrentChanged fires, and navigates through commands that call the service. The sidebar list calls such a command when clicked, and binds SelectedItem with Mode=OneWay so it still highlights the current page, because a two-way binding to a read-only property throws. The same ContentControl shows the result. Keeping the earlier load hook as well would load every page twice.

Dialogs without breaking testability

A view model must never call MessageBox.Show or new SaveFileDialog() itself. It becomes untestable, because a unit test that reaches that line has nobody to click the button. It also drags System.Windows into your logic layer. Put it behind an interface.

IN PLAIN TERMS

The view model should not talk to the user directly; it should ask a question and get an answer back. The interface is the list of questions it may ask: "confirm this", "pick a file", "show this dialog". In the running app, a WPF class answers by showing real windows. In a test, a stand-in answers from a script, such as "the user clicked Yes", and the view model cannot tell the difference.

Below are the interface and a WPF implementation. The Active property finds the window the user is currently in, so every dialog gets the right owner. ConfirmAsync returns a Task even though MessageBox.Show is synchronous, so you can later replace it with an in-window overlay dialog without changing a single caller. The snippet leaves out AlertAsync and PickSaveFile, which follow the same shape as their neighbours.

C# 
public interface IDialogService
{
    Task<bool> ConfirmAsync(string title, string message);
    Task AlertAsync(string title, string message);
    string? PickOpenFile(string filter, string? initialDirectory = null);
    string? PickSaveFile(string filter, string suggestedName);
    Task<TResult?> ShowAsync<TViewModel, TResult>(TViewModel viewModel)
        where TViewModel : DialogViewModel<TResult>;
}

public sealed class DialogService(IServiceProvider services) : IDialogService
{
    private Window Active =>
        Application.Current.Windows.OfType<Window>().FirstOrDefault(w => w.IsActive)
        ?? Application.Current.MainWindow;

    public Task<bool> ConfirmAsync(string title, string message) =>
        Task.FromResult(MessageBox.Show(Active, message, title,
            MessageBoxButton.YesNo, MessageBoxImage.Question) == MessageBoxResult.Yes);

    public string? PickOpenFile(string filter, string? initialDirectory = null)
    {
        var dlg = new OpenFileDialog { Filter = filter, InitialDirectory = initialDirectory ?? "" };
        return dlg.ShowDialog(Active) == true ? dlg.FileName : null;
    }

    public async Task<TResult?> ShowAsync<TViewModel, TResult>(TViewModel vm)
        where TViewModel : DialogViewModel<TResult>
    {
        // Resolve the window by convention or by an explicit registration
        var window = services.GetRequiredService<DialogWindow>();
        window.Owner = Active;
        window.DataContext = vm;

        var tcs = new TaskCompletionSource<TResult?>();
        vm.RequestClose += result => { window.DialogResult = true; tcs.TrySetResult(result); };
        window.Closed += (_, _) => tcs.TrySetResult(default);

        window.ShowDialog();
        return await tcs.Task;
    }
}

ShowAsync resolves a DialogWindow from the container, sets its owner and data context, and completes in one of two ways: the view model raises RequestClose with a result, or the user closes the window with the title-bar button and the result is default.

ShowDialog() shows a modal window, one that blocks input to the rest of the app until it closes. Your code stops at that line, while WPF keeps the application painting and responding through a nested message loop. So by the time await tcs.Task runs, the task has already completed. Setting DialogResult closes the window, which then raises Closed; the second TrySetResult there does nothing, because the result is already set.

ShowAsync expects each dialog's view model to derive from a base class that can ask to be closed with a result. That class has not appeared yet; this is enough:

C# 
public abstract class DialogViewModel<TResult> : ObservableObject
{
    public event Action<TResult?>? RequestClose;

    protected void Close(TResult? result) => RequestClose?.Invoke(result);
}

A derived dialog calls Close(result) from its OK command. In tests you substitute a fake IDialogService that returns scripted answers, and your view model logic is exercisable without a UI thread.

Window details that bite

  • Always set Owner on a dialog. The owner is the window a dialog belongs to: it stays on top of its owner and minimises with it. Without one, the dialog can end up behind the main window and will not centre correctly. WindowStartupLocation="CenterOwner" needs an owner to mean anything.
  • DialogResult only works on windows shown with ShowDialog(). Setting it on a modeless window, one opened with Show() that leaves the rest of the app usable, throws.
  • Cancelling a close happens in Closing, by setting e.Cancel = true. You cannot usefully await inside that handler. If you need an async confirmation, cancel unconditionally, run the async check, then close again with a flag set.
IN PLAIN TERMS

Closing asks a yes-or-no question and reads your answer the moment your handler returns. An await returns control to WPF at that point, so WPF acts on whatever e.Cancel says right then and never sees the answer you arrive at later. The workaround says "no" straight away, goes to find out, and if the answer turns out to be yes, starts a fresh close that the flag waves through.

Here is that pattern as a window override. The first pass always cancels; once the view model agrees, the flag is set and a second close is queued. Calling Close() directly would throw whenever CanCloseAsync finishes without waiting, because WPF forbids starting a close while one is still running.

C# 
private bool _closeConfirmed;

protected override async void OnClosing(CancelEventArgs e)
{
    if (_closeConfirmed) { base.OnClosing(e); return; }

    e.Cancel = true;                       // stop this close
    var vm = (ShellViewModel)DataContext;
    if (!await vm.CanCloseAsync()) return;

    _closeConfirmed = true;
    Dispatcher.InvokeAsync(Close);         // a new close, once this one has finished
}

This is one of the places async void is acceptable, because it is a framework override. If CanCloseAsync can throw, catch inside the method, for the reason given in Part 17.

RECAP
  • Skip Frame and Page; swap screens with a ContentControl bound to the current view model.
  • Implicit data templates choose the view, and the view is rebuilt each time, so keep state in the view model.
  • Reach for a navigation service when pages open other pages or need to veto leaving.
  • View models ask for dialogs through an interface, so tests can answer with a fake.
  • Always give a dialog an owner, and use DialogResult only with ShowDialog().
  • To confirm a close asynchronously, cancel first, then close again once the answer is yes.
PART 17

Threading and async

Every WPF element, brush and animation belongs to the thread that created it, and touching it from any other thread throws, unless it is frozen.

Builds on Part 14, MVVM with the Community Toolkit ([RelayCommand] and [ObservableProperty]) and Part 8, resources (freezing brushes).

Without a model of threads, the first WPF bug you meet is usually one of two. Either the window turns white and says "Not Responding" while a file loads, or you move the load to a background thread and get InvalidOperationException: "The calling thread cannot access this object because a different thread owns it." This part explains both and the small set of rules that avoid them.

Precisely: every DispatcherObject, the base class of every element, every brush and every animation, remembers the thread that created it and throws if touched from another. That is thread affinity. Single-threaded apartment (STA) is the COM threading model the UI thread must use, which is why WPF's startup code marks the main thread with [STAThread].

IN PLAIN TERMS

The UI thread is a single cashier with one queue. Only the cashier may touch the till, which here means every element on screen (frozen objects, later in this part, are the exception). Other threads can do heavy work in the back room, but to change what the customer sees they must join the queue and hand the cashier a note. If the cashier is busy with one long job, the whole queue stops, and that is the "Not Responding" window.

The Dispatcher

One UI thread runs a message loop: it repeatedly takes the next piece of work (a click, a repaint, your code) and runs it. The Dispatcher is that loop's work queue, and it orders work by DispatcherPriority rather than strictly by arrival.

These are the ways to put work on that queue. Read the comments: they differ in whether you wait, and whether you can get a value back.

C# 
// Are we on the UI thread?
bool onUi = Dispatcher.CheckAccess();

// Post work to it (fire and forget, returns immediately).
// BeginInvoke takes a Delegate, so give the lambda an explicit type.
Dispatcher.BeginInvoke(new Action(() => status.Text = "Done"));
Dispatcher.InvokeAsync(() => status.Text = "Done");   // overload takes Action directly

// Post and await the result
var width = await Dispatcher.InvokeAsync(() => element.ActualWidth);

// Post and block until done: deadlock risk, avoid
Dispatcher.Invoke(() => status.Text = "Done");

// Run after layout has completed
Dispatcher.InvokeAsync(() => box.Focus(), DispatcherPriority.Loaded);

Priority matters more than people expect. DispatcherPriority.Background work runs only after input, rendering and normal work have been handled, which is how you keep a busy UI responsive. DispatcherPriority.Loaded is the hook for "after layout has run": work queued there runs once pending layout is done, which makes it the reliable way to focus a control that does not exist yet. Render is the priority layout itself runs at, so work queued at Render is not guaranteed to see the finished layout.

The rule you actually follow

With async/await, almost none of the above is needed day to day. await captures the current SynchronizationContext, an object that knows how to run code "back where you started". WPF installs a DispatcherSynchronizationContext on the UI thread, so the code after each await comes back to the UI thread automatically.

IN PLAIN TERMS

An await is like the cashier sending a slow order off to be prepared and keeping a ticket for it. Instead of standing and waiting, the cashier goes back to serving the queue. When the order is ready, the ticket (the rest of your method) joins the cashier's queue again, and the cashier finishes the order, not whoever prepared it. That is why the lines after await can touch the UI safely.

A typical command looks like this. The comments mark which thread each part runs on; notice that nothing in it mentions the dispatcher.

C# 
[RelayCommand]
private async Task LoadAsync(CancellationToken ct)
{
    // UI thread here.
    Status = "Loading…";

    var rows = await _repo.GetAllAsync(ct);          // UI thread is free while this waits

    // UI thread again: safe to touch bound collections.
    Items.Clear();
    foreach (var r in rows) Items.Add(new TaskViewModel(r));
    Status = $"{Items.Count} tasks";
}

While the repository call is in flight, the window stays responsive: the UI thread is free, not waiting. The three rules that keep this working:

  • Don't use ConfigureAwait(false) in view models and UI code. It tells the await not to come back to the captured context, and you want the context back. Do use it in library and service code that never touches UI.
  • Don't call .Result or .Wait() on the UI thread. The continuation needs the UI thread, which you are blocking while you wait for it. That is a textbook deadlock, two pieces of work each waiting for the other forever, and it is still the most common hang in WPF applications.
  • Do use Task.Run for genuinely CPU-bound work (work that keeps a processor busy, such as calculation), and nothing else. I/O-bound work (waiting on a disk, database or network) already frees the thread while it waits, so wrapping an async I/O call in Task.Run just burns a thread.
C# 
// CPU-bound: correct use of Task.Run
var report = await Task.Run(() => _engine.Recalculate(model), ct);

// I/O-bound: wrong
var rows = await Task.Run(() => _repo.GetAllAsync(ct));   // pointless thread hop
var rows = await _repo.GetAllAsync(ct);                   // correct

Progress reporting

Long work on a background thread usually needs to move a progress bar. Background code must not touch elements or bound collections. WPF forwards a plain property change from another thread, but not a collection change, unless you call BindingOperations.EnableCollectionSynchronization. Progress<T> gives the background code one safe channel: it remembers the context it was created on and posts every report back there.

C# 
[ObservableProperty] public partial double Progress { get; set; }

[RelayCommand]
private async Task ImportAsync(CancellationToken ct)
{
    // Progress<T> captures the current SynchronizationContext at construction,
    // so the callback runs on the UI thread automatically.
    var progress = new Progress<double>(p => Progress = p);

    await Task.Run(() => _importer.Run(_path, progress, ct), ct);
}

The importer only sees an IProgress<double> and calls Report; it knows nothing about WPF. Create the Progress<T> on the UI thread, as here; created on a background thread, its callbacks would run on the thread pool instead.

Freezables and cross-thread objects

Thread affinity has a cost: if a brush or a shape belongs to the UI thread, you cannot build a large one in the background. Freezing is the way out.

A Freezable is the kind of object brushes, geometries, transforms and animations are built on, and it can be made immutable with Freeze(). A frozen freezable loses thread affinity, so it can be created on a background thread and used on the UI thread. That is how you pre-build complex geometry off the UI thread:

C# 
var geometry = await Task.Run(() =>
{
    var g = BuildComplexPath(points);   // StreamGeometry, thousands of segments
    g.Freeze();                          // now thread-agnostic and faster to render
    return g;
});

path.Data = geometry;                    // UI thread, no marshalling needed

Freezing is one-way: to change a frozen object, clone it with Clone(), which gives you an unfrozen copy. A freezable with a binding or a running animation on it cannot be frozen; CanFreeze tells you before you try.

A second UI thread DEEPER

Rarely needed, occasionally the right answer: a splash screen that must animate while the main thread blocks on startup, or a diagnostics window that must stay responsive when the main UI hangs.

The code creates a thread, marks it STA, shows a window on it, and starts that thread's own dispatcher loop with Dispatcher.Run(). IsBackground = true means this thread will not keep the process alive after the main window closes. The main thread waits on ready until the window exists, so it has the splash thread's dispatcher in hand. splashDispatcher is a local here; in a real app, keep it in a field so other code can close the splash later.

C# 
Dispatcher? splashDispatcher = null;
using var ready = new ManualResetEventSlim();
var thread = new Thread(() =>
{
    var window = new SplashWindow();
    splashDispatcher = window.Dispatcher;
    window.Show();
    ready.Set();
    Dispatcher.Run();                      // this thread now has its own message loop
});
thread.SetApartmentState(ApartmentState.STA);
thread.IsBackground = true;
thread.Start();
ready.Wait();

// To close it later, from anywhere:
splashDispatcher!.InvokeShutdown();

The two windows cannot share element instances, and they cannot be parent and child in the normal sense. Treat them as separate applications that happen to share a process.

GOTCHA

async void is correct for event handlers and for framework overrides like OnStartup, and wrong everywhere else. Nobody can await an async void method, so nobody can catch its exceptions: an unhandled exception in one is rethrown on the synchronization context and takes the process down, unless a DispatcherUnhandledException handler marks it handled (Part 2). Every async void body should be wrapped in try/catch, or be a one-liner delegating to a Task-returning method that handles its own errors.

RECAP
  • Every WPF element, brush and animation belongs to the thread that created it, normally the UI thread.
  • The dispatcher is the UI thread's queue, and other threads hand work to it.
  • In view models, plain await brings you back to the UI thread; never block on .Result or .Wait().
  • Use Task.Run only for heavy computation, not to wrap calls that are already async.
  • Freeze brushes and geometry built on a background thread so the UI thread can use them.
  • Keep async void to event handlers and overrides, and catch errors inside it.
PART 18

Graphics and animation

WPF draws everything itself, from a button's border to a chart with fifty thousand points, and it can animate any of it. The skill is choosing how heavy each thing you draw should be, and making motion cheap.

Builds on Part 4, layout (measure and arrange), Part 5, dependency properties (value precedence), Part 8, resources and Part 9, styles and triggers.

Three levels of drawing

Draw a dot for each of 10,000 data points as an Ellipse and the app crawls: every dot is a full element taking part in layout, input and styling. WPF offers three levels of drawing, and picking the right one is most of graphics performance.

All three are retained mode: you describe what to draw once, WPF keeps that description and repaints from it whenever needed. You never write a paint handler that runs on every redraw.

LevelWhatCost
ShapeRectangle, Ellipse, Path, Line. Full FrameworkElements: layout, hit-testing, styling, binding.Highest. Fine for tens, painful for thousands.
DrawingGeometryDrawing, ImageDrawing inside a DrawingBrush or DrawingImage. No layout participation.Much cheaper. Right for icons.
VisualDrawingVisual + RenderOpen(), hosted in a FrameworkElement. Bare metal.Cheapest. Right for charts and canvases with thousands of primitives.
IN PLAIN TERMS

The difference is like putting ten thousand separate magnets on a fridge versus printing ten thousand dots on one sheet of paper. Each magnet can be picked up, moved and restyled on its own, and each one costs you. The printed sheet is far cheaper, but to know which dot someone pointed at, you have to work it out from the coordinates yourself.

Icons are the everyday case. Store the outline once as a Geometry, a resolution-independent description of a shape, and draw it with a Path wherever you need it. The data string is the path mini-language: M moves to a point, L draws a line to one, H and V draw horizontal and vertical lines to a coordinate, and Z closes the figure.

XAML: a Path icon, the sane way to do icons 
<ResourceDictionary>
  <Geometry x:Key="SaveGeometry">
    M3,3 H13 L17,7 V17 H3 Z M6,3 V8 H12 V3 M6,17 V12 H14 V17
  </Geometry>
</ResourceDictionary>

<Path Data="{StaticResource SaveGeometry}"
      Fill="{DynamicResource IconBrush}"
      Stretch="Uniform" Width="16" Height="16" />

Thousands of primitives with DrawingVisual DEEPER

For a scatter chart, draw every point into a single DrawingVisual. The host element must tell WPF about its visual children by overriding VisualChildrenCount and GetVisualChild; visuals it does not report are never drawn. RenderOpen() returns a DrawingContext you draw into, and the content is committed when that context is closed at the end of the using block.

C#: DrawingVisual for high element counts 
public sealed class ScatterSurface : FrameworkElement
{
    private readonly VisualCollection _visuals;

    public ScatterSurface() => _visuals = new VisualCollection(this);

    protected override int VisualChildrenCount => _visuals.Count;
    protected override Visual GetVisualChild(int index) => _visuals[index];

    public void Render(IReadOnlyList<Point> points, Brush brush)
    {
        _visuals.Clear();

        var visual = new DrawingVisual();
        using (DrawingContext dc = visual.RenderOpen())
        {
            // One visual, many primitives: far cheaper than one Ellipse each.
            foreach (var p in points)
                dc.DrawEllipse(brush, null, p, 2, 2);
        }
        _visuals.Add(visual);
    }

    protected override HitTestResult? HitTestCore(PointHitTestParameters p)
        => new PointHitTestResult(this, p.HitPoint);
}

However many points you pass, the tree gains one element and one visual. The HitTestCore override makes the whole surface count as hit, so it receives mouse events between the dots as well as on them. Working out which point was clicked is then your job, from the mouse position.

Brushes and transforms

A brush is an object that says how to paint an area: a flat colour, a gradient, an image, or even a live copy of another element. A transform moves, scales, rotates or skews an element without you changing its size or position properties. Both are set as properties, so they can be styled, bound and animated.

The snippet lists the brush types, then applies the same idea of a transform in the two places WPF accepts one. Watch the comments on the two transforms: the difference is whether layout knows about them.

XAML 
<!-- Brushes -->
<SolidColorBrush Color="#286983" />
<LinearGradientBrush StartPoint="0,0" EndPoint="1,1">…</LinearGradientBrush>
<RadialGradientBrush GradientOrigin="0.3,0.3" />
<ImageBrush ImageSource="/Assets/texture.png" TileMode="Tile"
            Viewport="0,0,64,64" ViewportUnits="Absolute" />
<VisualBrush Visual="{Binding ElementName=Source}" />   <!-- live mirror of an element -->

<!-- RenderTransform: after layout, does not affect siblings -->
<Image RenderTransformOrigin="0.5,0.5">
  <Image.RenderTransform>
    <TransformGroup>
      <ScaleTransform ScaleX="1.1" ScaleY="1.1" />
      <RotateTransform Angle="3" />
    </TransformGroup>
  </Image.RenderTransform>
</Image>

<!-- LayoutTransform: before layout, siblings move out of the way -->
<TextBlock Text="Axis label">
  <TextBlock.LayoutTransform><RotateTransform Angle="-90" /></TextBlock.LayoutTransform>
</TextBlock>
IN PLAIN TERMS

Layout is the seating plan for a dinner. A RenderTransform is a guest leaning back in their chair after everyone is seated: they may bump into a neighbour, but nobody moves and the plan is untouched. A LayoutTransform changes the size of the guest's chair before the plan is drawn up, so the whole table is reseated around it. Redrawing the plan is the expensive part, which is why only one of the two is safe to change on every frame.

RenderTransform is what you animate. It is cheap because changing it needs no layout: the new transform goes to the render thread, which recomposes the frame. LayoutTransform triggers a new layout pass, measuring and arranging the element and its neighbours again, and should never be animated.

Animation

A card that appears instantly looks abrupt; one that fades and slides into place tells the eye what just changed. WPF's animation system does this by animating dependency properties directly. Once per frame, the UI thread computes each animated value and applies it, and the render thread draws the result. Because the values are computed on the UI thread, a blocked UI thread freezes animations too, which is why the splash screen in Part 17 gets a thread of its own. The system is genuinely good and almost nobody uses it enough.

A storyboard groups animations so they start and stop together. An easing function changes the speed curve: CubicEase with EaseOut starts fast and slows into the end. The style below starts a storyboard when the card has loaded, fading opacity in and sliding the card up 8 pixels. The Setter for RenderTransform is there because the path RenderTransform.Y needs a TranslateTransform to exist before anything can animate its Y.

XAML 
<Style TargetType="Border" x:Key="Card">
  <Setter Property="RenderTransform">
    <Setter.Value><TranslateTransform /></Setter.Value>
  </Setter>

  <Style.Triggers>
    <EventTrigger RoutedEvent="Loaded">
      <BeginStoryboard>
        <Storyboard>
          <DoubleAnimation Storyboard.TargetProperty="Opacity"
                           From="0" To="1" Duration="0:0:0.18" />
          <DoubleAnimation Storyboard.TargetProperty="RenderTransform.Y"
                           From="8" To="0" Duration="0:0:0.22">
            <DoubleAnimation.EasingFunction>
              <CubicEase EasingMode="EaseOut" />
            </DoubleAnimation.EasingFunction>
          </DoubleAnimation>
        </Storyboard>
      </BeginStoryboard>
    </EventTrigger>
  </Style.Triggers>
</Style>

Animation types you'll use: DoubleAnimation, ColorAnimation, ThicknessAnimation, PointAnimation, plus the …UsingKeyFrames variants for multi-stop sequences, where key frames are a list of values to reach at set times. ObjectAnimationUsingKeyFrames handles discrete values like Visibility, which jump rather than blend.

From code, BeginAnimation starts an animation on one property. The helper below wraps it so you can await the end of the animation, then shows how to release the property afterwards.

C# 
// From code, with a completion await
static Task AnimateAsync(UIElement target, DependencyProperty prop, double to, TimeSpan duration)
{
    var tcs = new TaskCompletionSource();
    var anim = new DoubleAnimation(to, new Duration(duration))
    {
        EasingFunction = new QuadraticEase { EasingMode = EasingMode.EaseInOut },
        FillBehavior = FillBehavior.HoldEnd,
    };
    anim.Completed += (_, _) => tcs.TrySetResult();
    target.BeginAnimation(prop, anim);
    return tcs.Task;
}

// Release the animation's hold on the property afterwards:
element.BeginAnimation(UIElement.OpacityProperty, null);
element.Opacity = 1;
IN PLAIN TERMS

An animation does not overwrite a property's stored value. It sticks its own value on top, like a sticky note over a word on a page. Change the word underneath while the note is there and readers still see the note. Peel it off and the changed word shows.

GOTCHA

An animation with FillBehavior.HoldEnd (the default) keeps the property pinned at its final value until you remove the animation. It holds at precedence level 2, above any local value you set later (Part 5). So setting element.Opacity = 1 after a fade-out appears to do nothing. Clear the animation with BeginAnimation(prop, null) first, or use FillBehavior.Stop.

Respecting the user's motion setting

Some people turn animations off in Windows because motion distracts them or makes them feel unwell. And on a remote desktop session, animation is slow and choppy. Check both before you animate:

C# 
// Windows "Show animations" setting: honour it.
bool animate = SystemParameters.ClientAreaAnimation
            && RenderCapability.Tier > 0;

if (!animate) { element.Opacity = 1; return; }

RenderCapability.Tier tells you how much of WPF's rendering is hardware-accelerated. The tier is stored in the high word of the value, so read it as RenderCapability.Tier >> 16: 0 means software rendering (remote desktop, some VMs), 1 partial hardware acceleration, 2 full GPU. The > 0 test in the snippet works without the shift, because any hardware tier makes the raw value non-zero. Dropping animations at tier 0 makes a remote-desktop session feel dramatically better, which matters if your users are on Citrix or RDP.

RECAP
  • Use shapes for a handful of items, geometries and drawings for icons, and a DrawingVisual for thousands of points.
  • A RenderTransform changes how an element is drawn without moving its neighbours, so it is the one to animate.
  • A LayoutTransform makes layout run again, so never animate it.
  • Animations are computed on the UI thread, so a blocked UI thread stops them.
  • A finished animation keeps holding its value until you remove it, which hides values you set later.
  • Skip animations when Windows has them turned off or rendering is in software.
PART 19

Performance

WPF is fast until you accidentally turn off the thing making it fast. Most performance work is removing mistakes rather than adding optimisations.

Builds on Part 11, data binding, Part 12, collections and views, Part 17, threading and async (freezables) and Part 18, graphics and animation.

Virtualisation: the six ways to break it

Bind a list to 40,000 rows and, done right, it opens instantly and scrolls smoothly. Done wrong, the window takes seconds to appear and uses hundreds of megabytes, because WPF has built every row, including the 39,988 nobody can see.

Each row in a ListBox is wrapped in a container, a ListBoxItem holding that row's expanded template. Virtualisation means creating containers only for the rows in view, plus a small margin, and creating or reusing more as you scroll. A container that exists is said to be realised.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A virtualised list is like a station departure board with a dozen slots. Scrolling changes what the slots say; nobody builds a board for every train of the year. The trick depends on the board knowing how many slots fit. Give it unlimited height, as a StackPanel does, and it concludes every row is visible and builds them all.

A ListBox virtualises by default: only visible containers exist. Each of these silently disables it, or never turns it on:

  1. Putting the list inside a StackPanel (or any container that gives infinite space in the scroll direction). The list gets as tall as its content and never scrolls, so everything is "visible".
  2. Replacing ItemsPanel with a non-virtualising panel: StackPanel, WrapPanel, UniformGrid.
  3. Setting ScrollViewer.CanContentScroll="False", which switches to pixel-based scrolling and materialises everything.
  4. Grouping without VirtualizingPanel.IsVirtualizingWhenGrouping="True".
  5. Using a plain ItemsControl. Its default panel is a StackPanel, its template has no ScrollViewer, and content scrolling is off, so it never virtualises until you give it a VirtualizingStackPanel, a ScrollViewer in its template and ScrollViewer.CanContentScroll="True".
  6. Filling the list with UI elements, such as ListBoxItems written out in XAML or added to Items. Each one is its own container, so all of them exist from the start and none can be recycled.

A source that is not an IList still virtualises containers, but WPF copies every item into its own list first.

The safest habit is to state every setting explicitly, so a later change to a style or template cannot quietly undo it:

XAML: explicit and correct 
<ListBox ItemsSource="{Binding Rows}"
         VirtualizingPanel.IsVirtualizing="True"
         VirtualizingPanel.VirtualizationMode="Recycling"
         VirtualizingPanel.ScrollUnit="Pixel"
         VirtualizingPanel.CacheLength="1,2"
         VirtualizingPanel.CacheLengthUnit="Page"
         VirtualizingPanel.IsVirtualizingWhenGrouping="True"
         ScrollViewer.IsDeferredScrollingEnabled="False">
  <ListBox.ItemsPanel>
    <ItemsPanelTemplate>
      <VirtualizingStackPanel />
    </ItemsPanelTemplate>
  </ListBox.ItemsPanel>
</ListBox>

What each line asks for: virtualisation on; containers reused rather than rebuilt; smooth scrolling by pixels while still virtualising; one page of containers kept ready above the view and two below (CacheLength is before, after); virtualisation kept when grouping; the content updated live while the scroll thumb is dragged, rather than only when it is released; and VirtualizingStackPanel as the panel, already the default, stated so that a style cannot swap in one that does not virtualise.

VirtualizationMode="Recycling" reuses containers instead of creating and discarding them, which is the single biggest win. In the default Standard mode, a row scrolled out of view has its container thrown away and a new one built for the row coming in. Recycling is not the default for historical compatibility reasons, so set it explicitly every time.

The other common costs

Once lists virtualise, the remaining costs are usually one of these. The visual tree in them means every element actually created on screen, with all templates expanded.

  • Unfrozen freezables. Every unfrozen brush carries change tracking so it can notify whatever uses it. Freeze all static brushes, pens and geometries.
  • Deep visual trees. A template with eight nested Borders multiplied across 5,000 rows is 40,000 elements. Flatten templates used in item containers ruthlessly.
  • Binding to properties without change notification. WPF falls back to PropertyDescriptor subscriptions, which leak and are slow. If the source is a plain CLR object with no INotifyPropertyChanged, either add it or set Mode=OneTime.
  • DynamicResource everywhere. Each one keeps a live lookup. Use it only where the value actually changes.
  • Layout thrash, meaning layout forced to run again and again over large parts of the tree. Changing an inherited property (FontSize) on a root element invalidates the whole tree. Changing Visibility from Collapsed to Visible forces measure on the whole subtree. Opacity and IsHitTestVisible are cheaper if you just want it hidden, though keyboard focus can still reach an element hidden that way.
  • Text rendering. TextOptions.TextFormattingMode="Display" makes small text crisper at 100% DPI; Ideal (the default) is better at high DPI. TextRenderingMode="ClearType" vs Grayscale matters over remote desktop.

Some content is expensive to draw but rarely changes, such as a detailed diagram behind an interactive layer. You can ask WPF to rasterise it, turning its vector drawing into a bitmap, once, and reuse the bitmap until the content changes:

XAML: caching an expensive static subtree 
<Canvas>
  <Canvas.CacheMode>
    <BitmapCache RenderAtScale="1" SnapsToDevicePixels="True" />
  </Canvas.CacheMode>
  <!-- a complex, rarely-changing diagram -->
</Canvas>

BitmapCache rasterises a subtree once and reuses the bitmap. Excellent for static decoration that is transformed or scrolled; harmful for anything that redraws, since you pay for re-rasterising every frame.

Memory leaks

.NET collects objects nobody references, so a WPF leak is always something still holding a reference you forgot about. You notice it as memory that climbs every time a screen is opened and closed. Three sources, in order of frequency:

  1. Event handlers on long-lived objects. A view model subscribing to a singleton service's event is never collected. Use WeakReferenceMessenger, or unsubscribe in a dispose or deactivate hook.
  2. Bindings to non-INotifyPropertyChanged sources. The PropertyDescriptor fallback roots the source object for the application's lifetime.
  3. DataContext retained by a cached Window. Windows you Hide() instead of Close() keep their whole view model graph alive.
IN PLAIN TERMS

Subscribing to an event hands the publisher your phone number. As long as the publisher keeps that number, the garbage collector sees your object as still in use, even after its screen is gone. A service that lives as long as the app is a publisher that never throws a number away. A weak event subscription lets the publisher still call you, without holding your number counting as a reason to keep you alive.

When you cannot unsubscribe at the right moment and cannot change the publisher, subscribe through WeakEventManager:

C# 
// The weak event pattern, for when you can't control the publisher's lifetime.
WeakEventManager<ITaskRepository, TaskChangedEventArgs>
    .AddHandler(_repo, nameof(ITaskRepository.TaskChanged), OnTaskChanged);

Measuring

Guessing at performance problems wastes time; these tools show you where the cost is.

  • Visual Studio's WPF Tree Visualizer and the live visual tree, for element counts and "why is that 400px wide".
  • Snoop, the community tool that has outlived every alternative. Inspect and edit any running WPF app's visual tree, watch binding errors live, profile the visual tree size.
  • PerfView or dotnet-trace for allocation and JIT.
  • RenderCapability.Tier, checked at startup and logged (shift it right by 16 to get 0, 1 or 2, as Part 18 explains), so you know when a user is on software rendering.
RECAP
  • A virtualised list builds only the rows you can see, and one placed in a StackPanel builds them all.
  • Set VirtualizationMode="Recycling" on every large list, because it is not the default.
  • Freeze static brushes and geometries, and keep item templates shallow.
  • Bind only to objects that raise change notifications, or bind them one time.
  • Most leaks are event subscriptions to objects that outlive the screen.
  • Measure with the live visual tree, Snoop or a profiler before changing anything.
PART 20

Testing, packaging, interop

Three things stand between an app that works on your machine and one you can hand to people: tests that tell you it still works, a way to install and update it, and occasionally a piece of older technology it has to host.

Builds on Part 14, MVVM with the Community Toolkit (generated commands), Part 15, dependency injection, Part 16, navigation, windows, dialogs (services behind interfaces) and Part 17, threading and async (STA threads).

Testing view models

Clicking through every screen after each change does not scale, and nobody does it consistently. Automated tests do, but only if the logic can run without a window.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A well-built view model is your app with the screen unplugged. Every button is a command you can execute from code, and every label is a property you can read. A test does exactly what a user would, pressing and reading, without anything being drawn.

The point of keeping System.Windows out of your view models is that this needs no special infrastructure. Each test builds the view model with a fake, a hand-written stand-in for a real dependency that keeps its data in memory, then drives the commands. sut is the usual name for the system under test, the object being tested.

The first test checks that adding a task trims the text, appends the item and clears the draft. The second starts a slow refresh, cancels it, and checks that the running task ends in cancellation with no items loaded. RefreshCancelCommand is the cancel command the toolkit generates from [RelayCommand(IncludeCancelCommand = true)].

C# 
[Fact]
public void Add_appends_and_clears_the_draft()
{
    var repo = new FakeTaskRepository();
    var sut  = new TaskListViewModel(repo) { Draft = "  Write tests  " };

    Assert.True(sut.AddCommand.CanExecute(null));
    sut.AddCommand.Execute(null);

    Assert.Equal("Write tests", Assert.Single(sut.Items).Title);
    Assert.Equal("", sut.Draft);
    Assert.False(sut.AddCommand.CanExecute(null));
}

[Fact]
public async Task Refresh_stops_when_cancelled()
{
    var repo = new SlowFakeRepository(delay: TimeSpan.FromSeconds(30));
    var sut  = new TaskListViewModel(repo);

    Task running = sut.RefreshCommand.ExecuteAsync(null);
    Assert.True(sut.RefreshCommand.IsRunning);

    sut.RefreshCancelCommand.Execute(null);
    await Assert.ThrowsAnyAsync<OperationCanceledException>(() => running);

    Assert.Empty(sut.Items);
}

Testing things that need a UI thread DEEPER

Converters, custom controls and anything constructing a DispatcherObject need an STA thread, and xUnit does not run tests on one, so you need a helper. This one runs your function on a new STA thread, waits for it, and rethrows any failure on the test thread, wrapped in an InvalidOperationException:

C# 
public static class Sta
{
    public static T Run<T>(Func<T> func)
    {
        T result = default!;
        Exception? error = null;

        var t = new Thread(() =>
        {
            try { result = func(); }
            catch (Exception ex) { error = ex; }
        });
        t.SetApartmentState(ApartmentState.STA);
        t.Start();
        t.Join();

        if (error is not null) throw new InvalidOperationException("STA work failed", error);
        return result;
    }
}

[Fact]
public void Pill_template_applies()
{
    var actual = Sta.Run(() =>
    {
        var button = new Button { Style = TestResources.PillButton };
        var host = new Border { Child = button };
        host.Measure(new Size(500, 500));
        host.Arrange(new Rect(0, 0, 500, 500));
        button.ApplyTemplate();
        return button.Template is not null;
    });

    Assert.True(actual);
}

The test forces the button through a real measure and arrange, as a window would, before checking the template. The helper starts no dispatcher loop, so work queued on the dispatcher (BeginInvoke, a DispatcherTimer) never runs. Code after an await does not come back to this thread either: it resumes on the thread pool, where touching a WPF object throws. Keep these tests synchronous.

The Xunit.StaFact package wraps this: [StaFact] runs a test on an STA thread, and [WpfFact] also runs a dispatcher for it, so awaits work there. For end-to-end UI automation, FlaUI is the maintained choice. It drives the app through UI Automation, the Windows accessibility interface that screen readers also use, which is another reason to set AutomationProperties.Name on your controls (Part 31).

Publishing

dotnet publish turns the project into a folder you can ship. The main decision is whether the user's machine must already have .NET.

A framework-dependent app contains only your code and needs the .NET desktop runtime installed. A self-contained app carries the runtime inside it, so it runs on any matching Windows machine with no prerequisite, at the cost of size. -r win-x64 names the runtime identifier, the operating system and processor you are publishing for.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Framework-dependent is like selling a film on disc: small and cheap to ship, but the buyer needs their own player. Self-contained puts a player in the box with every film. Nothing is required from the buyer, the box is much heavier, and each copy carries its own player that you, not the buyer, keep up to date.

bash 
# Framework-dependent: small, requires the .NET 10 desktop runtime installed
dotnet publish -c Release -r win-x64 --self-contained false

# Self-contained: ~150 MB, no runtime prerequisite
dotnet publish -c Release -r win-x64 --self-contained true \
  -p:PublishSingleFile=true \
  -p:IncludeNativeLibrariesForSelfExtract=true \
  -p:EnableCompressionInSingleFile=true \
  -p:PublishReadyToRun=true

The extra switches on the second command: PublishSingleFile bundles everything into one executable. IncludeNativeLibrariesForSelfExtract puts the native DLLs WPF needs inside that file too, extracted when the app starts, rather than leaving them beside it. EnableCompressionInSingleFile shrinks the bundle at a small cost in startup time. PublishReadyToRun precompiles your code to native code, so less is compiled while the app starts, at the cost of larger files.

GOTCHA

Do not enable PublishTrimmed or Native AOT. Trimming removes code the build believes is unused, and Native AOT (compiling the whole app to native code ahead of time) depends on trimming. WPF relies on reflection for XAML type resolution and binding paths, so the trimmer cannot see that those types are used and removes them. The SDK refuses the combination at build time with error NETSDK1168; if you suppress that check, the failures are run-time XamlParseExceptions in whichever screen you didn't test. PublishReadyToRun is safe and helps startup noticeably.

Distribution options, roughly in order of how much you'll enjoy them:

  • MSIX: the current Microsoft answer. Clean install and uninstall, automatic updates, Store or sideload. Needs signing, and the packaged-app identity changes how file paths and registry writes behave.
  • Velopack (successor to Squirrel and Clowd.Squirrel): delta updates, no admin rights, minimal ceremony. The pragmatic choice for internal tools.
  • ClickOnce: still supported for .NET 10, still awkward, still the path of least resistance in some enterprises.
  • An MSI via WiX: when IT requires it.

Settings and file locations

An installed app usually cannot write to its own folder, which is often under Program Files and read-only for normal users. Settings, databases and logs need a per-user location instead.

The old Properties.Settings/app.config system still works but is a dead end. Use IOptions<T> for read-only configuration (Part 15) and write user state yourself. The class below keeps every path in one place, under the user's local application data folder (%LOCALAPPDATA%):

C# 
public static class AppPaths
{
    private static string Root => Path.Combine(
        Environment.GetFolderPath(Environment.SpecialFolder.LocalApplicationData), "Tasks");

    public static string Settings => Path.Combine(Root, "settings.json");
    public static string Database => Path.Combine(Root, "tasks.db");
    public static string Logs     => Path.Combine(Root, "logs");

    public static void EnsureCreated() => Directory.CreateDirectory(Root);
}

Call EnsureCreated() once at startup, before anything writes to these paths.

Interop DEEPER

Sometimes the control you need already exists in another technology: a WinForms PropertyGrid, or a web page. WPF can host both.

To follow the gotcha below you need one Windows term. An HWND is a Win32 window handle. In classic Windows UI, every control is its own small window with its own HWND. WPF instead draws the entire content of a window itself, inside a single HWND.

Hosting a WinForms control needs WinForms enabled in the project and two XML namespaces:

XAML: hosting a WinForms control 
<!-- csproj needs: <UseWindowsForms>true</UseWindowsForms> -->
xmlns:wfi="clr-namespace:System.Windows.Forms.Integration;assembly=WindowsFormsIntegration"
xmlns:wf="clr-namespace:System.Windows.Forms;assembly=System.Windows.Forms"

<wfi:WindowsFormsHost Height="300">
  <wf:PropertyGrid x:Name="Grid" />
</wfi:WindowsFormsHost>

Embedding a browser uses the WebView2 package, which renders with Microsoft Edge through the WebView2 Runtime installed on the machine:

XAML: embedding a browser 
<!-- PackageReference: Microsoft.Web.WebView2 -->
xmlns:wv2="clr-namespace:Microsoft.Web.WebView2.Wpf;assembly=Microsoft.Web.WebView2.Wpf"

<wv2:WebView2 Source="https://localhost:5001/report" />
IN PLAIN TERMS

A hosted control is like a television set into a painted wall. You can paint the wall however you like, but you cannot paint over the screen: anything you draw on the wall in that spot ends up behind the television. A WPF popup is different: it is a separate small window of its own, like a sign held up in front of the wall, so it can cover the screen.

GOTCHA

Both of these are airspace problems: they're HWND-based child windows rendered by a different system, so they always draw on top of the WPF content in their window, regardless of z-order. Adorners, overlay panels and in-window dialogs that should overlap them won't. Popups, menus and tooltips are the exception, because WPF shows them in windows of their own. Plan your layout so hosted content never needs WPF content floating above it. For web content, WebView2CompositionControl in the same package draws into WPF's own content and so avoids the airspace limit, at some cost in frame rate.

For Win32 proper, HwndHost hosts a native window and HwndSource hosts WPF content inside a native application. WindowInteropHelper gets you the HWND of a WPF window, which you need for most shell APIs and for DwmSetWindowAttribute if you want Mica or a custom title bar colour (Part 32).

RECAP
  • View models with no WPF types in them can be tested with plain unit tests and fakes.
  • Code that creates WPF objects needs an STA thread in tests.
  • Self-contained publishing ships the runtime with the app; framework-dependent needs it installed.
  • Never enable trimming or Native AOT for a WPF app; ReadyToRun is safe.
  • Write settings and data under the user's local application data folder, not beside the executable.
  • Hosted WinForms controls and the standard WebView2 control cover any WPF content drawn in the same window.
PART 21

Text, typography and documents

Most of any screen is text. WPF gives you a few text controls of very different weight, a typography engine that most of .NET lacks, and a document model for anything longer than a sentence. This part shows which to use, and the handful of details that make text look wrong when you miss them.

Builds on Part 7, the control content model, Part 8, resources and Part 9, styles and triggers.

TextBlock, Label, TextBox: which and why

WPF has several controls that show text, and they are not interchangeable. Use the heavy one everywhere and a long list carries a template and extra elements for every caption. Use the light one where a user needs to copy a value and they cannot select it. The table sorts them by job.

ControlBaseUse for
TextBlockFrameworkElementRead-only text. Lightest of the three: no template, no content model. The default choice.
LabelContentControlText with an access key that focuses another control (Target). Heavier; use it only when you need _Access keys.
TextBoxControlEditable. Set IsReadOnly rather than using a TextBlock when you want selectable text.
AccessTextFrameworkElementInside templates, renders _ as an underlined mnemonic.

An access key (also called a mnemonic) is the underlined letter that lets a keyboard user jump to a control with Alt plus that letter. You mark it with an underscore before the letter in the text.

A TextBlock is also more than a string. Its content is a collection of inlines: pieces of text that flow along the same lines but each carry their own formatting. A Run is a stretch of plain text, Span groups runs under shared formatting, LineBreak ends a line and Hyperlink is a clickable span.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Think of a sentence in a word processor where you made one word bold and another a link. It is still one sentence that wraps as a whole, but inside it are stretches of text with different formatting. Inlines are those stretches. What it does not hold is controls laid out the way a panel lays them out: a button sits in the sentence wrapped in an InlineUIContainer (XAML adds the wrapper for you when you put a control directly inside), where it flows like one oversized word.

The snippet shows both ideas. The Label's underscore makes R its access key, and Target says which control receives focus. The TextBlock mixes a bound bold run, a line break, a link and an italic span. LineStackingStrategy="BlockLineHeight" makes every line exactly the LineHeight you set, instead of growing to fit the tallest piece of text on it.

XAML 
<!-- Label with a mnemonic that focuses the box -->
<Label Content="_Registration number" Target="{Binding ElementName=RegBox}" />
<TextBox x:Name="RegBox" />

<!-- TextBlock's content property is Inlines: runs and spans; a control is wrapped in an InlineUIContainer -->
<TextBlock TextWrapping="Wrap" LineHeight="20" LineStackingStrategy="BlockLineHeight">
  <Run Text="Saved to " />
  <Run Text="{Binding FileName}" FontWeight="SemiBold" />
  <LineBreak />
  <Hyperlink NavigateUri="{Binding FolderUri}" RequestNavigate="OnNavigate">
    Open containing folder
  </Hyperlink>
  <Span FontStyle="Italic" Foreground="{DynamicResource MutedBrush}">
    <Run Text=" (read only)" />
  </Span>
</TextBlock>

Hyperlink does not open a browser by itself: it only raises RequestNavigate, and outside a navigation host nobody answers. Handle the event and start the browser yourself. UseShellExecute = true is what lets Windows pick the default browser for a URL; on modern .NET it defaults to false, and starting a URL without it fails.

C# 
private void OnNavigate(object sender, RequestNavigateEventArgs e)
{
    Process.Start(new ProcessStartInfo(e.Uri.AbsoluteUri) { UseShellExecute = true });
    e.Handled = true;
}
GOTCHA

Run.Text is bindable, but the whitespace between <Run> elements is significant and easy to lose. Writing each Run on its own line collapses the newline to a single space. That is usually what you want, occasionally not. Use xml:space="preserve" on the TextBlock if exact spacing matters.

Typography

A column of prices only lines up if every digit has the same width. Body text reads better with ligatures and proper fractions. Good fonts carry these alternatives inside the font file as OpenType features: optional variants of the letters and figures that an app has to ask for by name. WPF exposes the full OpenType feature set, which almost nothing else in .NET does.

The first TextBlock below switches features on through the Typography attached properties. The second sets TextOptions, which control how glyphs (the drawn shapes of the letters) are rendered, including where they land on the pixel grid and how their edges are anti-aliased, rather than which glyphs are chosen.

XAML 
<TextBlock FontFamily="Cambria" FontSize="15"
           Typography.NumeralStyle="OldStyle"
           Typography.NumeralAlignment="Tabular"
           Typography.StandardLigatures="True"
           Typography.DiscretionaryLigatures="False"
           Typography.Kerning="True"
           Typography.Fraction="Slashed"
           Text="1/2 kg, 1 234,50 kr" />

<!-- Rendering hints that matter for small UI text -->
<TextBlock TextOptions.TextFormattingMode="Display"
           TextOptions.TextRenderingMode="ClearType"
           TextOptions.TextHintingMode="Fixed"
           Text="Dense grid label" />

TextFormattingMode="Display" snaps glyph metrics to whole pixels. That is sharper for small text on 100% DPI displays, and worse for scaling and animation. Ideal, the default, keeps sub-pixel metrics and is correct on high-DPI screens. If your users are on 1080p at 100%, switch to Display app-wide and the UI immediately looks less blurry.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Ideal places each letter exactly where the font designer intended, even when that falls a third of the way between two pixels. The screen then shades both pixels, which reads as soft edges on small text at low resolution. Display nudges each letter onto whole pixels: crisper, but the spacing moves slightly. Text that grows or slides then visibly jumps from pixel to pixel instead of moving smoothly.

Embedding a font

Your users will not have your chosen font installed. Ship it inside the assembly: give the font files the Resource build action, point a FontFamily at them with a pack URI, and set it on the window. Keep the FontFamily resource in App.xaml: a StaticResource in the window's own attributes is resolved before the window's Resources are read, so it could not see a key defined there.

csproj + XAML 
<ItemGroup>
  <Resource Include="Assets\Fonts\Inter-Regular.ttf" />
  <Resource Include="Assets\Fonts\Inter-SemiBold.ttf" />
</ItemGroup>

<!-- App.xaml, inside Application.Resources -->
<!-- The # is followed by the font's FAMILY name, not the file name -->
<FontFamily x:Key="UiFont">pack://application:,,,/Assets/Fonts/#Inter</FontFamily>

<!-- MainWindow.xaml: FontFamily is inherited by everything inside the window -->
<Window x:Class="Tasks.MainWindow" FontFamily="{StaticResource UiFont}">
  ...
</Window>

The trailing slash before # is required and the name after it is the family name recorded inside the file, which is often not the filename. If the font doesn't appear, that mismatch is almost always why.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A font file has a filename, like Inter-SemiBold.ttf, and inside it a family name, like Inter, much as a music file can be called track07.mp3 while its tags name the album. The part before # tells WPF which folder to search; the part after it is the family name WPF compares with what each file says inside. Several files can share one family name, which is how Regular and SemiBold become one family with two weights.

The snippet sets the font on the <Window> element itself, not through a Window style. An implicit style applies only to elements of exactly its target type, and your window's real type is MainWindow, so a style for Window never reaches it (a style for Control misses Button and TextBox the same way). FontFamily is inherited: everything inside the window picks it up unless something overrides it. The theme styles for Menu, ContextMenu, ToolTip and StatusBar do override it with the system font, so give those their own style.

FlowDocument: rich text and reading views

A TextBlock is fine for a sentence. Release notes with headings, a bulleted list and a table, or an invoice someone may print, need text that rewraps when the window resizes and can be cut into pages.

A FlowDocument is WPF's model for that: a paginated, reflowable document built from headings, paragraphs, lists, tables, figures and floaters. It is the right tool for release notes, help pages, generated reports and anything print-shaped. Its content is made of blocks, elements that stack vertically (Paragraph, List, Table), and the blocks contain the same inlines a TextBlock uses.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A FlowDocument is closer to a web page than to a PDF. You describe the content in order (a heading, a paragraph, a list) and the viewer decides where lines and pages break, based on the space it has. Narrow the window and the text rewraps; switch the viewer to page mode and the same content is cut into pages. Unlike a PDF, nothing about the final positions is fixed until it is shown.

You always show a FlowDocument inside a host. Three hosts display one, and RichTextBox edits one:

HostBehaviour
FlowDocumentScrollViewerContinuous scroll. The one you usually want in an app.
FlowDocumentPageViewerPaginated, one page at a time.
FlowDocumentReaderLets the user switch between one page, two pages side by side and continuous scroll, plus a search bar and zoom. Heavy.
RichTextBoxEditable FlowDocument.

The example builds release notes in XAML. Notice that every piece of text sits in a Paragraph, even inside a list item or a table cell: list items and cells hold blocks, not bare text.

XAML 
<FlowDocumentScrollViewer VerticalScrollBarVisibility="Auto">
  <FlowDocument ColumnWidth="400" FontFamily="Georgia" FontSize="14">
    <Paragraph FontSize="20" FontWeight="Bold" Margin="0,0,0,8">
      Release 4.2
    </Paragraph>
    <Paragraph>
      The importer now streams rather than buffering. See
      <Hyperlink NavigateUri="https://example.invalid/notes">the notes</Hyperlink>.
    </Paragraph>
    <List MarkerStyle="Disc">
      <ListItem><Paragraph>Fixed clipboard on .NET 9</Paragraph></ListItem>
      <ListItem><Paragraph>Added dark theme</Paragraph></ListItem>
    </List>
    <Table CellSpacing="0">
      <Table.Columns>
        <TableColumn Width="2*" /><TableColumn Width="*" />
      </Table.Columns>
      <TableRowGroup>
        <TableRow Background="#EEE">
          <TableCell><Paragraph>Setting</Paragraph></TableCell>
          <TableCell><Paragraph>Default</Paragraph></TableCell>
        </TableRow>
      </TableRowGroup>
    </Table>
  </FlowDocument>
</FlowDocumentScrollViewer>
GOTCHA

FlowDocument is not a FrameworkElement and its content is not bindable the way element trees are. There is no ItemsSource. To generate one from data you build it in code, or bind the viewer's Document property to a FlowDocument your view model produced. That technically drags a presentation type (a class that belongs to the UI layer) into the view model, so most teams put the builder in a service returning FlowDocument.

Here is such a builder. It takes plain data and returns a finished document: a heading paragraph, then a two-column table with one row per invoice line. ColumnWidth = double.PositiveInfinity matters when the document is paginated or printed; left at its default, a wide page is split into several narrow newspaper-style columns.

C#: building one from data 
public static FlowDocument BuildInvoice(Invoice invoice)
{
    var doc = new FlowDocument
    {
        FontFamily = new FontFamily("Segoe UI"),
        FontSize = 12,
        PagePadding = new Thickness(48),
        ColumnWidth = double.PositiveInfinity,   // single column
    };

    doc.Blocks.Add(new Paragraph(new Run($"Invoice {invoice.Number}"))
    {
        FontSize = 22, FontWeight = FontWeights.Bold, Margin = new Thickness(0, 0, 0, 16)
    });

    var table = new Table { CellSpacing = 0 };
    table.Columns.Add(new TableColumn { Width = new GridLength(3, GridUnitType.Star) });
    table.Columns.Add(new TableColumn { Width = new GridLength(1, GridUnitType.Star) });

    var body = new TableRowGroup();
    foreach (var line in invoice.Lines)
    {
        var row = new TableRow();
        row.Cells.Add(new TableCell(new Paragraph(new Run(line.Description))));
        row.Cells.Add(new TableCell(new Paragraph(new Run(line.Amount.ToString("C", invoice.Culture))))
        {
            TextAlignment = TextAlignment.Right
        });
        body.Rows.Add(row);
    }
    table.RowGroups.Add(body);
    doc.Blocks.Add(table);

    return doc;
}

RichTextBox and persisting formatted text DEEPER

A RichTextBox lets users write formatted text, and sooner or later you need to save what they wrote and load it back. Both go through a TextRange: a stretch of the document between two positions. ContentStart to ContentEnd covers everything; Selection is the range the user has highlighted.

The code saves and loads the whole document as XAML, reads the selected text and makes the selection bold. XAML keeps every WPF formatting detail. Pass DataFormats.Rtf instead when the text has to open in other programs such as Word.

C# 
// Save the whole document as XAML (lossless) or RTF (portable)
public static string ToXaml(RichTextBox box)
{
    var range = new TextRange(box.Document.ContentStart, box.Document.ContentEnd);
    using var ms = new MemoryStream();
    range.Save(ms, DataFormats.Xaml);
    return Encoding.UTF8.GetString(ms.ToArray());
}

public static void LoadXaml(RichTextBox box, string xaml)
{
    var range = new TextRange(box.Document.ContentStart, box.Document.ContentEnd);
    using var ms = new MemoryStream(Encoding.UTF8.GetBytes(xaml));
    range.Load(ms, DataFormats.Xaml);
}

// Plain text of the current selection
string selected = box.Selection.Text;

// Apply formatting to the selection
box.Selection.ApplyPropertyValue(TextElement.FontWeightProperty, FontWeights.Bold);

DataFormats.XamlPackage is the third option: XAML plus embedded images in a single container. Use it when users paste screenshots.

RECAP
  • Use TextBlock for read-only text, Label only when you need an access key, and a read-only TextBox when users must select the text.
  • A TextBlock holds inlines, so one block of text can mix bold runs, line breaks and links.
  • A Hyperlink opens nothing until you handle RequestNavigate.
