  • For an embedded font, the name after # is the family name inside the file, not the filename.
  • Use a FlowDocument for long, structured, reflowing text, and build it in code when it comes from data.
  • Save RichTextBox content through a TextRange as XAML, RTF or XamlPackage.
PART 22

Images, media and imaging

Images are where WPF apps most often waste memory without anyone noticing. This part covers loading pictures at the size you show them, where image files belong in the project, producing and editing bitmaps in code, reading photo metadata, and playing sound and video.

Builds on Part 8, resources and Part 17, threading and async (freezable objects).

Loading images correctly

Picture a photo browser with a grid of two hundred thumbnails. It works on your test folder of small images, then a user points it at their camera roll and memory climbs past a gigabyte.

The cause is decoding. To decode an image is to unpack the compressed file (JPEG, PNG) into a grid of pixels in memory, four bytes per pixel. The naive <Image Source="photo.jpg" /> decodes at full resolution regardless of display size. For a 4000×3000 photo shown in a 120px thumbnail that is roughly 48 MB of pixels per image. DecodePixelWidth fixes it.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Showing a full-size photo in a thumbnail is like printing a wall poster and then looking at it from across the room: it looks the same as a postcard would, but you paid for the poster. Image scales the picture down for display, yet the memory holds every original pixel. DecodePixelWidth asks for the postcard-sized print in the first place.

The XAML below declares the bitmap explicitly so you can set its decoding options. Set the first two on almost every image from a file; add the third when the file at that path can change.

XAML 
<Image Width="120" Height="90">
  <Image.Source>
    <BitmapImage UriSource="{Binding PhotoPath}"
                 DecodePixelWidth="240"
                 CacheOption="OnLoad"
                 CreateOptions="IgnoreImageCache" />
  </Image.Source>
</Image>
SettingWhy
DecodePixelWidthDecode at this width only. Set it to roughly 2× the display size for high-DPI headroom. Set one dimension; the other follows.
CacheOption="OnLoad"Read the whole stream immediately and close the file. Without it the file stays locked and you cannot delete or overwrite it.
CreateOptions="IgnoreImageCache"Bypass WPF's URI-keyed cache. Needed when the file at a path changes and you want the new content.

In code, the same settings go between BeginInit and EndInit. This version reads from a stream you open and close yourself, so the file is not held open once the method returns. The last step is freezing the bitmap: Freeze() makes it permanently read-only, and a frozen object can be used from any thread. CacheOption = OnLoad is required here, not optional: the stream is closed as soon as the method returns, so the bitmap must have read every byte by then.

C#: loading from a stream without locking the file 
public static BitmapImage LoadThumbnail(string path, int width)
{
    using var stream = File.OpenRead(path);
    var bmp = new BitmapImage();
    bmp.BeginInit();
    bmp.CacheOption = BitmapCacheOption.OnLoad;
    bmp.DecodePixelWidth = width;
    bmp.StreamSource = stream;
    bmp.EndInit();
    bmp.Freeze();        // safe to hand to the UI thread from a background thread
    return bmp;
}

Because the result is frozen, this whole method can run on a thread-pool thread. That is how you build a responsive photo grid: decode in the background, hand the frozen bitmaps to the UI.

Build actions for images

An image you add to the project has to end up somewhere the running app can find it. The build action, set in the file's properties, decides where it goes: inside the compiled assembly, or as a loose file next to it. That in turn decides which pack URI reaches it; a pack URI is WPF's address format for files that belong to the application.

Build actionResultURI
ResourceEmbedded in the assembly. Default and usually right.pack://application:,,,/Assets/icon.png or /Assets/icon.png
Content + copyLoose file beside the exe.pack://application:,,,/Assets/icon.png (listed at build time), or pack://siteoforigin:,,,/Assets/icon.png for files added after the build
EmbeddedResourceCLR resource. Not reachable by pack URI: it needs GetManifestResourceStream. Avoid for images.No pack URI
IN PLAIN TERMS

Resource and EmbeddedResource both put the image inside the same DLL, which is why they are so easy to confuse. The difference is which compartment. Resource files go into the compartment WPF's pack URIs search; EmbeddedResource files go into the general .NET one, which WPF does not look in. The image is there either way, but only one of them can be found by Source="/Assets/icon.png".

Encoding, screenshots and pixel work DEEPER

Sometimes you need to produce an image rather than show one: export a chart, save a thumbnail, attach a screenshot of a form to a bug report. An encoder turns pixels in memory into a file format; a decoder does the reverse.

The method below renders any element into a RenderTargetBitmap, an off-screen bitmap you can draw a visual into, at the DPI you choose, then saves it as PNG. Pass 192 for a double-resolution image.

C#: render any element to a PNG 
public static void SaveElementAsPng(FrameworkElement element, string path, double dpi = 96)
{
    double scale = dpi / 96.0;
    var rtb = new RenderTargetBitmap(
        (int)Math.Ceiling(element.ActualWidth  * scale),
        (int)Math.Ceiling(element.ActualHeight * scale),
        dpi, dpi, PixelFormats.Pbgra32);

    // Render through a VisualBrush so element transforms and margins are respected.
    var visual = new DrawingVisual();
    using (var dc = visual.RenderOpen())
    {
        dc.DrawRectangle(new VisualBrush(element), null,
            new Rect(new Point(), new Size(element.ActualWidth, element.ActualHeight)));
    }
    rtb.Render(visual);

    var encoder = new PngBitmapEncoder();
    encoder.Frames.Add(BitmapFrame.Create(rtb));
    using var fs = File.Create(path);
    encoder.Save(fs);
}

Encoders available out of the box: PngBitmapEncoder, JpegBitmapEncoder (with QualityLevel), BmpBitmapEncoder, GifBitmapEncoder, TiffBitmapEncoder, WmpBitmapEncoder. Decoders mirror them, and BitmapDecoder.Create sniffs the format.

For generated images (a heat map, a waveform, a paint canvas) you want to set pixels yourself. WriteableBitmap gives you direct access to its memory. The pattern is always the same: lock, write into the back buffer, mark the changed area with AddDirtyRect, unlock.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A WriteableBitmap keeps two copies of its pixels: a back buffer you write into, and the copy the screen shows. It works like editing a draft while readers see the last published version. Lock gives you the draft, AddDirtyRect marks which part you changed, and Unlock publishes. Only the marked area is copied across, so a change outside it stays unseen until a later dirty rectangle covers it.

The loop fills every pixel with one colour. Each pixel is four bytes in blue, green, red, alpha order, and each row is BackBufferStride bytes long, which can be more than width times four.

C#: WriteableBitmap for direct pixel access 
var wb = new WriteableBitmap(width, height, 96, 96, PixelFormats.Bgra32, null);

wb.Lock();
try
{
    unsafe
    {
        var buffer = (byte*)wb.BackBuffer;
        int stride = wb.BackBufferStride;
        for (int y = 0; y < height; y++)
        for (int x = 0; x < width; x++)
        {
            int i = y * stride + x * 4;
            buffer[i + 0] = blue;
            buffer[i + 1] = green;
            buffer[i + 2] = red;
            buffer[i + 3] = 255;          // alpha: fully opaque
        }
    }
    wb.AddDirtyRect(new Int32Rect(0, 0, width, height));
}
finally { wb.Unlock(); }

Requires <AllowUnsafeBlocks>true</AllowUnsafeBlocks> in the project file. The safe alternative is wb.WritePixels(rect, array, stride, offset), which copies: fine for occasional updates, too slow for per-frame work.

Reading metadata DEEPER

A photo app that sorts by date taken or shows the camera model needs EXIF data: the facts a camera writes into the image file alongside the pixels. BitmapMetadata exposes the common fields as properties. For anything else, GetQuery takes a path into the metadata; 34855 is the EXIF tag number for ISO speed.

The creation and cache options ask the decoder to do no work up front, which keeps reading metadata cheap. Note that DateTaken is a string, hence the parse.

C# 
using var fs = File.OpenRead(path);
var decoder = BitmapDecoder.Create(fs, BitmapCreateOptions.DelayCreation,
                                       BitmapCacheOption.OnDemand);
var frame = decoder.Frames[0];

if (frame.Metadata is BitmapMetadata meta)
{
    string? camera = meta.CameraModel;
    DateTime? taken = DateTime.TryParse(meta.DateTaken, out var d) ? d : null;
    var tags = meta.Keywords;
    // Raw EXIF by path:
    object? iso = meta.GetQuery("/app1/ifd/exif/subifd:{uint=34855}");
}

Audio and video

MediaElement plays audio and video files. Declare it in XAML, then drive it from code or leave it to play on its own. Setting LoadedBehavior="Manual" is what hands control to your code, and the three events tell you when the media is ready, finished or broken.

XAML 
<MediaElement x:Name="Player"
              Source="{Binding VideoPath}"
              LoadedBehavior="Manual"
              UnloadedBehavior="Stop"
              ScrubbingEnabled="True"
              Stretch="Uniform"
              MediaOpened="OnOpened"
              MediaEnded="OnEnded"
              MediaFailed="OnFailed" />

With that in place, playback is a few calls. NaturalDuration is only known once the media has opened, which is why the code checks HasTimeSpan.

C# 
Player.Play();
Player.Pause();
Player.Position = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(30);
double seconds = Player.NaturalDuration.HasTimeSpan
    ? Player.NaturalDuration.TimeSpan.TotalSeconds : 0;
GOTCHA

Set LoadedBehavior to Manual before you drive playback with Play(), Pause() or Position. With the default, Play, the element runs the media itself and does not take instructions: Play(), Pause() and Stop() throw NotSupportedException unless LoadedBehavior or UnloadedBehavior is Manual. MediaElement also wraps Windows Media Player codecs, so format support depends on the machine and there is no error until MediaFailed fires. For anything serious, host LibVLCSharp.WPF or a WebView2 instead.

For short UI sounds, skip MediaElement entirely:

C# 
SystemSounds.Exclamation.Play();                      // system sounds
new SoundPlayer(@"Assets\ding.wav").Play();           // WAV only, fire and forget
RECAP
  • Set DecodePixelWidth on images shown smaller than their real size, or each one costs its full resolution in memory.
  • Use CacheOption="OnLoad" so loading an image does not lock the file.
  • Frozen bitmaps can be decoded on a background thread and handed to the UI.
  • Give image files the Resource build action; EmbeddedResource cannot be reached by a pack URI.
  • MediaElement needs LoadedBehavior="Manual" before code can control playback.
PART 23

Input, focus and keyboard

Every key press, click and touch goes to one particular element. Knowing which element, and why input sometimes does not arrive where you expect, is most of what input work in WPF comes down to.

Builds on Part 5, dependency properties (attached properties) and Part 6, routed events and commands.

Two kinds of focus

You are typing in a text box, then choose Edit, Cut from the menu. The menu now has the keyboard, yet Cut still has to know which text box to cut from. WPF solves this by tracking focus in two ways at once. This distinction causes more subtle bugs than anything else in WPF input.

  • Keyboard focus: exactly one element in the entire desktop has it. Keyboard.FocusedElement. This is where key events go.
  • Logical focus: one element per focus scope remembers that it was focused. A focus scope is a region that keeps its own record of focus. A Window, Menu, ToolBar and ContextMenu are each focus scopes by default.

So when you open a menu, the text box loses keyboard focus but keeps logical focus within the window's scope, which is how the menu's Cut command still knows what to act on.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Imagine reading two books with a finger kept in each. Your eyes can only be on one page at a time: that is keyboard focus. Each finger marks your place in its own book: that is logical focus, one per focus scope. Glance at the menu and your eyes move, but the finger in the window still marks the text box, so Cut knows where to act.

The calls below set and query both kinds. Most of the time Focus() on the element is all you need; the FocusManager calls matter when you work across scopes.

C# 
// Keyboard focus: returns the element that actually got it (may be null)
IInputElement? got = Keyboard.Focus(myTextBox);

// Or, equivalently for a UIElement:
bool ok = myTextBox.Focus();

// Logical focus within a scope
FocusManager.SetFocusedElement(FocusManager.GetFocusScope(myTextBox), myTextBox);

// Which scope am I in?
DependencyObject scope = FocusManager.GetFocusScope(myTextBox);

// Make a panel its own focus scope (rarely a good idea: it breaks tabbing)
FocusManager.SetIsFocusScope(myPanel, true);
GOTCHA

Focus() silently fails if the element is not yet loaded, is collapsed, or has Focusable="False". Focusing on startup or after a template swap must be deferred: Dispatcher.InvokeAsync(() => box.Focus(), DispatcherPriority.Loaded). A focus call that "does nothing" is nearly always a timing problem, not a focus problem.

An attached property for initial focus

A dialog should open with the cursor in its first field. Writing that in the code-behind of every window is repetitive and easy to get wrong because of the timing above. An attached property packages it once, so any element can opt in from XAML with local:FocusOnLoad.IsEnabled="True".

When the property is set, it waits for the element's Loaded event (or runs at once if that has already happened), then queues the focus call on the dispatcher so it runs after loading has finished. A text box also gets its contents selected, so typing replaces them.

C# 
public static class FocusOnLoad
{
    public static readonly DependencyProperty IsEnabledProperty =
        DependencyProperty.RegisterAttached(
            "IsEnabled", typeof(bool), typeof(FocusOnLoad),
            new PropertyMetadata(false, OnChanged));

    public static void SetIsEnabled(DependencyObject d, bool v) => d.SetValue(IsEnabledProperty, v);
    public static bool GetIsEnabled(DependencyObject d) => (bool)d.GetValue(IsEnabledProperty);

    private static void OnChanged(DependencyObject d, DependencyPropertyChangedEventArgs e)
    {
        if (d is not FrameworkElement fe || e.NewValue is not true) return;

        void Handler(object s, RoutedEventArgs _)
        {
            fe.Loaded -= Handler;
            fe.Dispatcher.InvokeAsync(() =>
            {
                fe.Focus();
                if (fe is TextBox tb) tb.SelectAll();
            }, DispatcherPriority.Input);
        }

        if (fe.IsLoaded) Handler(fe, new RoutedEventArgs());
        else fe.Loaded += Handler;
    }
}

Tab navigation

Keyboard users move through a form with Tab. By default the order follows the element tree, which is usually right. When it is not, or when Tab should stay inside a region, you change it per container.

Each element Tab stops on is a tab stop. TabIndex sets the order explicitly, and the attached property KeyboardNavigation.TabNavigation decides how a container's children take part.

XAML 
<Grid KeyboardNavigation.TabNavigation="Local">
  <TextBox TabIndex="0" />
  <TextBox TabIndex="1" />
  <Button  TabIndex="2" IsDefault="True"  Content="OK" />
  <Button  TabIndex="3" IsCancel="True"   Content="Cancel" />
</Grid>
TabNavigationBehaviour
ContinueDefault. Tab flows in and out of the container freely.
LocalTabIndex values are scoped to this container.
CycleTab wraps inside the container and never leaves. Correct for a modal dialog's content.
OnceThe whole container is a single tab stop. Right for a radio-button group or a toolbar.
NoneSkipped entirely.

IsDefault="True" makes Enter fire the button from anywhere in the window. IsCancel="True" wires Escape and, in a dialog, sets DialogResult = false. Both come free and both are routinely forgotten.

Keyboard and mouse events

Fixed shortcuts belong in key bindings (Part 6). Keys whose meaning depends on state, like Escape to revert only while editing, and dragging on a canvas need the raw events. Keyboard events are routed: handle PreviewKeyDown on a window or panel and you see the key before the focused control does, so you can claim it by setting e.Handled.

Two details in the handler below. When Alt is held, e.Key reports Key.System and the real key moves to e.SystemKey. Modifier keys can be read from Keyboard.Modifiers or, equivalently, e.KeyboardDevice.Modifiers.

C# 
private void OnPreviewKeyDown(object sender, KeyEventArgs e)
{
    // Same as e.KeyboardDevice.Modifiers
    bool ctrl  = (Keyboard.Modifiers & ModifierKeys.Control) != 0;
    bool shift = (Keyboard.Modifiers & ModifierKeys.Shift) != 0;

    // e.Key is the "logical" key; e.SystemKey holds it when Alt is down
    Key key = e.Key == Key.System ? e.SystemKey : e.Key;

    if (ctrl && key == Key.Enter) { Commit(); e.Handled = true; }
    if (key == Key.Escape)        { Revert(); e.Handled = true; }
}

private void OnMouseMove(object sender, MouseEventArgs e)
{
    Point local  = e.GetPosition((IInputElement)sender);
    Point screen = ((UIElement)sender).PointToScreen(local);

    if (e.LeftButton == MouseButtonState.Pressed) Drag(local);
}

In the mouse handler, GetPosition returns the pointer position relative to whichever element you pass, and PointToScreen converts it to screen coordinates, in physical pixels.

The last bullet below depends on hit testing: how WPF decides which element is under the pointer. It only counts areas that are actually painted.

  • Text input: use PreviewTextInput and TextInput, not KeyDown. Key events tell you which key was pressed; text events give you the actual characters after IME, dead keys and keyboard layout have been applied. Filtering a numeric field with KeyDown breaks for anyone using a non-US layout.
  • Mouse capture: element.CaptureMouse() routes all mouse input to that element until ReleaseMouseCapture(), even when the pointer leaves it. Essential for drag operations; leaking capture freezes the UI, because nothing else receives the mouse.
  • IsHitTestVisible="False" makes an element transparent to input without hiding it. Background="{x:Null}" on a panel makes it un-hittable too: set Background="Transparent" if you want a panel to receive mouse events over its empty area. This one catches everybody.
IN PLAIN TERMS

A panel with no background is like a window frame with no glass in it: you see through it and your hand goes straight through, so the click lands on whatever is behind. Transparent is clear glass. It looks exactly the same, but there is now something there to touch, so the panel gets the click.

Here is a drag done properly. Capture the mouse on button down so moves keep arriving even if the pointer leaves the element, release it on button up, and reset your state when capture is lost. Capture can be taken away without a button-up, for example when another window opens or the user switches apps.

C#: a correct drag with capture 
private Point _origin;
private bool _dragging;

protected override void OnMouseLeftButtonDown(MouseButtonEventArgs e)
{
    _origin = e.GetPosition(this);
    _dragging = true;
    CaptureMouse();
    e.Handled = true;
}

protected override void OnMouseMove(MouseEventArgs e)
{
    if (!_dragging) return;
    var p = e.GetPosition(this);
    Offset = new Vector(p.X - _origin.X, p.Y - _origin.Y);
}

protected override void OnMouseLeftButtonUp(MouseButtonEventArgs e)
{
    if (!_dragging) return;
    _dragging = false;
    ReleaseMouseCapture();
    e.Handled = true;
}

protected override void OnLostMouseCapture(MouseEventArgs e)
{
    _dragging = false;      // always reset here: capture can be stolen
}

Touch and manipulation DEEPER

On a touch screen people expect to drag, pinch to zoom and twist with two fingers. Tracking each finger yourself is tedious. A manipulation is WPF doing it for you: it combines one or more touch points into a single stream of changes, a translation, a scale and a rotation, which you apply to a transform.

Setting IsManipulationEnabled="True" turns manipulation events on for the element. The element's RenderTransform is a MatrixTransform so each change can be folded into one matrix.

XAML + C# 
<Border IsManipulationEnabled="True"
        ManipulationStarting="OnStarting"
        ManipulationDelta="OnDelta"
        ManipulationInertiaStarting="OnInertia">
  <Border.RenderTransform><MatrixTransform /></Border.RenderTransform>
</Border>

The handlers do three jobs. OnStarting picks the coordinate space and which kinds of movement to allow. OnDelta applies each change around the point between the fingers. OnInertia sets inertia, the coasting after the fingers lift: decelerations are in device-independent pixels per millisecond squared, so the expressions convert from inches per second squared.

C# 
private void OnStarting(object sender, ManipulationStartingEventArgs e)
{
    e.ManipulationContainer = canvas;     // coordinate space for the deltas
    e.Mode = ManipulationModes.All;       // translate + scale + rotate
}

private void OnDelta(object sender, ManipulationDeltaEventArgs e)
{
    var element = (FrameworkElement)e.Source;
    var transform = (MatrixTransform)element.RenderTransform;
    Matrix m = transform.Matrix;

    Point centre = e.ManipulationOrigin;
    m.ScaleAt(e.DeltaManipulation.Scale.X, e.DeltaManipulation.Scale.Y, centre.X, centre.Y);
    m.RotateAt(e.DeltaManipulation.Rotation, centre.X, centre.Y);
    m.Translate(e.DeltaManipulation.Translation.X, e.DeltaManipulation.Translation.Y);

    transform.Matrix = m;
    e.Handled = true;
}

private void OnInertia(object sender, ManipulationInertiaStartingEventArgs e)
{
    e.TranslationBehavior.DesiredDeceleration = 10.0 * 96 / (1000.0 * 1000.0);
    e.ExpansionBehavior.DesiredDeceleration   = 0.1 * 96 / (1000.0 * 1000.0);
}

By default WPF promotes unhandled touch to mouse events, so a touch-unaware app still works. Set Stylus.IsPressAndHoldEnabled="False" on elements where the press-and-hold-to-right-click delay makes dragging feel laggy.

RECAP
  • Keyboard focus is the one element receiving keys now; logical focus is the element each focus scope remembers.
  • A Focus() call that does nothing is almost always made too early; defer it with the dispatcher.
  • Use TabNavigation, IsDefault and IsCancel to make forms work from the keyboard.
  • Filter typed characters with PreviewTextInput, not KeyDown.
  • A panel needs a Transparent background, not none, to receive clicks on its empty area.
  • Capture the mouse while dragging and always reset your state when capture is lost.
PART 24

Popups, menus, tooltips and adorners

Dropdowns, flyouts, context menus and tooltips all float above the window. Most are built on one primitive, Popup, and most of their surprises come from the fact that each one lives in a window of its own. Adorners are the other way to draw on top: inside the same window, in a layer above the content.

Builds on Part 6, routed events and commands, Part 9, styles and triggers and Part 11, data binding.

Popup: the primitive under everything floating

A filter dropdown near the bottom of a small window is useless if it is cut off at the window's edge. WPF avoids that by giving every popup its own operating-system window.

Popup creates a separate top-level HWND, the Win32 handle of a real window. That is why it can extend past the window edge. It is also why it ignores window clipping, and stays put when the window moves. Its DataContext can also fail to reach it in some situations: when the popup is not in the element tree, because it was created in code or set as a property value. The gotcha below covers the fix.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A popup is a sticky note stuck on the glass of your monitor rather than something drawn on the page underneath. Because it is on the glass, it can hang over the edge of the window and nothing on the page clips it. It is also why dragging the window away leaves the note where it was: it was never attached to the page.

The example is a Filters toggle button that opens a small panel of check boxes. IsOpen is bound two-way to the button's IsChecked, so clicking the button opens the popup, and a click outside that closes it also unchecks the button.

XAML 
<ToggleButton x:Name="Toggle" Content="Filters" />

<Popup IsOpen="{Binding IsChecked, ElementName=Toggle, Mode=TwoWay}"
       PlacementTarget="{Binding ElementName=Toggle}"
       Placement="Bottom"
       HorizontalOffset="0" VerticalOffset="4"
       StaysOpen="False"
       AllowsTransparency="True"
       PopupAnimation="Fade"
       Focusable="False">
  <Border Background="{DynamicResource SurfaceBrush}"
          BorderBrush="{DynamicResource BorderBrush}" BorderThickness="1"
          CornerRadius="6" Padding="12"
          Effect="{StaticResource PopupShadow}">
    <StackPanel>
      <CheckBox Content="Only open" IsChecked="{Binding OnlyOpen}" />
      <CheckBox Content="Assigned to me" IsChecked="{Binding Mine}" />
    </StackPanel>
  </Border>
</Popup>
PropertyWhy it matters
StaysOpen="False"Closes on an outside click. Without it the popup is sticky and users hate it.
AllowsTransparency="True"Required for rounded corners, shadows, or any non-rectangular shape.
PlacementBottom, Right, Mouse, Relative, Custom. Custom takes a CustomPopupPlacementCallback for flip-and-fit logic.
Focusable="False"Keeps keyboard focus on the opener, which is usually what a filter or picker flyout wants.
GOTCHA

A Popup is not a visual child of the element you declared it under, so it inherits DataContext through the logical tree only. If your bindings inside a popup resolve to nothing, set the DataContext explicitly: DataContext="{Binding PlacementTarget.DataContext, RelativeSource={RelativeSource Self}}". Popups also stay behind when the window moves, in every placement mode, because moving a window triggers no layout pass. Close the popup when the window moves, or in the window's LocationChanged handler change HorizontalOffset and set it back, which forces the popup to reposition.

Context menus

A right-click menu on each row of a list, with Open and Delete running commands on your view model, is one of the most common requests in WPF, and one of the most common binding failures.

A ContextMenu is shown in a popup, so it is not part of the visual tree of the element you right-clicked. Bindings that search upward for an ancestor, or name an element elsewhere in the window, find nothing. The menu does receive the clicked element's DataContext, so a plain {Binding} inside it sees the item. Its one link back to the window is its PlacementTarget: the element it was opened for.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Most bindings find their data by walking up the tree of elements, from child to parent, until they reach what they need. A context menu sits in its own small tree, so that walk stops at the menu. WPF does hand the menu the clicked item's data, so a plain binding to the item still works. Anything beyond the item, such as the list's view model, has to be fetched across the one link back, PlacementTarget.

Here the menu is set per item through the item container style, and the commands live on the list's view model, not on each item. The style first copies the ListBox's DataContext into each item's Tag. Inside the menu, the RelativeSource finds the ContextMenu, and PlacementTarget.Tag steps across to the clicked ListBoxItem and reads that view model. CommandParameter="{Binding}" needs no hop: the menu already has the item as its data.

XAML 
<ListBox ItemsSource="{Binding Items}">
  <ListBox.ItemContainerStyle>
    <Style TargetType="ListBoxItem">
      <Setter Property="Tag"
              Value="{Binding DataContext, RelativeSource={RelativeSource AncestorType=ListBox}}" />
      <Setter Property="ContextMenu">
        <Setter.Value>
          <ContextMenu>
            <MenuItem Header="_Open"
                      Command="{Binding PlacementTarget.Tag.OpenCommand,
                                        RelativeSource={RelativeSource AncestorType=ContextMenu}}"
                      CommandParameter="{Binding}" />
            <Separator />
            <MenuItem Header="_Delete"
                      Command="{Binding PlacementTarget.Tag.DeleteCommand,
                                        RelativeSource={RelativeSource AncestorType=ContextMenu}}"
                      CommandParameter="{Binding}"
                      InputGestureText="Del" />
          </ContextMenu>
        </Setter.Value>
      </Setter>
    </Style>
  </ListBox.ItemContainerStyle>
</ListBox>

The PlacementTarget hop is the standard escape from a context menu's separate visual tree. Tag is a spare property every element has, which makes it a convenient carrier: here it holds the ListBox's DataContext, and the menu reaches it through PlacementTarget.Tag.

InputGestureText is display-only. It prints the shortcut in the menu but does not bind it; you still need an InputBinding.

Menus driven by data DEEPER

Plugin systems, recent-file lists and user-configurable toolbars need menus built from data rather than written in XAML. You bind ItemsSource to a list of menu view models, each with a header, children and a command, and map those onto the generated MenuItems with a style. A trigger turns items flagged as separators into a plain line.

XAML 
<Menu ItemsSource="{Binding MenuItems}">
  <Menu.ItemContainerStyle>
    <Style TargetType="MenuItem">
      <Setter Property="Header"           Value="{Binding Header}" />
      <Setter Property="ItemsSource"      Value="{Binding Children}" />
      <Setter Property="Command"          Value="{Binding Command}" />
      <Setter Property="CommandParameter" Value="{Binding Parameter}" />
      <Setter Property="IsCheckable"      Value="{Binding IsCheckable}" />
      <Setter Property="IsChecked"        Value="{Binding IsChecked, Mode=TwoWay}" />
      <Style.Triggers>
        <DataTrigger Binding="{Binding IsSeparator}" Value="True">
          <Setter Property="Template">
            <Setter.Value>
              <ControlTemplate><Separator /></ControlTemplate>
            </Setter.Value>
          </Setter>
        </DataTrigger>
      </Style.Triggers>
    </Style>
  </Menu.ItemContainerStyle>
</Menu>

Using ItemContainerStyle rather than ItemTemplate is deliberate here. For each data item, the menu generates a MenuItem as its container, the element that wraps the item. The style sets properties on that container; a template would only fill its header. A MenuItem must be the container for submenus and keyboard navigation to work. Templating the content instead produces a menu item containing a menu item, which behaves oddly.

Tooltips

A tooltip that appears too slowly, vanishes while someone is still reading, or never shows on a disabled button is worse than none. The ToolTipService attached properties tune all of that per element, and the tooltip itself can hold any content, not only a string.

XAML 
<Button Content="Export"
        ToolTipService.InitialShowDelay="400"
        ToolTipService.ShowDuration="20000"
        ToolTipService.BetweenShowDelay="0"
        ToolTipService.ShowOnDisabled="True"
        ToolTipService.Placement="Bottom">
  <Button.ToolTip>
    <ToolTip>
      <StackPanel MaxWidth="260">
        <TextBlock Text="Export to CSV" FontWeight="SemiBold" />
        <TextBlock TextWrapping="Wrap" Opacity=".8"
                   Text="Writes the current filtered view, not the whole table." />
      </StackPanel>
    </ToolTip>
  </Button.ToolTip>
</Button>

ShowOnDisabled="True" is the useful one: a disabled button that explains why it's disabled is worth a dozen support tickets.

Adorners

Sometimes you need to draw on top of a control without moving anything around it: a red border on a field with an error, a count badge on an icon, resize handles on a selected shape. Putting those in the layout would push neighbouring controls aside.

An adorner is a visual drawn in a separate layer on top of its adorned element. It does not participate in layout, so it can overflow without changing anything. Validation error visuals, drag ghosts, resize handles and focus rectangles are all adorners. The layer that holds them is the adorner layer, supplied by an AdornerDecorator somewhere above the element.

IN PLAIN TERMS

An adorner layer is a sheet of tracing paper laid over a form. You can draw a red badge that pokes past the edge of a text box, and the form underneath does not shift at all. The analogy has one catch: the tracing paper covers everything under that decorator, so what you draw sits above every control there, not just the one you meant to mark.

The adorner below draws a red badge with a label at the top-right corner of its element. OnRender is called with a drawing context, and IsHitTestVisible = false lets clicks pass through the badge to the control.

C# 
public sealed class BadgeAdorner : Adorner
{
    private readonly string _text;
    private readonly Brush _fill = Brushes.Firebrick;
    private readonly Typeface _face = new("Segoe UI");

    public BadgeAdorner(UIElement adorned, string text) : base(adorned)
    {
        _text = text;
        IsHitTestVisible = false;    // let clicks through to the control underneath
    }

    protected override void OnRender(DrawingContext dc)
    {
        var size = AdornedElement.RenderSize;

        var text = new FormattedText(_text, CultureInfo.CurrentUICulture,
            FlowDirection.LeftToRight, _face, 10, Brushes.White,
            VisualTreeHelper.GetDpi(this).PixelsPerDip);

        double r = Math.Max(8, text.Width / 2 + 6);
        var centre = new Point(size.Width - 2, 2);

        dc.DrawEllipse(_fill, null, centre, r, 8);
        dc.DrawText(text, new Point(centre.X - text.Width / 2, centre.Y - text.Height / 2));
    }
}

To show it, find the element's adorner layer, remove any badge already there so they do not stack, and add a new one.

C#: attaching and removing 
public static void ShowBadge(UIElement element, string text)
{
    var layer = AdornerLayer.GetAdornerLayer(element);
    if (layer is null) return;                       // no AdornerDecorator in the tree

    foreach (var existing in layer.GetAdorners(element)?.OfType<BadgeAdorner>() ?? [])
        layer.Remove(existing);

    layer.Add(new BadgeAdorner(element, text));
}
GOTCHA

AdornerLayer.GetAdornerLayer returns null when there is no AdornerDecorator above the element. A Window's default template has one; Popup, ToolTip, ContextMenu and custom window templates often do not. If adorners vanish inside a flyout, wrap its content in <AdornerDecorator>. Adorners also do not clip to the adorned element and are rendered above everything in that layer, including content you'd expect to be on top.

RECAP
  • A Popup is its own window, so it can overflow the main window but does not move with it.
  • Set StaysOpen="False" so a popup closes when the user clicks elsewhere.
  • Inside a context menu, a plain binding sees the clicked item; anything beyond it, such as the list's view model, goes through PlacementTarget.
  • InputGestureText only displays a shortcut; an InputBinding makes it work.
  • Set ToolTipService.ShowOnDisabled so disabled controls can explain themselves.
  • Adorners draw above an element without affecting layout, and need an AdornerDecorator above them.
PART 25

Drag and drop, and the clipboard

Dragging a task to reorder a list, dropping files from Explorer onto a window, copying rows to paste into Excel: all three move data through the same Windows machinery. This part builds the drag source, the drop target and the clipboard code, and covers what .NET 9 changed.

Builds on Part 7, the control content model (the TreeSearch helper), Part 23, input, focus and keyboard and Part 24, adorners.

A drag has two sides. The drag source is the element the user starts dragging from; it packages the data. The drop target is the element it lands on; it decides whether it accepts the data and what happens. Between them travels a DataObject, which holds the same data in one or more data formats, each identified by a name. The clipboard uses exactly the same DataObject.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A DataObject is a parcel with several labelled envelopes inside, each holding the same content in a different form: your own object for your app, plain text for Notepad, CSV for Excel. The receiver opens the envelope whose label it understands and ignores the rest. If none of the labels are ones it knows, it has nothing to take.

The drag source

Starting a drag correctly takes three steps. Remember where the button went down. On each mouse move, wait until the pointer has travelled far enough to count as a drag rather than a shaky click. Then package the data and call DragDrop.DoDragDrop.

The press is recorded in PreviewMouseLeftButtonDown because the ListBoxItem handles the ordinary button-down event for selection, so the bubbling version would never reach this handler. The code finds the item under the pointer with the TreeSearch helper and puts two formats in the parcel: the view model itself, and its title as text.

C# 
private Point _dragStart;

private void OnPreviewMouseLeftButtonDown(object sender, MouseButtonEventArgs e)
    => _dragStart = e.GetPosition(null);

private void OnMouseMove(object sender, MouseEventArgs e)
{
    if (e.LeftButton != MouseButtonState.Pressed) return;

    Vector moved = _dragStart - e.GetPosition(null);
    if (Math.Abs(moved.X) < SystemParameters.MinimumHorizontalDragDistance &&
        Math.Abs(moved.Y) < SystemParameters.MinimumVerticalDragDistance) return;

    if (((DependencyObject)e.OriginalSource) is not DependencyObject src) return;
    var container = TreeSearch.FindAncestor<ListBoxItem>(src);
    if (container?.DataContext is not TaskViewModel task) return;

    var data = new DataObject();
    data.SetData(typeof(TaskViewModel).FullName!, task);   // in-process, rich
    data.SetText(task.Title);                              // for other apps

    // Blocks until the drop completes or is cancelled.
    DragDrop.DoDragDrop(container, data, DragDropEffects.Move | DragDropEffects.Copy);
}

Honouring SystemParameters.MinimumHorizontalDragDistance is not optional polish. Without it, every click that wobbles by a pixel starts a drag, and selection becomes impossible.

The drop target

An element only takes part in drops once AllowDrop="True" is set. While something is dragged over it, DragOver fires repeatedly, and your answer each time decides the cursor the user sees. Drop fires once, when they let go.

XAML 
<ListBox AllowDrop="True"
         DragOver="OnDragOver"
         DragLeave="OnDragLeave"
         Drop="OnDrop" />

The answer is expressed as effects: Move, Copy, Link or None. The source said which it allows; in DragOver the target sets e.Effects to the one it would perform here. In the handler below, one of our own tasks is moved, or copied when Ctrl is held; files from Explorer are copied; anything else is refused. TaskFormat is the same string the source used, typeof(TaskViewModel).FullName, written out.

C# 
private const string TaskFormat = "Tasks.ViewModels.TaskViewModel";

private void OnDragOver(object sender, DragEventArgs e)
{
    bool ours  = e.Data.GetDataPresent(TaskFormat);
    bool files = e.Data.GetDataPresent(DataFormats.FileDrop);

    e.Effects = (ours, files) switch
    {
        (true, _) => (e.KeyStates & DragDropKeyStates.ControlKey) != 0
                        ? DragDropEffects.Copy : DragDropEffects.Move,
        (_, true) => DragDropEffects.Copy,
        _          => DragDropEffects.None,
    };
    e.Handled = true;       // required, or the parent decides for you
}

private void OnDrop(object sender, DragEventArgs e)
{
    if (e.Data.GetDataPresent(TaskFormat) &&
        e.Data.GetData(TaskFormat) is TaskViewModel task)
    {
        int index = InsertionIndex((ListBox)sender, e.GetPosition((IInputElement)sender));
        _viewModel.MoveTo(task, index);
    }
    else if (e.Data.GetDataPresent(DataFormats.FileDrop) &&
             e.Data.GetData(DataFormats.FileDrop) is string[] paths)
    {
        _viewModel.ImportCommand.Execute(paths);
    }
    e.Handled = true;
}
GOTCHA

Setting e.Effects without e.Handled = true lets an ancestor overwrite it, and you get the "no entry" cursor with no explanation.

DragDrop.DoDragDrop also runs a nested message loop: it keeps the UI responsive while the drag is in progress, but the code after the call does not execute until the drop finishes, and await inside a drag handler behaves unintuitively. Do the work in Drop, not around DoDragDrop.

An insertion indicator DEEPER

When reordering a list, users need to see where the item will land. First work out the index: walk the item containers and return the first one whose vertical midpoint is below the pointer. Items scrolled out of view in a virtualised list have no container, so they are skipped.

C# 
private static int InsertionIndex(ListBox list, Point position)
{
    for (int i = 0; i < list.Items.Count; i++)
    {
        if (list.ItemContainerGenerator.ContainerFromIndex(i) is not FrameworkElement c)
            continue;
        Point topLeft = c.TranslatePoint(new Point(0, 0), list);
        if (position.Y < topLeft.Y + c.ActualHeight / 2) return i;
    }
    return list.Items.Count;
}

Draw the line itself with an adorner (Part 24) added on DragOver and removed on DragLeave and Drop. Remember that DragLeave fires when the pointer crosses into a child element too, so debounce it or check e.GetPosition against the bounds.

The clipboard, and what .NET 9 changed

Copy and paste use the same DataObject as drag and drop, placed on the system clipboard instead of carried by the pointer. The part that has changed in recent .NET versions is what you may put in it.

IN PLAIN TERMS

The clipboard can only hold bytes, so any object you put on it has to be turned into bytes and back again. WPF used to do that silently for any serialisable object, using BinaryFormatter. .NET removed BinaryFormatter because reading those bytes back can be made to run code an attacker chose. Converting your data to JSON yourself keeps that step in your hands, and a string is something every process can read.

SINCE .NET 9 / .NET 10

.NET 9 removed the BinaryFormatter implementation. WPF used it internally for clipboard and drag-drop of arbitrary objects, so Clipboard.SetData(format, someCustomObject) with a non-primitive type now throws PlatformNotSupportedException unless the type is in WPF's known-safe set.

.NET 10 goes further: it unifies the WPF and WinForms clipboard implementations, obsoletes the methods that depended on binary serialisation, and adds JSON-based alternatives. If you are on .NET 9, serialise custom payloads yourself; on .NET 10 you can use the new typed and JSON methods.

The class below works on both. Copy puts three formats on the clipboard: the tasks as JSON under a private format name, their titles as plain text, and CSV. The .v1 in the format name leaves room to change the payload shape later without older builds misreading it. Paste reads only the private format and returns an empty list when it is absent.

C#: portable across .NET 9 and 10 
public static class TaskClipboard
{
    private const string Format = "Tasks.TaskPayload.v1";

    public static void Copy(IReadOnlyList<TaskViewModel> tasks)
    {
        var payload = JsonSerializer.Serialize(tasks.Select(t => t.ToDto()));

        var data = new DataObject();
        data.SetData(Format, payload);                            // a string: always safe
        data.SetText(string.Join(Environment.NewLine,
                     tasks.Select(t => t.Title)));                // for Notepad, Excel…
        data.SetData(DataFormats.CommaSeparatedValue, ToCsv(tasks));

        Clipboard.SetDataObject(data, copy: true);   // copy:true survives app exit
    }

    public static IReadOnlyList<TaskDto> Paste()
    {
        if (!Clipboard.ContainsData(Format)) return [];
        if (Clipboard.GetData(Format) is not string json) return [];
        return JsonSerializer.Deserialize<List<TaskDto>>(json) ?? [];
    }
}

The rule that survives every framework version: put a string on the clipboard, not an object graph. JSON in a private format name, plus text and CSV fallbacks for other applications, works on every .NET and does not depend on the receiving process having your types loaded.

Images and file lists have their own helpers, and the file list is what Explorer understands when the user pastes.

C#: images and files 
Clipboard.SetImage(bitmapSource);
BitmapSource? pasted = Clipboard.ContainsImage() ? Clipboard.GetImage() : null;

var files = new StringCollection();
files.Add(@"C:\reports\q3.pdf");
Clipboard.SetFileDropList(files);
GOTCHA

The clipboard is a shared OS resource and another process can hold it open. Clipboard.SetDataObject throws COMException (CLIPBRD_E_CANT_OPEN) intermittently, especially with remote-desktop clipboard redirection running. WPF already retries for about a second inside SetDataObject, so an exception means the clipboard stayed locked beyond that. Wrap clipboard access in your own retry with a short delay. WPF's Clipboard has no retry overload of its own: SetDataObject(data, copy, retryTimes, retryDelay) exists only in Windows Forms.

RECAP
  • A DataObject carries the same data in several named formats, and the receiver takes the one it understands.
  • Only start a drag once the pointer has moved past the system drag distance.
  • In DragOver, set e.Effects and e.Handled = true, or a parent element can override your effect and show the "no entry" cursor.
  • DoDragDrop does not return until the drop is over, so do the work in Drop.
  • Put strings on the clipboard, such as JSON under a private format plus plain text, never custom objects.
  • Clipboard calls can fail because another process holds the clipboard, so retry them.
PART 26

DataGrid in depth

The heaviest control in the box and the one most business applications are built on. Worth knowing properly.

Builds on Part 11, data binding, Part 9, styles and triggers, Part 13, validation and Part 19, performance.

IN PLAIN TERMS

A DataGrid looks like a spreadsheet, but it owns no data. Each row is one of your objects and each column is a view onto one property of that object. When the user finishes editing a cell, the value goes into that property through a binding, and when your code changes the property, the cell updates.

Columns

Left alone, a DataGrid generates one column per public property of your row type, with the property name as the header. That is fine for a quick look at data and wrong for anything a person will use: you cannot choose the order, the widths, the formatting or which properties appear at all. So real screens set AutoGenerateColumns="False" and declare every column.

There are five column types. DataGridTextColumn shows text, DataGridCheckBoxColumn a tick box, DataGridComboBoxColumn a pick list, DataGridHyperlinkColumn a clickable link, and DataGridTemplateColumn anything you can put in a DataTemplate. Column widths take a fixed number (in device-independent pixels), Auto, SizeToCells, SizeToHeader, or a star size such as 2*, which means a proportional share of whatever width is left over.

Each cell has two faces. It shows a display element while the user is just looking (a TextBlock for a text column) and swaps in an editing element once they start editing (a TextBox). ElementStyle styles the first and EditingElementStyle the second.

The grid below declares five columns. Notice the settings on the grid itself: SelectionMode="Extended" allows several rows to be selected with Ctrl and Shift, FrozenColumnCount="1" keeps the first column in place when the user scrolls sideways, and the virtualisation settings are covered under performance below. In the columns, notice the red overdue date done with a DataTrigger in ElementStyle, and the delete button, whose command binding has to climb to the DataGrid because each row's DataContext is the row object, not the screen's view model.

XAML 
<DataGrid ItemsSource="{Binding Rows}"
          AutoGenerateColumns="False"
          CanUserAddRows="False"
          CanUserDeleteRows="False"
          CanUserReorderColumns="True"
          SelectionMode="Extended"
          SelectionUnit="FullRow"
          GridLinesVisibility="Horizontal"
          HeadersVisibility="Column"
          EnableRowVirtualization="True"
          EnableColumnVirtualization="False"
          VirtualizingPanel.VirtualizationMode="Recycling"
          RowHeight="26"
          FrozenColumnCount="1">

  <DataGrid.Columns>
    <DataGridTextColumn Header="Title" Width="2*"
                        Binding="{Binding Title}"
                        SortMemberPath="Title" />

    <DataGridTextColumn Header="Due" Width="110"
                        Binding="{Binding DueDate, StringFormat=yyyy-MM-dd}">
      <DataGridTextColumn.ElementStyle>
        <Style TargetType="TextBlock">
          <Setter Property="HorizontalAlignment" Value="Right" />
          <Style.Triggers>
            <DataTrigger Binding="{Binding IsOverdue}" Value="True">
              <Setter Property="Foreground" Value="{DynamicResource DangerBrush}" />
            </DataTrigger>
          </Style.Triggers>
        </Style>
      </DataGridTextColumn.ElementStyle>
    </DataGridTextColumn>

    <DataGridCheckBoxColumn Header="Done" Width="60"
                            Binding="{Binding IsDone, UpdateSourceTrigger=PropertyChanged}" />

    <DataGridComboBoxColumn Header="Assignee" Width="150"
        SelectedValueBinding="{Binding AssigneeId}"
        SelectedValuePath="Id"
        DisplayMemberPath="Name">
      <DataGridComboBoxColumn.ElementStyle>
        <Style TargetType="ComboBox">
          <Setter Property="ItemsSource"
                  Value="{Binding DataContext.People,
                                  RelativeSource={RelativeSource AncestorType=DataGrid}}" />
        </Style>
      </DataGridComboBoxColumn.ElementStyle>
      <DataGridComboBoxColumn.EditingElementStyle>
        <Style TargetType="ComboBox">
          <Setter Property="ItemsSource"
                  Value="{Binding DataContext.People,
                                  RelativeSource={RelativeSource AncestorType=DataGrid}}" />
        </Style>
      </DataGridComboBoxColumn.EditingElementStyle>
    </DataGridComboBoxColumn>

    <DataGridTemplateColumn Header="" Width="40">
      <DataGridTemplateColumn.CellTemplate>
        <DataTemplate>
          <Button Content="×" Width="20" Height="20"
                  Command="{Binding DataContext.DeleteCommand,
                                    RelativeSource={RelativeSource AncestorType=DataGrid}}"
                  CommandParameter="{Binding}" />
        </DataTemplate>
      </DataGridTemplateColumn.CellTemplate>
    </DataGridTemplateColumn>
  </DataGrid.Columns>
</DataGrid>
GOTCHA

DataGridComboBoxColumn is the single most frustrating class in WPF. A column is a recipe, not something on screen: the grid reads it once per row to build that row's cell. The column itself is not part of the visual tree, so it has no parent to inherit the DataGrid's DataContext from. A binding on one of the column's own properties, such as ItemsSource or Header, therefore has nothing to bind against. Binding and SelectedValueBinding are different, because the column passes them to each cell, where the row object is the DataContext.

That is why the ItemsSource has to be pushed in through both ElementStyle and EditingElementStyle: those styles land on the ComboBox generated in each cell, which is on screen and can walk up the tree to the DataGrid. The alternative that many teams settle on is a DataGridTemplateColumn containing a plain ComboBox, which behaves like everything else.

Selection from a view model

Your view model often needs to know which rows the user picked, for example to delete them or to enable a toolbar button. A single selection is easy: SelectedItem is a dependency property and binds like any other. Multiple selection is the problem. SelectedItems is not a dependency property, so it cannot be bound. Three options:

  1. Put an IsSelected property on the row view model and bind it in RowStyle. Cleanest, and it survives virtualisation.
  2. A behaviour that syncs SelectedItems to a collection.
  3. SelectionChanged in code-behind pushing into the view model. Works, but it's the one that gets copied everywhere.

Option 1 uses RowStyle, a style the grid applies to every row container: the DataGridRow element it creates to host each of your objects. The setter binds the row's own IsSelected to the property on your object in both directions, so selecting a row sets the property and setting the property selects the row. The same style shows the other common use of RowStyle, colouring a whole row from a flag on the data.

XAML: option 1 
<DataGrid.RowStyle>
  <Style TargetType="DataGridRow">
    <Setter Property="IsSelected" Value="{Binding IsSelected, Mode=TwoWay}" />
    <Style.Triggers>
      <DataTrigger Binding="{Binding HasConflict}" Value="True">
        <Setter Property="Background" Value="{DynamicResource WarnSurfaceBrush}" />
      </DataTrigger>
    </Style.Triggers>
  </Style>
</DataGrid.RowStyle>

It survives virtualisation because the selection is stored on your objects. Row containers are thrown away or reused as the user scrolls, but when a row comes back into view the binding reads the flag again.

Editing, commit and validation

Picture a user who edits the title, the due date and the assignee of a task, then realises it was the wrong row. They expect to back out of all three changes at once. The grid supports this with a two-level commit model: an edit is committed first at cell level (the value is written to your property when the user leaves the cell) and then at row level (the row edit ends when the user leaves the row).

Your row type can take part in the row level by implementing IEditableObject. That gives you transactional row editing for free:

IN PLAIN TERMS

IEditableObject is the grid telling your object when to take a photocopy. BeginEdit means "the user started changing this row, keep a copy". CancelEdit means "put the copy back". EndEdit means "the changes stand, throw the copy away". The grid only says when; making and restoring the copy is your code, and your properties really do change during the edit, so anything watching them sees the in-between values.

In the sketch below, Snapshot, Restore and TaskSnapshot are your own helpers, not shown. The ??= keeps the first copy if BeginEdit is called again before the row edit ends.

C# 
public partial class TaskRowViewModel : ObservableValidator, IEditableObject
{
    private TaskSnapshot? _backup;

    public void BeginEdit()   => _backup ??= Snapshot();
    public void CancelEdit()  { if (_backup is { } b) { Restore(b); _backup = null; } }
    public void EndEdit()     => _backup = null;
}

Escape once cancels the cell; Escape twice cancels the row and calls CancelEdit.

Some rules span several cells, such as "the due date must not be before the start date". Checking each cell alone cannot catch that. To validate the whole row, use a DataGrid.RowValidationRules entry: a ValidationRule of your own (see Part 13). Give it ValidationStep="UpdatedValue", which runs the rule after the new values have been written to your object, so the rule can look at the row as a whole:

XAML 
<DataGrid.RowValidationRules>
  <rules:TaskRowRule ValidationStep="UpdatedValue" />
</DataGrid.RowValidationRules>

<DataGrid.RowValidationErrorTemplate>
  <ControlTemplate>
    <Grid Margin="0,-2,0,-2" ToolTip="{Binding RelativeSource={RelativeSource
              AncestorType=DataGridRow}, Path=(Validation.Errors)[0].ErrorContent}">
      <Ellipse Width="12" Height="12" Fill="{DynamicResource DangerBrush}" />
      <TextBlock Text="!" Foreground="White" FontWeight="Bold" FontSize="9"
                 HorizontalAlignment="Center" VerticalAlignment="Center" />
    </Grid>
  </ControlTemplate>
</DataGrid.RowValidationErrorTemplate>

RowValidationErrorTemplate decides what a failed row looks like. This one draws a small red circle with an exclamation mark and puts the first error message in its tooltip.

Performance rules for large grids

IN PLAIN TERMS

A grid bound to 10,000 rows stays fast only because it builds rows for what is on screen, perhaps 30 of them, and reuses those as the user scrolls. That is virtualisation, covered in Part 19. Almost every rule below avoids something that makes the grid look at every row, which quietly throws the saving away.

  • Set an explicit RowHeight. Without it, the grid measures every row to find the tallest, defeating virtualisation on first layout.
  • Avoid Width="Auto" and SizeToCells columns. They force every row to be measured. Use * and fixed widths.
  • Leave EnableColumnVirtualization="False" unless you genuinely have 50+ columns; it costs more than it saves below that.
  • Keep CellStyle and templates shallow. Every extra Border is multiplied by rows × columns.
  • Do not bind ItemsSource to a LINQ query. A query is not a list, so it re-enumerates: every time the grid walks it, the query runs again. Materialise to a list first.
  • Sort on the server if the data lives there. Keep CanUserSortColumns on and handle Sorting yourself with e.Handled = true, as below.

Sorting on the server DEEPER

When the grid shows one page of a table with two million rows, its built-in sort would only reorder the page it holds. Clicking a header should instead ask the database for a new page in the new order.

The Sorting event fires when the user clicks a header, before the grid sorts. Setting e.Handled = true stops the built-in sort. Your handler then flips the column's SortDirection itself, so the arrow in the header still shows, clears the arrow from every other column, and asks the view model to reload. The event only fires while CanUserSortColumns (and the column's own CanUserSort) is true; turn it off and header clicks do nothing at all.

C#: server-side sorting 
private async void OnSorting(object sender, DataGridSortingEventArgs e)
{
    e.Handled = true;
    var column = e.Column;

    column.SortDirection = column.SortDirection == ListSortDirection.Ascending
        ? ListSortDirection.Descending
        : ListSortDirection.Ascending;

    foreach (var c in ((DataGrid)sender).Columns)
        if (c != column) c.SortDirection = null;

    await _viewModel.SortAsync(column.SortMemberPath, column.SortDirection.Value);
}
RECAP
  • Turn off automatic columns and declare each column, so you control order, width and formatting.
  • A column is not on screen, so a binding on its own properties, such as ItemsSource, cannot see the grid's data context, while Binding and SelectedValueBinding work because each cell receives them.
  • Bind multiple selection through an IsSelected flag on each row object, set up in RowStyle.
  • Edits commit per cell and then per row, and IEditableObject lets a row be cancelled as a whole.
  • Large grids stay fast only while virtualisation works: fixed row heights, no auto-sized columns, shallow templates.
PART 27

Authoring custom controls

Sooner or later you need a piece of UI that WPF does not ship: a search box with a placeholder, a count badge, a text box that only accepts numbers. There are three ways to build one, and picking the wrong one costs either flexibility or days.

Builds on Part 5, dependency properties, Part 9, styles and triggers, Part 10, templates and Part 11, data binding.

UserControl or Control: the decision

IN PLAIN TERMS

Every built-in Button is lookless. Its class knows how to be pressed, focused and clicked, but it contains no drawing at all: its appearance comes from a template that a theme, or you, can replace wholesale. A UserControl is the opposite. It is a fixed arrangement of existing controls that you lay out once, like a pre-printed form: people can fill it in, but not redesign it.

Precisely: a UserControl is a composite whose visual tree you author in XAML alongside its code. A lookless control derives from Control and holds only behaviour and properties; its whole look is a ControlTemplate that consumers can swap out.

UserControlControl (lookless)
Defined byA fixed XAML tree you authorA template consumers can replace
RestyleableNo, not meaningfullyYes, completely
EffortMinutesHours
Right forA screen, a panel, a composite specific to one appA control shipped to other teams or reused across apps

The honest default is UserControl. Most "custom controls" in line-of-business apps are screen fragments that will never be restyled, and lookless controls cost real time. Reach for Control when you are building a library. A third route, deriving from an existing control, comes at the end of this part and is often the cheapest of all.

A UserControl with real dependency properties

A UserControl is only reusable if the screens that host it can pass data in and get data out with bindings, as in <local:SearchBox Query="{Binding Filter}" />. A binding can only target a dependency property, so an ordinary C# property on the control will not do. Each value the host should be able to bind needs a registered dependency property with a plain wrapper around it.

The code-behind below declares three. Query is registered with BindsTwoWayByDefault, so a host binding pushes the user's typing back without writing Mode=TwoWay, the same way TextBox.Text behaves. Placeholder has a default of "Search…". SearchCommand lets the host hand in a command; wiring it to a key or button is left out here.

SearchBox.xaml.cs 
public partial class SearchBox : UserControl
{
    public SearchBox() => InitializeComponent();

    public static readonly DependencyProperty QueryProperty =
        DependencyProperty.Register(
            nameof(Query), typeof(string), typeof(SearchBox),
            new FrameworkPropertyMetadata(
                defaultValue: string.Empty,
                flags: FrameworkPropertyMetadataOptions.BindsTwoWayByDefault));

    public string Query
    {
        get => (string)GetValue(QueryProperty);
        set => SetValue(QueryProperty, value);
    }

    public static readonly DependencyProperty PlaceholderProperty =
        DependencyProperty.Register(nameof(Placeholder), typeof(string), typeof(SearchBox),
            new PropertyMetadata("Search…"));

    public string Placeholder
    {
        get => (string)GetValue(PlaceholderProperty);
        set => SetValue(PlaceholderProperty, value);
    }

    public static readonly DependencyProperty SearchCommandProperty =
        DependencyProperty.Register(nameof(SearchCommand), typeof(ICommand), typeof(SearchBox),
            new PropertyMetadata(null));

    public ICommand? SearchCommand
    {
        get => (ICommand?)GetValue(SearchCommandProperty);
        set => SetValue(SearchCommandProperty, value);
    }
}

The XAML names its root Root so inner elements can bind to the control's own properties with ElementName=Root. Notice three details. Delay=250 waits a quarter of a second after the user stops typing before it updates Query, so a filter does not rerun on every keystroke. The placeholder TextBlock sits on top of the TextBox with IsHitTestVisible="False", so clicks pass through to the box underneath. And a DataTrigger shows the placeholder only while the box is empty.

SearchBox.xaml 
<UserControl x:Class="Tasks.Controls.SearchBox"
             xmlns="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml/presentation"
             xmlns:x="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml"
             x:Name="Root">
  <Grid>
    <TextBox x:Name="Input"
             Text="{Binding Query, ElementName=Root,
                            UpdateSourceTrigger=PropertyChanged, Delay=250}" />
    <TextBlock Text="{Binding Placeholder, ElementName=Root}"
               Margin="6,0,0,0" VerticalAlignment="Center" IsHitTestVisible="False"
               Foreground="{DynamicResource MutedBrush}">
      <TextBlock.Style>
        <Style TargetType="TextBlock">
          <Setter Property="Visibility" Value="Collapsed" />
          <Style.Triggers>
            <DataTrigger Binding="{Binding Text, ElementName=Input}" Value="">
              <Setter Property="Visibility" Value="Visible" />
            </DataTrigger>
          </Style.Triggers>
        </Style>
      </TextBlock.Style>
    </TextBlock>
  </Grid>
</UserControl>
IN PLAIN TERMS

DataContext is passed down the element tree like a family's home address: every child uses its parent's unless it is given its own. The binding a host writes on your control's tag is evaluated at your control's address. If your control sets its own DataContext, it has moved house, and {Binding Filter} now looks for Filter in your internal object instead of in the host's view model.

GOTCHA

Never set DataContext on a UserControl's root. It breaks the consumer's inherited context, so <local:SearchBox Query="{Binding Filter}" /> would bind against your internal object instead of theirs. Bind to your own properties with ElementName pointing at a named root, as above, or RelativeSource AncestorType=UserControl.

A lookless control

Write a lookless control when the same behaviour must look different in different places: another team's brand, a dark theme, a compact variant. The example is a Badge that shows a count and fades out at zero.

Three pieces: the class, a default style in Themes/Generic.xaml, and an assembly-level ThemeInfo.

ThemeInfo tells WPF where to look for a control's default style. The first argument covers dictionaries per Windows theme, which almost nobody writes any more, so it is None. The second says the generic dictionary, the fallback used for every theme, lives in this assembly. WPF finds it by a fixed path, so the file must be called Themes/Generic.xaml exactly.

AssemblyInfo.cs 
[assembly: ThemeInfo(
    ResourceDictionaryLocation.None,            // theme-specific dictionaries
    ResourceDictionaryLocation.SourceAssembly)] // generic dictionary

In the class, notice four things:

  • The static constructor overrides DefaultStyleKey with typeof(Badge). That is the key WPF uses to look up the default style. A new control deriving from Control requires this: without it, the control keeps the key it inherits, which is not typeof(Badge) and WPF never finds the style you write in Generic.xaml.
  • TemplatePart and TemplateVisualState are documentation for template authors and design tools: they list the named parts and states a replacement template should provide. WPF does not enforce them.
  • The Count property has a change callback that moves the control to the matching visual state.
  • OnApplyTemplate runs whenever a template is applied, and sets the state again without animation, so a fresh template starts in the right state instead of fading into it.
Badge.cs 
[TemplatePart(Name = PartHost, Type = typeof(FrameworkElement))]
[TemplateVisualState(GroupName = "CountStates", Name = "HasCount")]
[TemplateVisualState(GroupName = "CountStates", Name = "Empty")]
public class Badge : Control
{
    public const string PartHost = "PART_Host";

    static Badge() => DefaultStyleKeyProperty.OverrideMetadata(
        typeof(Badge), new FrameworkPropertyMetadata(typeof(Badge)));

    public static readonly DependencyProperty CountProperty =
        DependencyProperty.Register(nameof(Count), typeof(int), typeof(Badge),
            new FrameworkPropertyMetadata(0, OnCountChanged));

    public int Count
    {
        get => (int)GetValue(CountProperty);
        set => SetValue(CountProperty, value);
    }

    private static void OnCountChanged(DependencyObject d, DependencyPropertyChangedEventArgs e)
        => ((Badge)d).UpdateStates(useTransitions: true);

    public override void OnApplyTemplate()
    {
        base.OnApplyTemplate();
        UpdateStates(useTransitions: false);
    }

    private void UpdateStates(bool useTransitions)
        => VisualStateManager.GoToState(this, Count > 0 ? "HasCount" : "Empty", useTransitions);
}
IN PLAIN TERMS

Visual states are like the labelled positions on a dial. The control's code only ever says "go to HasCount" or "go to Empty". What each position looks like, and how the control moves between them, is written in the template. A different template can make HasCount a bounce instead of a fade, and the C# never changes.

The default style has no x:Key, only a TargetType, which is what makes it the default (theme) style for every Badge. A consumer's own style does not replace it: it applies on top, and anything the consumer's style leaves unset, including the template, still comes from this one. Inside the template, {TemplateBinding} copies the Badge's own Background, Foreground and FontSize onto the template's elements, so a consumer setting Background="Green" on a Badge still works. Count uses a full Binding with RelativeSource TemplatedParent instead, because the number has to become text and the lighter TemplateBinding does not convert types. The VisualStateManager block defines the two states and a 0.15-second transition between them.

Themes/Generic.xaml 
<ResourceDictionary xmlns="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml/presentation"
                    xmlns:x="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml"
                    xmlns:local="clr-namespace:Tasks.Controls">

  <Style TargetType="{x:Type local:Badge}">
    <Setter Property="Background"  Value="#B4352C" />
    <Setter Property="Foreground"  Value="White" />
    <Setter Property="MinWidth"    Value="18" />
    <Setter Property="Height"      Value="18" />
    <Setter Property="FontSize"    Value="10" />

    <Setter Property="Template">
      <Setter.Value>
        <ControlTemplate TargetType="{x:Type local:Badge}">
          <Border x:Name="PART_Host"
                  Background="{TemplateBinding Background}"
                  CornerRadius="9" Padding="5,0">
            <VisualStateManager.VisualStateGroups>
              <VisualStateGroup x:Name="CountStates">
                <VisualStateGroup.Transitions>
                  <VisualTransition GeneratedDuration="0:0:0.15" />
                </VisualStateGroup.Transitions>
                <VisualState x:Name="Empty">
                  <Storyboard>
                    <DoubleAnimation Storyboard.TargetName="PART_Host"
                                     Storyboard.TargetProperty="Opacity" To="0" />
                  </Storyboard>
                </VisualState>
                <VisualState x:Name="HasCount">
                  <Storyboard>
                    <DoubleAnimation Storyboard.TargetName="PART_Host"
                                     Storyboard.TargetProperty="Opacity" To="1" />
                  </Storyboard>
                </VisualState>
              </VisualStateGroup>
            </VisualStateManager.VisualStateGroups>

            <TextBlock Text="{Binding Count, RelativeSource={RelativeSource TemplatedParent}}"
                       Foreground="{TemplateBinding Foreground}"
                       FontSize="{TemplateBinding FontSize}"
                       HorizontalAlignment="Center" VerticalAlignment="Center" />
          </Border>
        </ControlTemplate>
      </Setter.Value>
    </Setter>
  </Style>
</ResourceDictionary>

Triggers or VisualStateManager? DEEPER

Both are in WPF and they do overlapping jobs. Triggers are older, declarative and reversible: when the condition stops being true, the value goes back by itself. The VisualStateManager (VSM) is state-machine shaped, came from Silverlight, and supports transitions between named states.

  • Use triggers for WPF-only controls. Less ceremony, and the value-precedence rules are the ones you already know.
  • Use VSM when states are mutually exclusive and you want animated transitions between them, or when the control will be ported to WinUI/Uno later.

Do not mix them for the same property on the same control. Triggers set values at precedence levels 4 to 7, depending on where they are declared, and VSM animates at level 2 (see the table in Part 5), so VSM always wins and the trigger appears broken. In practice: a hover trigger that sets PART_Host's Opacity to 0.5 never shows, because whichever state is current holds PART_Host's Opacity with an animation (0 in Empty, 1 in HasCount). The trigger's value is there; it is simply outranked.

Deriving from an existing control

Often the cheapest option. Suppose you need a text box that accepts only numbers. Caret, selection, undo, clipboard and the default look should all stay exactly as they are. Deriving from TextBox keeps every bit of that behaviour, and the default template stays valid.

There is no static constructor. A subclass inherits TextBox's DefaultStyleKey, so it gets the TextBox default style and template without asking. The real work happens in OnPreviewTextInput. Preview events run before the control itself handles the input (see Part 23), so marking one handled stops the character from ever arriving. The override builds the text as it would be after the keystroke, replacing any selection, and rejects the character if the result would not parse as a number in the user's culture.

C# 
public class NumericTextBox : TextBox
{
    protected override void OnPreviewTextInput(TextCompositionEventArgs e)
    {
        // Text input, not key codes: works with any keyboard layout.
        string candidate = string.Concat(Text[..SelectionStart], e.Text,
                                          Text[(SelectionStart + SelectionLength)..]);
        e.Handled = !decimal.TryParse(candidate, NumberStyles.Number,
                                      CultureInfo.CurrentCulture, out _);
        base.OnPreviewTextInput(e);
    }

    protected override void OnPreviewKeyDown(KeyEventArgs e)
    {
        if (e.Key == Key.Space) e.Handled = true;   // space isn't a TextInput event
        base.OnPreviewKeyDown(e);
    }
}

One consequence to know: a character is accepted only if the whole text would then parse as a number, so typing - or the decimal separator into an empty box is rejected, because neither is a number on its own. The decimal separator follows the user's culture: in sv-SE the , key is accepted after digits, while . is always rejected.

One catch with styles: an unkeyed style matches its exact target type only. An app-wide implicit TextBox style, including the Fluent styles that .NET 9 and 10 add to app resources, does not apply to NumericTextBox. Opt in with Style="{StaticResource {x:Type TextBox}}" where you use it, or give NumericTextBox its own implicit style with BasedOn set to the TextBox one.

Paste still bypasses PreviewTextInput. Handle it with DataObject.AddPastingHandler(this, OnPaste) in the constructor.

RECAP
  • Start with a UserControl; build a lookless Control only when others must be able to restyle it.
  • Anything a host should bind must be a dependency property on your control.
  • Never set DataContext on the UserControl itself; bind to your own properties by element name instead.
  • A lookless control needs a class, a default style in Themes/Generic.xaml, and ThemeInfo pointing at it.
  • Deriving from an existing control keeps all its behaviour and is often the quickest route.
PART 28

Effects, shaders and 3D

Effects change how an element looks after it has been drawn: a shadow, a blur, a colour shift on every pixel. 3D goes the other way and builds a picture from geometry, lights and a camera. Both need no extra packages, and both are easy to make slow.

Builds on Part 18, graphics and animation and Part 5, dependency properties.

Built-in effects

A card that should float above the page needs a soft shadow; a photo behind a dialog often needs blurring so the dialog stands out. WPF ships two effects for exactly this, DropShadowEffect and BlurEffect, and you attach either to any element through its Effect property.

In the snippet, the shadow's BlurRadius sets how soft its edge is, ShadowDepth how far it sits from the element, and Direction the angle it falls in (270 is straight down). RenderingBias trades speed for quality. The blur's Radius sets its strength, and KernelType chooses a smooth Gaussian blur or a cheaper Box blur.

XAML 
<Border>
  <Border.Effect>
    <DropShadowEffect BlurRadius="18" ShadowDepth="4" Direction="270"
                      Opacity="0.22" Color="#101828" RenderingBias="Quality" />
  </Border.Effect>
</Border>

<Image>
  <Image.Effect><BlurEffect Radius="8" KernelType="Gaussian" /></Image.Effect>
</Image>
IN PLAIN TERMS

Normally WPF hands an element's shapes and text straight to the graphics card to draw. An effect needs the finished picture to work on, so WPF first paints the element and everything inside it onto an off-screen bitmap, like photographing a painting so you can put a filter over the photo, and then draws the filtered photo. That photo costs memory and time for every element that has an effect, and it is taken again whenever the content changes.

That off-screen bitmap is called a render target. Text painted into one cannot use WPF's normal hardware text rendering, so it can look softer than the text around it.

GOTCHA

An Effect forces the entire subtree into a render target and disables hardware text rendering inside it. A DropShadowEffect on every item in a 500-row list is one of the reliable ways to make WPF crawl. Use it on a handful of chrome elements only. For shadows on list items, draw a gradient Border instead, or use a nine-slice image (one whose corners stay fixed while its edges stretch).

Custom pixel shaders DEEPER

Two built-in effects do not go far. A greyed-out look for a disabled panel, a colour tint or a highlight that follows brightness all need your own effect.

A pixel shader is a small program that runs on the graphics card once for every pixel of the element's picture: it receives the pixel's position, reads its colour, and returns a new colour. You write it in HLSL, the C-like shading language from DirectX. WPF supports Shader Model 2.0/3.0 HLSL compiled to .ps bytecode. It's niche, but it's the only way to get per-pixel effects without a third-party renderer.

IN PLAIN TERMS

Think of a shader as one short instruction handed to thousands of workers at once. Each worker gets a single pixel, applies the instruction, and hands back a colour. No worker sees any other worker's result, which is why shaders run so fast in parallel, and why they suit per-pixel changes such as greyscale far better than anything that has to reason about the image as a whole.

The shader below turns the picture grey. register(s0) is input slot 0, where WPF supplies the element's picture; register(c0) is constant slot 0, which the C# class fills with Amount. The three numbers in dot weight red, green and blue by how bright each looks to the eye, and lerp blends between the original colour and the grey by Amount, so 0 leaves the picture untouched and 1 makes it fully grey.

HLSL: Greyscale.fx 
sampler2D Input : register(s0);
float Amount : register(c0);

float4 main(float2 uv : TEXCOORD) : COLOR
{
    float4 c = tex2D(Input, uv);
    float  g = dot(c.rgb, float3(0.2126, 0.7152, 0.0722));
